##### A3.C4.M1.L1 — Network Interfaces and the ip Command
- **Slug:** network-interfaces-ip-command | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** NET-02, NET-05 | **Prereqs:** A3.C3.M4.L4 (A3.C3 Capstone ≥80%)
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (ip addr show, ip addr show eth0, ip -br addr, ip link show, ip link set eth0 up, ip link set eth0 down, ip -s link, ip addr add 192.168.1.100/24 dev eth0, ip addr del 192.168.1.100/24 dev eth0, ip route show, ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0, ip route del default, ip route add 10.0.0.0/8 via 10.1.1.1, ip neigh show, nmcli device status, cat /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml, sudo netplan apply, sudo netplan try), ClickableDiagram (interface output anatomy: interface-name/flags/mtu/MAC-address/inet-address/prefix-length/brd/scope — click each field for definition and security relevance), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** ip-addr-show-field-by-field-decode, lower-up-flag-link-state-meaning, netplan-yaml-structure-static-vs-dhcp, ip-addr-add-arp-broadcast-effect
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 109, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, RHCSA EX200 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Network Security Engineer, DevOps Engineer, SOC Analyst | **Accessibility:** Interface output diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per field; flags list explained in a plain-language table; Netplan YAML structure annotated field by field with screen-reader descriptions; all command outputs in accessible code blocks; color-coded interface states also described textually

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Read and interpret `ip addr show` output completely: the first line shows interface index, name, flags (UP means administratively enabled, LOWER_UP means physical link is detected — an interface that is UP but not LOWER_UP has no cable or Wi-Fi association), and MTU (maximum transmission unit, default 1500 bytes on Ethernet); the `link/ether` line shows the MAC address and broadcast address; the `inet` line shows the IPv4 address in CIDR notation (192.168.1.10/24), the broadcast address (brd), the scope (global means routeable, link means link-local only, host means loopback), and the interface it is assigned to — and use `ip -br addr` for the concise one-line-per-interface summary format preferred when scanning many interfaces at once
2. Configure interface addressing and routing: assign a temporary (non-persistent) IP address with `ip addr add 192.168.1.100/24 dev eth0` and verify with `ip addr show eth0`; remove it with `ip addr del 192.168.1.100/24 dev eth0`; view the routing table with `ip route show` — each route entry shows the destination network, via (next-hop gateway), dev (outbound interface), and src (preferred source IP); add a default gateway with `ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0` (the default route is the next hop for all traffic not matched by a more specific route); understand that ip command changes are runtime-only and do not survive reboot — on Ubuntu 20.04+ persistence requires editing /etc/netplan/*.yaml and applying with `sudo netplan apply`
3. Manage persistent network configuration with Netplan: Netplan is Ubuntu's network configuration abstraction — YAML files in /etc/netplan/ are rendered to the underlying backend (NetworkManager or systemd-networkd) at boot; a minimal static configuration sets `dhcp4: false`, `addresses: [192.168.1.100/24]`, `routes: [{to: default, via: 192.168.1.1}]`, and `nameservers: {addresses: [1.1.1.1, 8.8.8.8]}` under the interface name; apply changes with `sudo netplan apply` (immediate, no reboot) or `sudo netplan try` (applies for 120 seconds then reverts if not confirmed — a safety net for remote administration where a wrong configuration would lock you out of SSH)

**Skills Gained:** ip addr show field decoding (flags/mtu/MAC/inet/scope), UP vs. LOWER_UP distinction, ip -br addr concise format, ip addr add/del (temporary assignment), ip route show (destination/via/dev/src), ip route add default, ip route del, ip neigh show (ARP table), ip -s link (interface statistics — RX/TX errors and drops), Netplan YAML structure (dhcp4/addresses/routes/nameservers), netplan apply vs. netplan try, nmcli device status as NetworkManager alternative
**AI Mentor:** The `netplan try` safety net is operationally critical: "If you apply a broken network configuration to a remote server with `netplan apply` and the configuration disables your network interface or removes your IP address, you lose SSH access permanently until someone physically accesses the console. `netplan try` applies the configuration immediately but reverts automatically in 120 seconds unless you type `yes` to confirm. If your configuration is correct, you confirm; if you lose connectivity, the old configuration restores itself when the timer expires. Always use `netplan try` for remote network configuration changes on production systems."

---

##### A3.C4.M1.L2 — DNS, Name Resolution, and hostnamectl
- **Slug:** dns-name-resolution-hostnamectl | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 40 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** NET-07 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M1.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (cat /etc/resolv.conf, cat /etc/nsswitch.conf, cat /etc/hosts, getent hosts, dig google.com, dig @1.1.1.1 google.com, dig +short google.com, dig google.com MX, dig google.com AAAA, dig -x 8.8.8.8, dig +trace google.com, nslookup google.com, host google.com, resolvectl status, resolvectl query google.com, hostnamectl, hostnamectl set-hostname sec-lab-01, hostname, hostname -f, sudo nano /etc/hosts), ClickableDiagram (name resolution priority: /etc/hosts first → DNS resolvers in /etc/resolv.conf → search domain expansion — each step labeled with the nsswitch.conf directive that controls it), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** resolv-conf-nameserver-and-search-field-roles, etc-hosts-exact-match-short-circuit, dig-output-question-answer-authority-additional-sections, dig-trace-delegation-chain, nslookup-vs-dig-output-difference
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 109, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder | **Accessibility:** Name resolution priority diagram text-labeled per step with aria descriptions; dig output sections (QUESTION/ANSWER/AUTHORITY/ADDITIONAL) each explained in text before the animation; /etc/nsswitch.conf hosts line decoded field by field; all DNS record types listed in a plain-language table; screen-reader output enabled for all TerminalSimulator commands

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Explain the Linux name resolution order and the three configuration files that control it: /etc/nsswitch.conf determines the order — the `hosts: files dns` line means try /etc/hosts first (files), then DNS (dns); /etc/hosts maps hostnames directly to IP addresses — any entry here overrides DNS entirely for that hostname, which is why attackers add entries to /etc/hosts as a persistence mechanism to redirect legitimate domain lookups to attacker-controlled IPs; /etc/resolv.conf specifies the DNS resolvers and search domains — `nameserver 192.168.1.1` sets the resolver, `search corp.example.com example.com` means unqualified hostnames (like webserver) are tried as webserver.corp.example.com then webserver.example.com before failing — and verify resolution with `getent hosts hostname` which uses the full nsswitch.conf order rather than querying DNS directly
2. Query DNS with dig: `dig hostname` sends a query through the configured resolver and shows the full response — the QUESTION section shows what was asked, the ANSWER section shows the resolved record (A record for IPv4, AAAA for IPv6, MX for mail, CNAME for alias), the AUTHORITY section shows the authoritative nameservers, and the ADDITIONAL section shows glue records; `dig @8.8.8.8 hostname` queries a specific resolver instead of the configured one (useful for bypass testing during DNS misconfiguration troubleshooting); `dig +short hostname` returns only the answer value — ideal for scripting; `dig -x IP` performs a reverse PTR lookup; `dig +trace hostname` traces the full delegation chain from root to authoritative nameserver — useful for diagnosing NXDOMAIN errors caused by misconfigured delegation
3. Manage the system hostname with hostnamectl: `hostnamectl` shows the static hostname (what you set), the transient hostname (set by DHCP or mDNS), and the pretty hostname (a free-text display name); `hostnamectl set-hostname sec-lab-01` sets the static hostname persistently (writes to /etc/hostname and notifies the system); the static hostname must resolve to the machine's own IP — either in /etc/hosts (127.0.1.1 sec-lab-01 is the convention on Ubuntu) or in DNS; a hostname that does not resolve to a local address causes sudo to warn "unable to resolve host" on every invocation, which looks alarming in logs and can indicate /etc/hostname was modified after provisioning — a persistence indicator during forensic investigation

**Skills Gained:** /etc/nsswitch.conf hosts line (files dns order), /etc/hosts exact-match short-circuit and security risk, /etc/resolv.conf nameserver and search directives, getent hosts for full-order resolution, dig (standard query), dig @resolver (specific resolver bypass), dig +short (scripting), dig record types (A/AAAA/MX/CNAME/PTR), dig -x reverse lookup, dig +trace delegation chain, nslookup (legacy — still common in Windows environments), host (quick lookup), resolvectl status (systemd-resolved configuration), hostnamectl (get/set static/transient/pretty hostname), /etc/hostname persistence, sudo hostname resolution warning as forensic indicator
**AI Mentor:** The /etc/hosts attack vector is frequently overlooked: "During any Linux incident, /etc/hosts is mandatory reading. Attackers modify it to redirect legitimate hostnames — update servers, authentication endpoints, logging collectors — to attacker-controlled IPs. An entry like `203.0.113.10 updates.ubuntu.com` silently intercepts update traffic. Compare /etc/hosts against a known-good baseline, or look at the file's mtime with `ls -la /etc/hosts` and cross-reference against expected configuration change timestamps in the journal. An /etc/hosts modification outside a configuration management run is a high-confidence indicator of unauthorized access."

---

##### A3.C4.M1.L3 — Network Troubleshooting: ping, traceroute, ss, and tcpdump
- **Slug:** network-troubleshooting-ping-traceroute-ss-tcpdump | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** NET-12, NET-09 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M1.L1, A3.C4.M1.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (ping -c 4 8.8.8.8, ping -c 4 -W 2 192.168.1.1, ping -c 4 -I eth0 8.8.8.8, traceroute -n 8.8.8.8, traceroute -n -T 8.8.8.8, mtr -n 8.8.8.8, ss -tlnp, ss -tnp, ss -s, ss -4s, ss -u, ss -tlnp state established, ss -tlnp state time-wait, netstat -tlnp, netstat -anp, netstat -s, tcpdump -i eth0 -c 20, tcpdump -i eth0 'port 22' -c 10, tcpdump -i eth0 'host 10.0.1.5' -c 10, tcpdump -i eth0 'icmp' -c 5, tcpdump -i eth0 -w /tmp/capture.pcap, tcpdump -r /tmp/capture.pcap, tcpdump -i eth0 -nn -v 'port 22' -c 5), ClickableDiagram (5-step connectivity diagnostic: (1) ip addr — interface/IP assigned? → (2) ip route — default route present? → (3) ping gateway — Layer 3 reachability? → (4) dig hostname — DNS resolving? → (5) ping/curl destination — end-to-end connectivity? — each step labeled with what a failure at that step indicates), ComparisonTable (ss vs. netstat — availability/speed/socket types/state display/process info/modern recommendation), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** ping-icmp-echo-reply-rtt-decode, traceroute-ttl-decrement-hop-sequence, ss-state-filter-established-vs-listening, tcpdump-packet-capture-output-anatomy, mtr-running-stats-update-per-hop
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 109, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Network Security Engineer | **Accessibility:** 5-step diagnostic diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per step and failure meaning; ping output fields labeled in an accessible table (icmp_seq/ttl/time); traceroute hop output decoded field by field; tcpdump packet line decoded field by field in plain text; ss state filters listed with plain-language state descriptions; no color-only differentiation in any diagram

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Diagnose connectivity layer by layer using a structured 5-step sequence: (1) `ip addr show` — confirm the interface has an IP address assigned and the LOWER_UP flag is set; (2) `ip route show` — confirm a default route exists; (3) `ping -c 4 <gateway-IP>` — test Layer 3 reachability to the default gateway; if this fails, the problem is local (interface, cable, or VLAN); (4) `dig <hostname> +short` — confirm DNS resolves; if ping passes but dig fails, the problem is DNS configuration; (5) `ping -c 4 <destination>` or `curl -I https://target` — test end-to-end reachability; if ping fails but DNS resolves, the path has a routing or firewall problem beyond the gateway — and use `traceroute -n <destination>` to map each hop, with `*` asterisks indicating a hop that drops ICMP or is behind a firewall, and `mtr -n <destination>` for real-time per-hop packet loss statistics
2. Inspect socket state with ss: `ss -tlnp` shows all TCP listening sockets (t=TCP, l=listening, n=numeric — do not resolve names, p=show process) with columns: State, Recv-Q, Send-Q, Local Address:Port, Peer Address:Port, and Process (PID and name) — this is the fastest way to confirm which process owns a given port; `ss -tnp` shows all established TCP connections with the same process information; `ss -s` shows a summary of all socket counts by type and state; `ss -4s` limits to IPv4; filter by state with `ss -tnp state established` or `ss -tnp state time-wait` — an unexpectedly large number of time-wait states or SYN-recv states is a behavioral indicator of a SYN flood; `netstat -tlnp` is the older equivalent, still encountered on legacy systems but deprecated in favour of ss because ss reads directly from kernel socket tables via /proc/net/, making it significantly faster on systems with thousands of connections
3. Capture and read network traffic with tcpdump: `tcpdump -i eth0` captures all traffic on the eth0 interface and prints one line per packet; `tcpdump -i eth0 -c 20` captures exactly 20 packets then exits; apply BPF filters to focus capture: `'port 22'` captures only SSH traffic, `'host 10.0.1.5'` captures traffic to or from a specific host, `'icmp'` captures only ping; `tcpdump -nn` disables name resolution for both hosts (first n) and port numbers (second n) — always use -nn in security contexts to prevent the capture itself from generating additional DNS traffic; save a capture for offline analysis with `tcpdump -i eth0 -w /tmp/capture.pcap` and replay it with `tcpdump -r /tmp/capture.pcap`; add `-v` or `-vv` for progressively more verbose packet detail including TTL, window size, and TCP flags — identify SYN packets (flag S only), SYN-ACK (flags S and A), RST (immediate connection rejection by the destination)

**Skills Gained:** 5-step connectivity diagnostic sequence (ip addr → ip route → ping gateway → dig → ping destination), ping -c (count) -W (timeout) -I (interface), traceroute -n (numeric), mtr -n (real-time per-hop stats), asterisk hops (ICMP-blocked vs. routing failure distinction), ss -tlnp (listening sockets with process), ss -tnp (established connections), ss -s (socket summary), ss state filters (established/time-wait/syn-recv), netstat -tlnp (legacy equivalent), tcpdump -i/-c/-nn/-w/-r, BPF filters (port/host/icmp), TCP flag identification (S=SYN S.=SYN-ACK R=RST F=FIN), -v/-vv verbosity levels, pcap file for offline Wireshark analysis
**AI Mentor:** Connect the security lens to every tool: "Every troubleshooting tool in this lesson is also a detection tool. `ss -tnp` run on a server that should have no outbound connections reveals a reverse shell. `tcpdump -i eth0 'port 443' -c 50 -nn` shows whether an unexpected process is making HTTPS connections. `ss -s` showing thousands of SYN-recv states during a traffic spike is a SYN flood in progress. Learn these tools for troubleshooting — but remember them for detection. The same commands you use to debug a broken network are the ones a responder uses to catch an attacker in the act."

---

##### A3.C4.M1.L4 — Remote File Transfer: scp, rsync, wget, and curl
- **Slug:** remote-file-transfer-scp-rsync-wget-curl | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L05, NET-11 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M1.L1, A3.C4.M1.L2, A3.C4.M1.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (scp file.txt user@10.0.1.5:/tmp/, scp -r ./configs/ user@10.0.1.5:/etc/app/, scp -P 2222 user@10.0.1.5:/var/log/app.log ., rsync -avz user@10.0.1.5:/var/www/html/ /backup/html/, rsync -avz --delete src/ dest/, rsync -avz --dry-run --delete src/ dest/, rsync --progress -avz src/ dest/, rsync -avz --bwlimit=1000 src/ dest/, rsync -e "ssh -p 2222" src/ user@host:dest/, wget https://example.com/file.tar.gz, wget -c https://example.com/file.tar.gz, wget -q -O - https://example.com/file.txt, wget --spider https://example.com/, curl -s https://api.example.com/status, curl -I https://example.com, curl -v https://example.com 2>&1 | head -30, curl -o file.tar.gz https://example.com/file.tar.gz, curl -L https://example.com, curl -H "Authorization: Bearer token" https://api.example.com/data, curl -X POST -d '{"key":"value"}' -H "Content-Type: application/json" https://api.example.com/), ComparisonTable (scp vs. rsync vs. wget vs. curl: transport protocol, encryption, resumes interrupted transfers, bandwidth limiting, primary use case, typical scenario), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** scp-ssh-channel-wrapping, rsync-delta-transfer-only-changed-blocks, rsync-delete-sync-before-after, wget-resume-with-c-flag, curl-verbose-handshake-output-anatomy
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 2, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, DevOps Engineer, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder | **Accessibility:** Comparison table has proper row and column headers; rsync --dry-run output explained in text before live demo; curl -v output decoded field by field in accessible code block; all flag combinations documented in a plain-language reference table; TerminalSimulator screen-reader accessible throughout

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Transfer files securely with scp and rsync: scp (secure copy) uses the SSH protocol to encrypt files in transit — `scp source user@host:destination` copies a local file to a remote host and `scp user@host:source destination` retrieves a remote file; use `-r` for recursive directory copies and `-P` (capital P, unlike ssh -p) to specify a non-default SSH port; scp is appropriate for one-off transfers but is inefficient for large or frequently updated directories because it always transfers the entire file even if only a few bytes changed; rsync solves this with delta transfer: `rsync -avz src/ dest/` transfers only the blocks that differ between source and destination (a=archive — preserves permissions/timestamps/symlinks/owner, v=verbose progress, z=compress in transit — useful on slow links, omit on LAN transfers); `--delete` removes files from the destination that no longer exist in the source (use `--dry-run` first to preview changes without executing them); rsync also works over SSH: `rsync -avz -e "ssh -p 2222" user@host:/var/data/ /backup/`
2. Download content over HTTP/HTTPS with wget: `wget https://url/file` downloads a file and saves it under the original filename in the current directory; `wget -c url` resumes an interrupted download — critical for large files on unreliable connections; `wget -q -O - url` runs silently (-q) and writes output to stdout (-O -) instead of a file — the standard pattern for piping downloaded content (use with extreme caution: `wget -O - url | bash` is the canonical insecure pattern used by attackers and by lazy install scripts — never pipe untrusted content to bash); `wget --spider url` checks whether a URL exists without downloading it, returning only the HTTP status code; wget is session-less and cannot send request headers or POST data, which is why curl is preferred for any interaction beyond simple downloads
3. Interact with HTTP APIs and perform diagnostic HTTP requests with curl: `curl -I url` sends a HEAD request and returns only response headers (status code, Content-Type, Server, security headers like Strict-Transport-Security) — the fastest way to check whether a web server is responding and what headers it sends; `curl -v url` shows the full TLS handshake, request headers, and response headers (verbose output goes to stderr — append `2>&1` to pipe it); `curl -L url` follows HTTP redirects automatically; `curl -o filename url` saves output to a named file; `curl -H "Header: value"` adds a request header; `curl -X POST -d '{"key":"val"}' -H "Content-Type: application/json" url` sends a JSON POST body — making curl the universal HTTP debugging tool that replaces dedicated API clients in a terminal session

**Skills Gained:** scp syntax (local-to-remote/remote-to-local), scp -r recursive, scp -P port, rsync -avz (archive/verbose/compress), rsync --delete (sync remove), rsync --dry-run (preview), rsync --progress, rsync --bwlimit (bandwidth cap), rsync -e ssh -p port, wget basic download, wget -c resume, wget -q -O - (stdout pipe), wget --spider (URL existence check), wget-to-bash security risk, curl -I (headers only), curl -v (verbose TLS + headers), curl -L (follow redirects), curl -o (named output), curl -H (custom headers), curl -X POST -d body (API POST), curl vs. wget decision matrix
**AI Mentor:** The wget-pipe-to-bash pattern is a security critical teaching moment: "You will encounter installation instructions that say `curl -s https://example.com/install.sh | bash` or `wget -O - url | bash`. This pattern downloads and executes code from the internet in a single command with no opportunity to review what runs. If the URL is compromised or the TLS certificate is forged by a MITM attacker, arbitrary code executes with your current privileges — often root. The secure alternative: download first, review the script, then execute. `wget -O install.sh url && less install.sh && bash install.sh`. This is not paranoia — it is the same supply-chain hygiene principle you would apply to any untrusted executable. A legitimate software project will not penalise you for reviewing before executing."

### Module A3.C4.M1 — Practical

**Title:** Diagnose and Repair a Broken Network Configuration
**Type:** guided-lab | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** A newly provisioned Ubuntu 22.04 application server at Helix Corp has been reported as unable to reach the internet. A junior administrator made several configuration changes before giving up. The learner must diagnose the exact failure layer and restore full network connectivity without rebooting the system.

**Tasks:**
1. Run `ip addr show` — observe that eth0 has no IP address assigned (only the link-local fe80:: IPv6 address is present). Add a temporary address with `ip addr add 192.168.50.100/24 dev eth0`; verify with `ip -br addr`.
2. Run `ip route show` — no default route exists. Add one with `ip route add default via 192.168.50.1 dev eth0`; verify with `ping -c 2 192.168.50.1`.
3. Run `cat /etc/resolv.conf` — nameserver is set to 192.168.50.200 which does not exist in this lab. Run `dig google.com` and confirm SERVFAIL. Edit /etc/resolv.conf to replace the nameserver with 1.1.1.1; run `dig +short google.com` to confirm resolution returns an IP.
4. Run `ping -c 4 google.com` to confirm end-to-end connectivity. Then identify why the issue will reappear on next boot: run `cat /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml` and observe dhcp4 is set to false with no static address configured. Edit the file to add the correct static IP, gateway, and nameserver; run `sudo netplan try` and confirm the configuration applies correctly.
5. Run `ss -tlnp` and document all listening services and their PIDs. Identify any listening service not in the expected baseline (provided in /opt/baseline/listening-services.txt) and note it for the security review.

**Assessment Criteria:** Correct identification of each failure layer in sequence (40%), ip and netplan commands executed correctly (30%), Netplan YAML valid and persistent configuration confirmed (20%), unexpected listening service identified (10%)

### Module A3.C4.M2 — SSH and Secure Remote Administration

**Module Objectives:**
1. Explain the OpenSSH architecture, authentication flow, and the cryptographic key exchange that protects each session
2. Generate SSH key pairs, deploy public keys to remote hosts, and manage keys with ssh-agent and ssh-add
3. Harden an OpenSSH server by disabling root login, disabling password authentication, restricting allowed users, and deploying Fail2Ban
4. Transfer files and create encrypted tunnels with scp, SFTP, and SSH local and remote port forwarding

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L05 (SSH — primary node for this entire module — key-based vs. password authentication, architecture, hardening, tunneling)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner generates an Ed25519 key pair, deploys the public key to a remote host with ssh-copy-id, adds the key to ssh-agent, edits /etc/ssh/sshd_config to disable root login and password authentication, verifies the hardened configuration with sshd -t, installs and configures Fail2Ban for SSH, uses sftp to transfer a file interactively, and creates a local port forward to tunnel a remote web service through SSH
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (ssh user@host, ssh -p 2222 user@host, ssh -v user@host, ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 user@host, ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "label", ssh-keygen -t rsa -b 4096, ssh-copy-id user@host, ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub user@host, cat ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, chmod 700 ~/.ssh && chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, eval $(ssh-agent -s), ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519, ssh-add -l, ssh -A user@host, cat /etc/ssh/sshd_config, sudo grep -v "^#" /etc/ssh/sshd_config | grep -v "^$", sudo nano /etc/ssh/sshd_config, sudo sshd -t, sudo systemctl reload ssh, sudo cat /var/log/auth.log | grep "Failed password", sudo fail2ban-client status sshd, sudo fail2ban-client set sshd unbanip IP, sftp user@host, sftp -P 2222 user@host, get remotefile, put localfile, ls, lcd, lls, ssh -L 8080:localhost:80 user@host, ssh -R 9090:localhost:3000 user@host, ssh -N -f -L 8080:localhost:80 user@host), ClickableDiagram (SSH authentication flow: TCP connect → protocol version exchange → key exchange (ECDH) → server host key verification → authentication (pubkey or password) → channel open → session — each step labeled with what is encrypted and when), ComparisonTable (RSA 4096 vs. Ed25519 — key size/security level/performance/support/recommendation), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** ssh-key-exchange-ecdh-simplified, server-host-key-verification-known-hosts, pubkey-auth-challenge-response-flow, sshd-config-hardening-before-after, fail2ban-log-analysis-ban-trigger, ssh-local-port-forward-tunnel-diagram
**Simulations:** SSH authentication flow (step-through the full handshake: learner selects what happens at each phase); sshd_config security scorer (learner edits config options → security score updates in real time showing impact of each setting)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L3; quiz-and-challenge for L2 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Generate an Ed25519 key pair with `ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "lab-key"`; identify the two files created, explain the difference between the public and private key, and state the permissions that must be set on each
- **Intermediate:** Edit /etc/ssh/sshd_config to set PermitRootLogin no and PasswordAuthentication no; run `sudo sshd -t` to validate; explain what would happen if you applied this config before deploying a public key
- **Advanced:** Create a local port forward `ssh -L 8080:localhost:80 admin@10.0.1.5 -N -f`; open http://localhost:8080 in the browser and explain why traffic is encrypted even though HTTP is used
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level

##### A3.C4.M2.L1 — OpenSSH Architecture and Authentication Flow
- **Slug:** openssh-architecture-authentication-flow | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** understand | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L05 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M1.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (ssh user@10.0.1.5, ssh -v user@10.0.1.5 2>&1 | head -40, ssh -p 2222 user@10.0.1.5, ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 user@10.0.1.5, cat ~/.ssh/known_hosts, ssh-keyscan 10.0.1.5, cat /etc/ssh/sshd_config | grep -v "^#" | grep -v "^$", systemctl status ssh, sudo ss -tlnp | grep sshd, who, w, last | head -10), ClickableDiagram (SSH connection phases: TCP handshake → SSH protocol version string exchange → algorithm negotiation → key exchange (ECDH — server proves identity with host key) → user authentication (pubkey challenge-response or password) → session channel opened — click each phase for detail on what is negotiated and what is protected), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** tcp-to-ssh-layering, ecdh-key-exchange-shared-secret-without-transmitting, server-host-key-fingerprint-verification, known-hosts-trust-on-first-use-tofu
- **Practical:** simulation | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 2, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Security Engineer, Incident Responder | **Accessibility:** SSH phase diagram fully text-labeled with aria descriptions per phase and what is protected at each step; ECDH concept described in plain language without requiring cryptography prerequisites; known_hosts file format decoded field by field in text; -v verbose output explained line by line in an accessible reference; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Explain the OpenSSH architecture: OpenSSH consists of two components — the sshd daemon (SSH server, started by systemd, listens on TCP port 22 by default, reads configuration from /etc/ssh/sshd_config) and the ssh client (reads per-user configuration from ~/.ssh/config and system defaults from /etc/ssh/ssh_config); the connection proceeds through four phases: (1) TCP connection to port 22; (2) protocol version string exchange — both sides announce "SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_X.X"; (3) algorithm negotiation and key exchange using Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDH) — both sides independently compute a shared session key without ever transmitting it, meaning even a complete recording of the TCP stream cannot decrypt the session; (4) user authentication — the server challenges the client to prove identity using either public key authentication (most secure) or password authentication (acceptable but weaker)
2. Understand host key verification and the known_hosts file: on first connection to a host, SSH presents the server's host key fingerprint and asks "Are you sure you want to continue connecting?" — answering yes adds the host's public key to ~/.ssh/known_hosts; on every subsequent connection, SSH verifies that the server presents the same host key — if the key changes, SSH refuses to connect with a WARNING: REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED message; this protects against man-in-the-middle attacks where an attacker intercepts the connection with a different server; this is the Trust On First Use (TOFU) model — if the first connection was to a legitimate server, all future connections are verified; to retrieve a server's fingerprint out-of-band (before first connection) use `ssh-keyscan hostname 2>/dev/null | ssh-keygen -lf -` and compare against the fingerprint provided by the server's administrator
3. Identify the security advantage of public key authentication over password authentication: in password authentication, the client sends the password to the server (encrypted inside the SSH session) and the server verifies it — if the server is compromised, passwords can be intercepted at verification time; in public key authentication, the server challenges the client to sign a random value with its private key — the server verifies the signature against the stored public key without ever seeing the private key; the private key never leaves the client machine, making it immune to server-side credential theft, replay attacks, and brute force attempts from the network — and explain why disabling password authentication is the single highest-impact SSH hardening step

**Skills Gained:** OpenSSH client/server architecture (sshd/ssh), /etc/ssh/sshd_config vs. ~/.ssh/config, default port 22, ECDH key exchange (shared secret without transmission), algorithm negotiation, host key fingerprint verification, known_hosts file (format: hostname/algorithm/base64-pubkey), TOFU trust model, WARNING: REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED meaning, ssh-keyscan for out-of-band fingerprint retrieval, password vs. pubkey auth security comparison, -v verbose flag for connection debugging, -p for non-default port, -i for explicit identity file
**AI Mentor:** Make the MITM protection concrete: "The known_hosts warning that scares beginners is actually one of the most valuable security signals in Linux. If you connect to a server you connect to every day and SSH suddenly shows 'REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED,' do not bypass it. Possible causes: the server was rebuilt (check with the sysadmin), the server's IP was reassigned to a different host, or someone is performing a man-in-the-middle attack. Investigate before proceeding. The two seconds it takes to verify with the sysadmin are worth it."

---

##### A3.C4.M2.L2 — SSH Key Authentication
- **Slug:** ssh-key-authentication | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L05 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M2.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "prosper@helix-lab-2026", ssh-keygen -t rsa -b 4096 -C "backup-key", ls -la ~/.ssh/, cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub, ssh-copy-id user@10.0.1.5, ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub -p 2222 user@10.0.1.5, cat ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, chmod 700 ~/.ssh, chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, chmod 644 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub, chmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519, ssh user@10.0.1.5 "cat ~/.ssh/authorized_keys", eval $(ssh-agent -s), ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519, ssh-add -l, ssh-add -d ~/.ssh/id_ed25519, ssh -A user@10.0.1.5, cat ~/.ssh/config), ComparisonTable (RSA 4096 vs. Ed25519 vs. ECDSA-256 — key size, security bits, key generation speed, sign speed, support age, recommendation), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** ssh-keygen-public-private-pair-creation, ssh-copy-id-authorized-keys-append, ssh-agent-key-cache-unlock-once, pubkey-challenge-response-sequence, agent-forwarding-hop-through-bastion
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 2, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, DevOps Engineer, SOC Analyst | **Accessibility:** Key pair creation steps documented as a numbered sequence in text before the animation; authorized_keys file format decoded field by field; chmod permission requirements listed in a plain-language table with explanations; ssh-agent workflow described as numbered steps; agent forwarding security risk explained in text; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Generate and deploy SSH key pairs: `ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "descriptive-label"` generates a modern Ed25519 key pair — ed25519 is preferred over RSA because it is shorter, faster, and provides equivalent or greater security; the -C flag adds a comment to the public key (conventionally username@hostname or a service identifier) that appears in authorized_keys files to identify which key belongs to whom; the command creates two files: ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 (the private key — never share, never copy to a server, must be readable only by the owner — `chmod 600`) and ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub (the public key — safe to share, placed on every server the user needs access to — `chmod 644`); add a passphrase to the private key during generation to encrypt it at rest — if the private key file is stolen, the passphrase prevents immediate use
2. Deploy a public key to a remote host: `ssh-copy-id user@hostname` reads ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub (or the first available public key) and appends it to ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the remote host, creating the file and setting correct permissions if necessary; `ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub user@hostname` explicitly specifies which public key to deploy; manually appending requires: creating ~/.ssh/ on the remote host with `mkdir -p ~/.ssh && chmod 700 ~/.ssh`, appending the public key content to ~/.ssh/authorized_keys with `cat id_ed25519.pub >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys`, and setting `chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys` — SSH will refuse to use authorized_keys if the file or its parent directory is group- or world-writable, because a world-writable authorized_keys allows any user to add their own public key and gain unauthorised access
3. Manage keys with ssh-agent: `eval $(ssh-agent -s)` starts the agent and sets the SSH_AUTH_SOCK environment variable; `ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519` adds the private key to the agent — the agent prompts for the passphrase once and holds the decrypted key in memory for the session; `ssh-add -l` lists all keys currently held by the agent; `ssh-add -d ~/.ssh/id_ed25519` removes a key from the agent; enable agent forwarding with `ssh -A user@bastion` to allow the bastion host to use the agent for onward connections — the private key still never leaves the local machine; agent forwarding must be used cautiously: if the bastion host is compromised, root on the bastion can use the agent socket to authenticate as the user to any server — only forward the agent to trusted hosts you fully control

**Skills Gained:** ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C label, ed25519 vs. RSA 4096 recommendation, private/public key file names and required permissions (600/644), passphrase encryption of private key, ssh-copy-id (automatic deploy), ssh-copy-id -i (explicit key), manual authorized_keys append sequence, ~/.ssh/ directory (700) and authorized_keys (600) permissions enforcement, why world-writable authorized_keys fails, eval $(ssh-agent -s), ssh-add / -l / -d, single passphrase unlock for session, ssh -A agent forwarding, agent forwarding security risk (bastion root can use socket)
**AI Mentor:** The permission requirement for authorized_keys is a common misconfiguration: "If you add a public key to authorized_keys and SSH still asks for a password, 90% of the time the problem is permissions. SSH refuses to read authorized_keys if the file is writable by anyone other than the owner, or if ~/.ssh/ is group- or world-writable. Run `ls -la ~/.ssh/` on the server and verify the directory is 700 and authorized_keys is 600. If it is 664 or 755, fix it with `chmod 700 ~/.ssh && chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys` and try again. Also check `ssh -v user@host` — the verbose output tells you exactly which step failed and why."

---

##### A3.C4.M2.L3 — SSH Server Hardening
- **Slug:** ssh-server-hardening | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L05 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M2.L1, A3.C4.M2.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (sudo cat /etc/ssh/sshd_config, sudo cp /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/ssh/sshd_config.bak, sudo nano /etc/ssh/sshd_config, sudo sshd -t, sudo sshd -T | grep -iE "permitroot|passwordauth|port|allowusers|maxauthtries|logingraceime|x11|printmotd|banner", sudo systemctl reload ssh, sudo systemctl status ssh, sudo grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | tail -20, sudo grep "Accepted publickey" /var/log/auth.log | tail -10, sudo grep "Invalid user" /var/log/auth.log | tail -20, sudo apt install fail2ban, sudo systemctl enable --now fail2ban, sudo cat /etc/fail2ban/jail.conf, sudo nano /etc/fail2ban/jail.local, sudo systemctl restart fail2ban, sudo fail2ban-client status, sudo fail2ban-client status sshd, sudo fail2ban-client set sshd unbanip 203.0.113.45, sudo iptables -L -n | grep DROP | head -10), ClickableDiagram (sshd_config security option map: PermitRootLogin/PasswordAuthentication/MaxAuthTries/LoginGraceTime/AllowUsers/Port/X11Forwarding/PrintLastLog — each option with secure vs. default value and risk description), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** sshd-config-hardening-before-after-comparison, fail2ban-log-watch-ban-action-sequence, iptables-fail2ban-drop-rule-insertion, sshd-t-validate-before-reload-safety
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 2, CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domain 2, CIS Benchmark Ubuntu 22.04 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, DevOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** sshd_config option table has proper column and row headers with secure vs. default value columns; Fail2Ban configuration file structure decoded field by field in text; fail2ban-client status output explained in plain language; iptables DROP rule insertion described as numbered steps; all configuration file examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Harden /etc/ssh/sshd_config with the eight most impactful security settings: `PermitRootLogin no` — prevents direct root SSH logins (administrators must connect as a regular user and use sudo, creating an audit trail of who elevated privileges and when); `PasswordAuthentication no` — disables all password-based SSH logins after confirming key-based authentication works, eliminating the entire brute-force attack surface; `MaxAuthTries 3` — disconnects after 3 failed authentication attempts per connection, slowing automated attacks; `LoginGraceTime 30` — reduces the window during which an unauthenticated connection holds a session slot from the default 120 seconds; `AllowUsers prosper alice` — restricts SSH access to a whitelist of named accounts, preventing any account not explicitly listed from authenticating even with a valid password or key; `X11Forwarding no` — disables X11 graphical forwarding, which opens a backward channel that has been exploited historically; `Banner /etc/ssh/banner.txt` — displays a legal warning before authentication, required by many compliance frameworks and establishes a documented notice that unauthorized access is prohibited; always validate changes with `sudo sshd -t` before reloading — a syntax error in sshd_config can lock all SSH sessions out of the server
2. Change the SSH listening port and explain the trade-off: setting `Port 2222` (or any non-default port) in sshd_config moves the SSH listener away from port 22; this reduces noise from automated scanners that only probe standard ports, making auth.log less cluttered and reducing Fail2Ban false-positive banning risk — but it is not a security control (security through obscurity); changing the port does not stop a targeted attacker who runs `nmap -p-` to scan all ports; the operational trade-off is that every SSH client, script, bastion configuration, and monitoring tool must be updated to specify the new port — document the change in the server's infrastructure record before applying; use `Port 22` and `Port 2222` simultaneously (two Port directives) during migration to allow a transition period without locking anyone out
3. Deploy Fail2Ban to automatically block brute-force SSH attempts: Fail2Ban monitors /var/log/auth.log for repeated authentication failures matching configurable regex patterns; when the `maxretry` threshold is crossed within the `findtime` window, it inserts an iptables or nftables DROP rule for the offending IP for the duration of `bantime`; configure a local override by creating /etc/fail2ban/jail.local (never edit jail.conf directly — it is overwritten on package upgrade) with a `[sshd]` section specifying `enabled = true`, `maxretry = 5`, `findtime = 5m`, and `bantime = 1h`; restart Fail2Ban with `systemctl restart fail2ban`; check status with `fail2ban-client status sshd` which shows the number of currently banned IPs and the total ban count since startup; unban a legitimate IP with `fail2ban-client set sshd unbanip IP` — an essential administrative operation for when a team member's home IP is banned after a typo

**Skills Gained:** /etc/ssh/sshd_config backup before editing, eight hardening settings (PermitRootLogin no/PasswordAuthentication no/MaxAuthTries 3/LoginGraceTime 30/AllowUsers whitelist/X11Forwarding no/Banner path/Port change), sudo sshd -t syntax validation before reload, sudo systemctl reload ssh (graceful — no session disruption), sshd -T (dump effective running config), /var/log/auth.log patterns (Failed password/Accepted publickey/Invalid user), Fail2Ban install and enable, /etc/fail2ban/jail.local override pattern, jail.local [sshd] section (enabled/maxretry/findtime/bantime), fail2ban-client status sshd, fail2ban-client set sshd unbanip, port change trade-off (noise reduction vs. not a true security control)
**AI Mentor:** The validation step is non-negotiable: "The most dangerous moment in SSH hardening is reloading sshd with a configuration file that has a syntax error. A reloaded but broken sshd may keep existing sessions alive but refuse all new connections — including any new SSH session you try to open to fix the mistake. Always run `sudo sshd -t` before `sudo systemctl reload ssh`. If sshd -t reports an error, fix it before touching the live daemon. And keep at least one existing SSH session open while testing the reload — if the new session works, close the old one. If the new session fails, fall back from the open session. This protocol has prevented more accidental lockouts than any other single practice."

---

##### A3.C4.M2.L4 — Secure File Transfer and SSH Tunneling
- **Slug:** secure-file-transfer-ssh-tunneling | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L05 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M2.L1, A3.C4.M2.L2, A3.C4.M2.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (sftp user@10.0.1.5, sftp -P 2222 user@10.0.1.5, sftp> ls, sftp> lls, sftp> cd /var/www, sftp> lcd /tmp, sftp> get index.html, sftp> put config.yaml /etc/app/config.yaml, sftp> mkdir backups, sftp> chmod 640 uploaded-file.conf, sftp> bye, ssh -L 8080:internal-web:80 user@bastion -N, ssh -L 5432:db-server:5432 user@bastion -N -f, ssh -R 9090:localhost:3000 user@remote-server -N, ssh -D 1080 user@proxy-host -N -f, ssh -L 8080:localhost:80 user@host -o ServerAliveInterval=60 -o ExitOnForwardFailure=yes, curl http://localhost:8080/), ClickableDiagram (SSH tunnel types: local forward — client binds local port → SSH encrypts → server connects to destination; remote forward — server binds remote port → SSH encrypts → client connects to local destination; dynamic SOCKS5 — client binds SOCKS proxy port → SSH encrypts → server proxies to any destination), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** sftp-interactive-session-flow, ssh-local-forward-data-path, ssh-remote-forward-use-case, socks5-dynamic-proxy-arbitrary-destination
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 2, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, DevOps Engineer, Penetration Tester | **Accessibility:** SSH tunnel type diagram text-labeled per tunnel type with data flow described as numbered steps; sftp interactive commands listed in an accessible reference table; local vs. remote vs. dynamic forward comparison in plain-language table; -N and -f flags explained in text before use; all connection examples decoded step by step in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Transfer files interactively with SFTP: SFTP (SSH File Transfer Protocol) runs over the SSH connection and provides an interactive FTP-like session; connect with `sftp user@hostname` (or `sftp -P 2222 user@hostname` for a non-default port); inside the SFTP prompt: `ls` lists the remote directory, `lls` lists the local directory (l prefix = local), `cd remotepath` changes the remote directory, `lcd localpath` changes the local directory, `get remotefile` downloads a file to the local current directory, `put localfile remotepath` uploads a file, `mkdir dirname` creates a remote directory, `chmod 640 filename` sets remote file permissions, `bye` or `exit` closes the session; SFTP is preferred over plain FTP for compliance environments because all data and commands are encrypted inside the SSH session — the same audit trail in /var/log/auth.log records the authentication event
2. Create a local port forward to access an isolated service securely: `ssh -L localport:destinationhost:destinationport user@sshserver -N` binds `localport` on the local machine — any connection to localhost:localport is encrypted through the SSH tunnel to `sshserver`, which then forwards the traffic to `destinationhost:destinationport` (which may be `sshserver` itself, or a host reachable from it); `-N` tells SSH not to execute a remote command (tunnel-only mode); `-f` sends the process to the background; example: `ssh -L 5432:10.0.2.5:5432 admin@bastion -N -f` allows a local PostgreSQL client to connect to `localhost:5432` while the actual database at 10.0.2.5 is behind the bastion — enabling encrypted database access without exposing the database port publicly; add `-o ExitOnForwardFailure=yes` so the tunnel exits cleanly if the destination is unreachable rather than silently forwarding to nothing
3. Understand remote and dynamic port forwarding: remote forwarding (`ssh -R remoteport:localhost:localport user@remoteserver -N`) binds a port on the remote SSH server — connections to that port on the remote server are tunneled back to `localport` on the local machine; this is how reverse SSH tunnels work — a server behind NAT connects outbound to an accessible host, and the tunnel allows the accessible host to reach the NAT-protected server; dynamic SOCKS forwarding (`ssh -D 1080 user@host -N -f`) binds a local SOCKS5 proxy port — applications configured to use localhost:1080 as a SOCKS5 proxy will have their traffic encrypted through SSH and proxied from the remote server, effectively routing traffic through the SSH server's network; all three tunnel types are legitimate administrative tools, and all three are techniques commonly used in post-exploitation lateral movement — recognizing their network signatures (long-lived SSH sessions with symmetric traffic patterns) is a detection skill for SOC analysts

**Skills Gained:** sftp interactive session (ls/lls/cd/lcd/get/put/mkdir/chmod/bye), sftp -P port, sftp vs. SFTP vs. SCP distinction, ssh -L local port forward (localport:desthost:destport), -N (no command/tunnel only), -f (background), -o ExitOnForwardFailure=yes, ssh -L use case (isolated service access through bastion), ssh -R remote port forward (reverse tunnel through NAT), ssh -D SOCKS5 dynamic proxy, all three tunnel types as dual-use (admin tool and post-exploitation technique), network signature of active SSH tunnels for detection
**AI Mentor:** The dual-use nature of SSH tunneling is a recurring theme in security: "Every SSH tunnel feature in this lesson is a legitimate administrative tool and a known attacker technique. A DevOps engineer uses `-L` to securely access a database behind a bastion — an attacker who compromised an internal server uses `-R` to punch a tunnel out through the firewall and give themselves remote access. Both produce long-lived SSH sessions with sustained traffic. Detection relies on anomaly: is this SSH session from a known account, at a normal time, to a known server? If a database server is initiating an outbound SSH connection to an external IP, that is a reverse tunnel for command-and-control. Context is everything."

---

### Module A3.C4.M2 — Practical

**Title:** Harden a Linux Server for Secure Remote Administration
**Type:** guided-lab | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** A fresh Ubuntu 22.04 server at Helix Corp has been provisioned with default SSH configuration. The security team requires it to meet the SSH hardening baseline before any production workloads are deployed. Key-based authentication is mandatory; all administrative accounts must be on an approved user list; root login must be disabled; and Fail2Ban must be active and configured.

**Tasks:**
1. Generate an Ed25519 key pair locally: `ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "admin@helix-lab"`. Deploy the public key to the server: `ssh-copy-id admin@10.0.1.10`. Test key-based login with `ssh admin@10.0.1.10` and confirm the connection succeeds without a password prompt.
2. Back up the configuration: `sudo cp /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/ssh/sshd_config.bak`. Apply the six required hardening settings in /etc/ssh/sshd_config: `PermitRootLogin no`, `PasswordAuthentication no`, `MaxAuthTries 3`, `LoginGraceTime 30`, `AllowUsers admin`, `X11Forwarding no`. Validate: `sudo sshd -t`. Reload: `sudo systemctl reload ssh`.
3. Open a new terminal and test that key-based login still works. Attempt to connect as root and confirm it is rejected. Attempt to connect as an unlisted user and confirm it is rejected.
4. Install and configure Fail2Ban: `sudo apt install fail2ban -y`. Create /etc/fail2ban/jail.local with a [sshd] section setting `enabled = true`, `maxretry = 3`, `findtime = 2m`, `bantime = 30m`. Restart Fail2Ban. Check status: `sudo fail2ban-client status sshd`.
5. Add the `Banner /etc/ssh/banner.txt` directive to sshd_config. Create /etc/ssh/banner.txt with an authorized-use-only notice. Reload sshd and test that the banner appears on the next login.

**Assessment Criteria:** Key pair generated and deployed correctly (20%), all six sshd_config settings applied and validated with sshd -t (30%), Fail2Ban active with correct jail.local settings confirmed by fail2ban-client status (30%), banner displayed on login (10%), new terminal test confirming root and unlisted user rejection (10%)

### Module A3.C4.M3 — Host Security and Access Control

**Module Objectives:**
1. Configure UFW to implement a default-deny host firewall policy and understand nftables as the underlying kernel technology
2. Configure SELinux and AppArmor to enforce mandatory access control and interpret and resolve policy violations
3. Implement least privilege using correct sudoers configuration, locked service accounts, and SUID binary auditing
4. Configure auditd to monitor privileged commands, sensitive file access, and user account changes

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L11 (SELinux/AppArmor — primary node for this module), OS-L15 (Auditd — introduced in L4), OS-L03 (users — service accounts and sudo), OS-L02 (permissions — SUID auditing)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner configures UFW to allow only SSH and HTTPS traffic from a specific subnet while blocking all other inbound connections; puts AppArmor in enforce mode for a web server profile and interprets a denial in /var/log/audit/audit.log or /var/log/syslog; creates a sudoers file granting a service account access to exactly one command; and writes three auditd rules to monitor /etc/passwd modifications, sudo command execution, and SSH logins
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (ufw status verbose, ufw default deny incoming, ufw default allow outgoing, ufw allow 22/tcp, ufw allow from 10.0.0.0/8 to any port 443, ufw deny 23/tcp, ufw enable, ufw logging on, ufw show added, ufw reset, nft list ruleset, nft list table inet filter, getenforce, setenforce 0, setenforce 1, sestatus, cat /etc/selinux/config, ls -laZ /var/www/html/, chcon -t httpd_sys_content_t /var/www/html/index.html, restorecon -Rv /var/www/html/, semanage port -a -t http_port_t -p tcp 8080, sealert -a /var/log/audit/audit.log, aa-status, aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, aa-complain /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, cat /var/log/syslog | grep "apparmor", auditctl -l, auditctl -w /etc/passwd -p wa -k identity, auditctl -a always,exit -F arch=b64 -S execve -F euid=0 -k privileged, ausearch -k identity, ausearch -k privileged -ts recent, aureport --summary, aureport -l, aureport -au), ClickableDiagram (MAC vs. DAC layers: hardware → kernel → DAC (rwx permissions + ACLs) → MAC (SELinux/AppArmor) → process — click each layer for description and how bypass is blocked), ComparisonTable (SELinux vs. AppArmor — model/distribution/label style/policy management/violation log location/tool for audit), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** ufw-rule-processing-order-default-policy, nftables-ufw-backend-relationship, selinux-context-label-anatomy, selinux-type-enforcement-allow-deny-decision, apparmor-profile-enforce-vs-complain-mode, auditd-rule-syscall-filter-log-path
**Simulations:** UFW rule builder (add/remove rules → preview effective policy → test connection result); SELinux context mismatch fixer (read AVC denial → generate chcon command → verify resolution); auditd rule designer (select object/operations/key → preview auditctl command → test ausearch)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L2; quiz-and-challenge for L3 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Run `ufw status verbose` on the simulator; identify the default incoming and outgoing policy, list all active allow rules, and explain what happens to a packet that does not match any rule
- **Intermediate:** Run `getenforce` and `sestatus`; read the /etc/selinux/config file; explain the difference between permissive mode (logs but does not enforce) and enforcing mode (logs and blocks) and state which mode is required for production
- **Advanced:** Write three auditctl rules — one watching /etc/passwd for writes (-p wa), one watching /etc/sudoers for reads and writes (-p rwa), one watching all execve syscalls by root (euid=0) — then use ausearch -k to confirm each rule logged a test event
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level
