##### A3.C4.M3.L1 — Linux Firewall Concepts, nftables, and UFW
- **Slug:** linux-firewall-nftables-ufw | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** NET-20, OS-L05 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M2.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (ufw status, ufw status verbose, ufw status numbered, ufw default deny incoming, ufw default allow outgoing, ufw allow ssh, ufw allow 22/tcp, ufw allow from 10.0.1.0/24 to any port 22, ufw allow from 10.0.1.0/24 to any port 443, ufw deny 23/tcp, ufw deny from 203.0.113.0/24, ufw enable, ufw disable, ufw logging on, ufw logging medium, ufw show added, ufw delete 3, ufw reset, nft list ruleset, nft list tables, cat /var/log/ufw.log | tail -20), ClickableDiagram (packet processing decision tree: arriving packet → default incoming policy check → rule list top-to-bottom → first match wins → allow or deny — each decision point labeled with the UFW directive that controls it), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** ufw-default-deny-implicit-drop-all, ufw-rule-first-match-order, nftables-tables-chains-rules-hierarchy, ufw-logging-on-deny-entry-anatomy
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 111, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domain 3, CIS Benchmark Ubuntu 22.04 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Network Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, DevOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** Packet processing flowchart text-labeled with aria descriptions per decision node; UFW command syntax explained in a plain-language reference table; nftables hierarchy (tables/chains/rules) described in text before the animation; ufw log entry decoded field by field; all command examples in accessible code blocks; rule numbering in ufw status numbered explained for screen-reader users

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Understand the Linux host firewall architecture: the kernel's netfilter framework processes all incoming and outgoing packets through hook points in the network stack; nftables is the modern kernel subsystem (replacing iptables as of kernel 5.x) that programs these hook points with match-and-action rules organised into tables (inet, ip, ip6), chains (input, output, forward), and rules (match conditions → verdict); UFW (Uncomplicated Firewall) is the Ubuntu-native management frontend that translates human-readable commands into nftables (or iptables, depending on the system) rules, making host firewall management approachable without requiring nftables syntax knowledge — view the underlying rules that UFW generated with `nft list ruleset`; the base policy before any rules are configured is to allow all traffic — making the first step of any host hardening `ufw default deny incoming` (which establishes an implicit DROP for all inbound traffic not explicitly allowed) while `ufw default allow outgoing` (which allows all outbound traffic from the host by default, permitting package updates and outbound connections)
2. Write UFW rules for a host firewall policy: `ufw allow 22/tcp` allows SSH from any source; `ufw allow from 10.0.1.0/24 to any port 443` restricts HTTPS access to the admin subnet only — always apply the most specific rule for sensitive services; `ufw deny 23/tcp` explicitly blocks Telnet; `ufw deny from 203.0.113.0/24` blocks all traffic from a specific source network; rules are processed in order — the first matching rule wins, which is why explicit deny rules for known-bad sources should appear early; `ufw enable` activates the firewall (persists across reboots); `ufw logging on` enables logging of blocked packets to /var/log/ufw.log with one line per denied packet showing the input interface, source IP:port, destination IP:port, protocol, and the rule that matched (or [UFW BLOCK] for the default policy)
3. Manage and troubleshoot UFW rules: `ufw status verbose` shows the full policy with default rules and all active rules in order; `ufw status numbered` adds index numbers for use with `ufw delete N` to remove a specific rule without rewriting the whole policy; `ufw show added` lists rules as the ufw commands that would recreate them; test the firewall from the outside by using `nc -zv 10.0.1.5 22` (should connect) and `nc -zv 10.0.1.5 80` (should hang or refuse) from a source host — or read /var/log/ufw.log to confirm blocked packets are being logged; `ufw reset` returns UFW to its initial unconfigured state and disables it — use with extreme caution on production systems

**Skills Gained:** netfilter/nftables/iptables relationship, UFW as nftables frontend, ufw default deny incoming / allow outgoing, ufw allow port/protocol, ufw allow from subnet to any port N, ufw deny port, ufw deny from network, ufw enable/disable, ufw logging on, /var/log/ufw.log entry anatomy, ufw status verbose (full policy), ufw status numbered + delete N, ufw show added (command form), nft list ruleset (underlying rules), first-match-wins rule ordering, nc -zv for firewall testing
**AI Mentor:** The default-deny principle is the foundation of host hardening: "A correctly configured host firewall is a default-deny policy with a whitelist of explicitly allowed services. If a new service starts listening on an unexpected port, it is automatically blocked — you have to intentionally open it. Most Linux servers need only two or three inbound rules: SSH from the admin network, the service port from its clients, and nothing else. Every other port is closed. This makes `ss -tlnp` output on a hardened server very short — which is exactly what you want."

---

##### A3.C4.M3.L2 — SELinux and AppArmor: Mandatory Access Control
- **Slug:** selinux-apparmor-mandatory-access-control | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L11 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M3.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (getenforce, sestatus, cat /etc/selinux/config, ls -laZ /var/www/html/, ls -laZ /etc/shadow, ps -eZ | grep httpd, id -Z, chcon -t httpd_sys_content_t /var/www/html/index.html, restorecon -v /var/www/html/index.html, restorecon -Rv /var/www/html/, semanage fcontext -l | grep "/var/www", semanage port -l | grep http, semanage port -a -t http_port_t -p tcp 8080, setenforce 0, setenforce 1, grep "avc:  denied" /var/log/audit/audit.log | tail -5, sealert -a /var/log/audit/audit.log 2>/dev/null | head -40, audit2allow -a -M mymodule, aa-status, aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, aa-complain /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, aa-disable /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, cat /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx, grep "apparmor" /var/log/syslog | grep "DENIED" | tail -10), ClickableDiagram (MAC enforcement layers: process → DAC check (rwx) → MAC check (SELinux type enforcement or AppArmor profile) → allow or deny with AVC log entry — click each layer showing how a compromised process is still blocked even with valid DAC permissions), ComparisonTable (SELinux vs. AppArmor: MAC model/labeling/policy type/primary distros/violation log/management tools/complexity), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** selinux-context-label-four-parts, type-enforcement-process-to-file-allow-check, apparmor-profile-capability-rules-structure, avc-denied-log-entry-anatomy, restorecon-relabeling-effect
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, RHCSA EX200, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder | **Accessibility:** SELinux context label four-part anatomy described in text before animation; AVC denied log entry decoded field by field in a plain-language table; AppArmor profile capability rules listed with plain-language descriptions; MAC vs. DAC diagram text-labeled with aria per layer; all command examples in accessible code blocks; SELinux and AppArmor sections clearly headed for navigation

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Understand mandatory access control and SELinux: Discretionary Access Control (DAC) — standard Linux rwx permissions — is controlled by the file owner and can be bypassed by root; Mandatory Access Control (MAC) is enforced by the kernel regardless of owner decisions and cannot be bypassed even by root; SELinux implements MAC through type enforcement: every process runs with a security context (user:role:type:level, e.g., `system_u:system_r:httpd_t:s0`) and every file has a security context (`system_u:object_r:httpd_sys_content_t:s0`) — the SELinux policy defines which process types are allowed to access which file types; if the httpd process (type httpd_t) tries to access a file labeled with type shadow_t (the type for /etc/shadow), the kernel denies the access regardless of Unix permissions because the policy contains no allow rule for httpd_t to shadow_t; view process contexts with `ps -eZ | grep httpd` and file contexts with `ls -laZ /var/www/html/`; check the current mode with `getenforce` (Enforcing/Permissive/Disabled) and `sestatus` for full policy information
2. Diagnose and resolve SELinux policy violations: when SELinux denies access, it writes an AVC (Access Vector Cache) denial to /var/log/audit/audit.log — the entry shows the source context (the process), target context (the file or socket), class (file/tcp_socket/capability), permission requested (read/write/connect), and whether the policy contains a dontaudit rule that would suppress similar denials; `sealert -a /var/log/audit/audit.log` provides human-readable explanations of each denial with suggested remediation commands; the most common fix is a mislabeled file — use `chcon -t correct_type_t /path/to/file` for a temporary fix, then `restorecon -Rv /path/` to restore the file's label to the policy default persistently; when a service needs to use a non-standard port, add a port label with `semanage port -a -t http_port_t -p tcp 8080`; `audit2allow -a -M mymodule` converts all current AVC denials into a custom policy module — use sparingly and review the generated rules before loading, because audit2allow will generate allow rules for anything in the log including security events
3. Configure AppArmor profiles: AppArmor is the default MAC system on Ubuntu (SELinux is default on RHEL/Fedora); instead of labeling all files, AppArmor confines specific programs using profiles stored in /etc/apparmor.d/ — each profile lists the filesystem paths the program may access and what capabilities it may use; check AppArmor status with `aa-status` (shows loaded profiles, enforcing profiles, and confined processes); put a profile in enforce mode with `aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/profilename` (denies and logs violations) or complain mode with `aa-complain /etc/apparmor.d/profilename` (logs but does not deny — useful for testing a new profile); reload a modified profile with `apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/profilename`; AppArmor violations appear in /var/log/syslog as lines containing `apparmor="DENIED"` followed by the operation, profile name, and the exact path or capability that was blocked

**Skills Gained:** DAC vs. MAC concept (why MAC resists root bypass), SELinux four-part context label (user:role:type:level), type enforcement (process type → file type allow rules), getenforce/setenforce 0/1/sestatus, /etc/selinux/config (SELINUX=enforcing/permissive/disabled), ls -laZ and ps -eZ context display, AVC denied log entry fields (scontext/tcontext/tclass/permission), sealert -a (human-readable denial explanation), chcon -t (temporary context fix), restorecon -Rv (persistent policy-default relabeling), semanage port -a (port context addition), audit2allow -a -M (custom module — use cautiously), aa-status, aa-enforce/aa-complain (enforce vs. complain mode), apparmor_parser -r (profile reload), AppArmor violation in /var/log/syslog
**AI Mentor:** The permissive mode trap is common: "A SELinux system in permissive mode is not protected. Permissive mode logs denials but allows everything — it is a development tool, not a production setting. After troubleshooting, always return to enforcing with `setenforce 1` and make the fix persistent in /etc/selinux/config by setting `SELINUX=enforcing`. A compliance audit that finds SELINUX=permissive or SELINUX=disabled is a failed audit. The same applies to AppArmor profiles in complain mode — complain mode is for profile development; enforce mode is for production."

---

##### A3.C4.M3.L3 — Least Privilege, sudo Security, and Permission Auditing
- **Slug:** least-privilege-sudo-security-permission-auditing | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L03, OS-L02 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M3.L1, A3.C4.M3.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (sudo -l, sudo -l -U alice, sudo visudo, sudo visudo -f /etc/sudoers.d/webapp, cat /etc/sudoers, sudo -u webapp /opt/app/reload.sh, useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/lib/webapp webapp, passwd -l webapp, passwd --status webapp, chage -l webapp, id webapp, find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null, find / -perm -2000 -type f 2>/dev/null, find / -perm -o+w -not -path "/proc/*" -not -path "/tmp/*" -not -path "/dev/*" -type f 2>/dev/null, ls -laZ /usr/bin/passwd, stat /usr/bin/sudo, chmod u-s /usr/local/bin/suspicious-binary, getfacl /var/www/html/, setfacl -m u:webapp:rX /var/www/html/, getfacl /var/www/html/), ComparisonTable (sudoers rule components — user/host/runas/commands — with least-privilege examples vs. over-permissive examples), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** sudo-rule-anatomy-user-host-runas-commands, suid-execution-owner-privilege-elevation, world-writable-file-attack-vector, service-account-nologin-shell-restriction
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, RHCSA EX200, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder | **Accessibility:** sudoers rule anatomy diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per field; SUID execution flow described in numbered steps; world-writable risk explained in text before animation; service account creation steps in an accessible numbered sequence; all find command examples decoded step by step; ACL commands explained with plain-language descriptions

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Configure sudoers for least privilege: the sudoers file (/etc/sudoers, edited only with `sudo visudo` which validates syntax before saving) grants users the ability to run specific commands as specific other users; the rule syntax is `user host=(runas_user) commands` — `alice ALL=(root) /usr/bin/systemctl restart webapp` grants alice permission to restart the webapp service as root from any host, nothing else; `webapp ALL=(root) NOPASSWD: /opt/app/reload.sh` grants the webapp service account permission to run one specific reload script without a password (appropriate for automation); never grant `ALL=(ALL) ALL` (unrestricted root) or `ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL` (unrestricted root without password) unless the account is a human administrator who needs broad access — these are effectively root shells; `sudo -l` shows a user's own sudo permissions; `sudo -l -U alice` (requires root) shows another user's permissions; drop sudoers snippets in /etc/sudoers.d/ for package-managed or application-specific grants — simpler to manage than editing the main file
2. Create and secure service accounts: services should run as dedicated accounts with no login capability — use `useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/lib/servicename servicename` (the -r flag creates a system account with a UID below 1000, -s /usr/sbin/nologin prevents interactive shell access, -d sets the home directory to the service's data directory rather than /home/); lock the account password with `passwd -l servicename` (the -l flag prefixes the hashed password with ! making it invalid — the account cannot authenticate with a password but can still be used for su -s /bin/bash with sudo if needed for testing); verify with `passwd --status servicename` which shows L (locked) or P (password set); running services as root is the single most common configuration that turns a remote code execution vulnerability into a complete system compromise — the attacked service runs with root privileges and the attacker inherits them
3. Audit SUID binaries and world-writable files: `find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null` lists all SUID executables on the system — SUID (set-user-ID) means the file executes with the owner's privileges rather than the caller's privileges, so a SUID root binary allows any user to run it with root privileges; compare the output against a known-good baseline of expected SUID binaries (maintained in /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt); any SUID binary not in the baseline is a finding — investigate its purpose and remove the SUID bit with `chmod u-s /path/to/binary` if it is not legitimately needed; `find / -perm -o+w -not -path "/proc/*" -not -path "/tmp/*" -not -path "/dev/*" -type f 2>/dev/null` finds world-writable files outside expected locations — any world-writable file in /etc or /usr is a critical finding because any local user can modify it; view and set POSIX ACLs for fine-grained access with `getfacl` and `setfacl -m u:username:permissions path`

**Skills Gained:** sudo visudo (safe edit with validation), sudoers rule syntax (user host=(runas) commands), /etc/sudoers.d/ drop-in snippets, least-privilege sudo examples vs. over-permissive (ALL=(ALL)), NOPASSWD scoped to specific commands only, sudo -l (own permissions), sudo -l -U user (another user's permissions), useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d (system/service account), passwd -l (lock), passwd --status (L=locked/P=set/NP=none), why services as root multiplies exploit impact, find -perm -4000 (SUID binary audit), SUID execution mechanism (file owner privilege, not caller), chmod u-s to remove SUID, find -perm -o+w (world-writable audit), getfacl/setfacl ACL management
**AI Mentor:** Frame service account security in terms of blast radius: "Every service running as root has root-level blast radius. If nginx has a buffer overflow vulnerability and nginx runs as root, a remote attacker gets a root shell. If nginx runs as the www-data account with no sudo access, a root shell requires a second privilege escalation step — and if SELinux or AppArmor is enforcing, even that may be blocked. The principle of least privilege is not a compliance checkbox — it is a force multiplier for every other security control on the system."

---

##### A3.C4.M3.L4 — System Auditing with auditd
- **Slug:** system-auditing-auditd | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L15 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M3.L1, A3.C4.M3.L2, A3.C4.M3.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (systemctl status auditd, auditctl -l, auditctl -s, auditctl -w /etc/passwd -p wa -k identity, auditctl -w /etc/sudoers -p rwa -k sudoers-change, auditctl -w /etc/shadow -p rwa -k identity, auditctl -a always,exit -F arch=b64 -S execve -F euid=0 -k privileged, auditctl -a always,exit -F arch=b64 -S openat -F path=/etc/ssh/sshd_config -F perm=wa -k ssh-config, auditctl -D, cat /etc/audit/rules.d/audit.rules, nano /etc/audit/rules.d/hardening.rules, augenrules --load, ausearch -k identity, ausearch -k privileged -ts recent, ausearch -k identity -ts today -i, ausearch -k identity -ui alice, aureport --summary, aureport -l, aureport -au, aureport -f, ausearch --comm sudo -ts today -i | tail -30), ClickableDiagram (auditd architecture: syscall/file event → kernel audit hook → audit daemon → /var/log/audit/audit.log — click each component for description of what it captures and how rules control it), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** auditctl-watch-rule-syscall-event-trigger, ausearch-key-filter-matching-records, aureport-summary-statistics-generation, audit-log-record-type-fields-decode
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, LPIC-1 Topic 108, CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domain 2, CIS Benchmark Ubuntu 22.04 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Linux Administrator, Forensics Analyst, Security Engineer | **Accessibility:** auditd architecture diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per component; audit rule types (-w file watch vs. -a syscall rule) documented in separate accessible tables; ausearch output fields decoded field by field; aureport report types listed with plain-language descriptions; all auditctl rule syntax decoded with field-by-field explanations in text; screen-reader accessible throughout

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Understand the auditd architecture and write file-watch rules: auditd is the kernel-level auditing subsystem — the kernel has an audit hook at the system call layer; when a monitored event occurs, the kernel writes an audit record to the audit buffer, which the auditd daemon reads and appends to /var/log/audit/audit.log; rules are loaded into the kernel with auditctl or from /etc/audit/rules.d/*.rules files (persistent, loaded by `augenrules --load` or on auditd restart); `auditctl -w /etc/passwd -p wa -k identity` creates a file-watch rule: -w specifies the path to watch, -p specifies the permission (r=read, w=write, x=execute, a=attribute change), -k specifies a key string used to search and filter; the rule generates an audit record whenever /etc/passwd is written to or its attributes are changed; add parallel rules for /etc/shadow, /etc/sudoers, /etc/ssh/authorized_keys, and /etc/ssh/sshd_config to create a comprehensive configuration change audit trail; check active rules with `auditctl -l` and the auditd subsystem status with `auditctl -s`
2. Write syscall-level audit rules: `auditctl -a always,exit -F arch=b64 -S execve -F euid=0 -k privileged` creates a syscall rule: -a always,exit means generate a record on every exit from the specified syscall (always=always generate, exit=generate at syscall exit point); -F arch=b64 filters to 64-bit syscalls (specify both b64 and b32 for complete coverage on systems that run 32-bit binaries); -S execve matches the execve system call (every program execution passes through execve); -F euid=0 filters to events where the effective UID at the time of the syscall is 0 (root); -k privileged is the key; this single rule logs every command executed as root, including via sudo — providing a complete audit trail of privileged command usage; write rules to persistent files in /etc/audit/rules.d/ and load with `augenrules --load` so they survive reboots; place locks at the end of the rules file to prevent in-memory modification during an incident (`-e 2` locks the audit configuration until reboot)
3. Search and analyse audit logs: `ausearch -k keyname` searches /var/log/audit/audit.log for records matching the specified key; `-ts recent` limits to the last 10 minutes; `-ts today` limits to today; `-i` translates UIDs, GIDs, and syscall numbers to human-readable names; `-ui username` filters by user; `aureport --summary` generates a summary report of all audit event types since log rotation; `aureport -l` reports all login events; `aureport -au` reports all authentication attempts (success and failure); `aureport -f` reports all file access events; for incident response, `ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | grep "sudo\|bash\|python\|sh"` extracts all privileged command executions today and filters for common attacker tools — any unexpected privileged execution of bash, python, or sh is a high-confidence indicator of post-exploitation activity

**Skills Gained:** auditd architecture (kernel hook → audit buffer → auditd daemon → /var/log/audit/audit.log), auditctl -l (list rules), auditctl -s (subsystem status), auditctl -D (delete all rules — use for testing only), file-watch rule syntax (-w path -p rwa -k key), permission letters (r/w/x/a), syscall rule syntax (-a action,filter -F arch=b64 -S syscall -F field=value -k key), execve syscall for command execution logging, -F euid=0 for root-effective-uid filter, /etc/audit/rules.d/ persistent rules, augenrules --load, -e 2 lock flag, ausearch -k/-ts/-i/-ui, ausearch recent/today time shortcuts, aureport --summary/-l/-au/-f, privileged execution detection pattern (ausearch -k privileged with bash/python/sh filter)
**AI Mentor:** Position auditd as the continuous evidence trail: "auditd is the difference between being able to say 'the attacker ran these commands at these times using this account' and 'something happened, we're not sure what.' Without auditd rules on /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow, and /etc/sudoers, a privilege escalation leaves no forensic evidence in system logs — only in the volatile process table, which is lost at reboot. With auditd, every write to those files produces a timestamped record including the PID, the UID, the parent process, and the full syscall context. This is the evidence base that lets an incident report say something specific rather than something vague."

---

### Module A3.C4.M3 — Practical

**Title:** Apply a Security Baseline to a Linux Server
**Type:** guided-lab | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** A Linux application server at Helix Corp has passed network configuration review but has not yet had its host security baseline applied. The learner must configure the host firewall, verify MAC enforcement, implement least-privilege for the webapp service account, and deploy auditd monitoring rules.

**Tasks:**
1. Configure UFW: `ufw default deny incoming`, `ufw default allow outgoing`, `ufw allow from 10.0.1.0/24 to any port 22`, `ufw allow 443/tcp`, `ufw logging on`, `ufw enable`. Run `ufw status verbose` and document the complete policy.
2. Verify AppArmor is enforcing: `aa-status`. Identify any profiles in complain mode; put them in enforce mode with `aa-enforce`. Confirm nginx is confined: `aa-status | grep nginx`.
3. Create the webapp service account: `useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/lib/webapp webapp`. Lock the password: `passwd -l webapp`. Verify: `passwd --status webapp`. Grant the account a single sudoers privilege: create /etc/sudoers.d/webapp containing `webapp ALL=(root) NOPASSWD: /opt/app/reload.sh`. Validate with `sudo visudo -c`.
4. Audit SUID binaries: `find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null`. Compare against the baseline at /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt. Identify and document the one unexpected SUID binary present in the simulator.
5. Deploy auditd rules: create /etc/audit/rules.d/hardening.rules with four rules: watch /etc/passwd (-p wa -k identity), watch /etc/sudoers (-p rwa -k sudoers-change), watch /etc/shadow (-p rwa -k identity), and a syscall rule auditing all execve calls by root (-F euid=0 -k privileged). Load: `augenrules --load`. Verify: `auditctl -l`. Trigger a test: write to /etc/passwd with `sudo touch /etc/passwd`. Confirm the event: `ausearch -k identity -ts recent -i`.

**Assessment Criteria:** UFW policy complete and verified with status verbose (25%), AppArmor profiles in enforce mode confirmed (15%), webapp service account locked with correct sudoers grant (25%), unexpected SUID binary identified (10%), auditd rules loaded and test event confirmed with ausearch (25%)

### Module A3.C4.M4 — Linux Hardening and Maintenance

**Module Objectives:**
1. Apply security patch management using apt security updates, dpkg --verify, and unattended-upgrades
2. Implement backup strategies using rsync, tar, and test-restore validation; understand recovery planning fundamentals
3. Execute a systematic hardening checklist covering service minimisation, filesystem mount options, kernel parameters, and logging configuration
4. Apply CIS Benchmark Level 1 principles and implement a repeatable continuous hardening workflow

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L15 (Auditd — continuous monitoring in hardening context), OS-L04 (package management — patch and verification), OS-L09 (logging — hardening the log pipeline), GRC-01 (governance — CIS Benchmark as a compliance framework)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner applies all pending security updates, verifies package integrity with dpkg --verify, creates a full rsync backup and validates it with a test restore, disables three unnecessary services, applies three hardening kernel parameters via sysctl, and produces a written hardening assessment score against a CIS-derived checklist with identified gaps and remediation commands for each failing control
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (apt update, apt list --upgradable, apt upgrade -y, apt-get dist-upgrade, unattended-upgrades --dry-run, dpkg --verify, dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$", dpkg -l | grep "^rc", apt autoremove, rsync -avz --delete /var/www/html/ /backup/html/, rsync -avz --checksum /backup/html/ /var/www/html/, tar -czf /backup/etc-$(date +%Y%m%d).tar.gz /etc/, sha256sum /backup/etc-*.tar.gz > /backup/checksums.sha256, sha256sum -c /backup/checksums.sha256, systemctl list-units --state=active --type=service, systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled, systemctl disable --now avahi-daemon cups bluetooth, cat /etc/fstab, mount | grep "on / ", sysctl -a | grep "net.ipv4", sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward, sysctl -w net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects=0, cat /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf, sysctl --system, journalctl --disk-usage, journalctl --vacuum-size=500M, cat /etc/systemd/journald.conf), ClickableDiagram (hardening lifecycle: assess → plan → implement → verify → schedule reassessment — each phase labeled with tools and deliverables), ComparisonTable (backup approaches — full rsync vs. tar archive vs. LVM snapshot — use case/speed/space/restore complexity/off-site suitability), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** apt-security-update-vs-full-upgrade, dpkg-verify-modified-file-detection, rsync-backup-verify-checksum, sysctl-kernel-parameter-immediate-effect, unattended-upgrades-schedule-and-log, cis-benchmark-control-assessment-flow
**Simulations:** Vulnerability manager (shows pending security updates with CVE references → learner selects and applies → severity reduction score updates); hardening scorer (learner applies sysctl settings → score against CIS Level 1 updates in real time)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L3; quiz-and-challenge for L2 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Run `apt list --upgradable` and count how many security updates are pending; run `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null` and explain what a line beginning with 5 indicates about that file
- **Intermediate:** Create a tar archive of /etc with a datestamped filename, generate a SHA-256 checksum file, then verify the checksum matches; explain why checksum verification detects both corruption and tampering
- **Advanced:** Set three sysctl hardening parameters persistently in /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf (net.ipv4.ip_forward=0, net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects=0, kernel.randomize_va_space=2); reload with `sysctl --system`; verify each parameter with `sysctl parametername`
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level

##### A3.C4.M4.L1 — Patch Management and Package Verification
- **Slug:** patch-management-package-verification | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L04 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M3.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (apt update, apt list --upgradable, apt list --upgradable 2>/dev/null | grep -i security, apt upgrade -y, apt-get dist-upgrade, unattended-upgrades --dry-run, unattended-upgrades --debug, cat /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/50unattended-upgrades, cat /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/20auto-upgrades, dpkg --verify, dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^\.$", dpkg -V openssh-server, dpkg -l | grep "^rc", apt purge $(dpkg -l | awk '$1=="rc"{print $2}'), apt autoremove --purge, cat /var/log/unattended-upgrades/unattended-upgrades.log | tail -20), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** apt-update-vs-upgrade-distinction, security-updates-ubuntu-repo-label, dpkg-verify-checksum-mismatch-detection, unattended-upgrades-reboot-required-indicator
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 102, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, DevOps Engineer, Security Engineer | **Accessibility:** apt upgrade output decoded field by field in text; dpkg --verify output field meanings (permissions/type/checksum/links/owner/group/size/modification time) in an accessible table; unattended-upgrades configuration file annotated field by field; auto-upgrade configuration Boolean values explained in plain language; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Manage security updates systematically: `apt update` refreshes the package index from all configured repositories (does not install anything); `apt list --upgradable 2>/dev/null | grep -i security` filters upgradable packages to those from Ubuntu's security pocket (packages marked with the `security` origin are patches for published CVEs, not feature updates); `apt upgrade -y` installs all available upgrades in place without removing packages; `apt-get dist-upgrade` additionally resolves dependency changes — appropriate for kernel updates and packages that require removing outdated versions; after major upgrades, check whether a reboot is needed with `test -f /var/run/reboot-required && echo "REBOOT REQUIRED"` (kernel and libc updates require a reboot to take effect); remove packages that were automatically installed as dependencies and are no longer needed with `apt autoremove --purge` (--purge removes configuration files as well as binaries), and remove leftover configuration remnants from previously removed packages with `apt purge $(dpkg -l | awk '$1=="rc"{print $2}')` (rc in dpkg -l means removed but config remains)
2. Automate security updates with unattended-upgrades: `unattended-upgrades` applies security updates automatically on a schedule — the package is installed by default on Ubuntu 20.04+ and configured in two files: /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/20auto-upgrades (controls whether automatic update and upgrade are enabled — `APT::Periodic::Update-Package-Lists "1"` and `APT::Periodic::Unattended-Upgrade "1"` enable daily operation) and /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/50unattended-upgrades (controls which origins are updated and advanced options including automatic reboot); test the configuration with `unattended-upgrades --dry-run --debug` to see what would be applied without making changes; review the log at /var/log/unattended-upgrades/unattended-upgrades.log to confirm daily runs are succeeding and check whether updates are being held back due to configuration restrictions; unattended-upgrades for security patches is a CIS Benchmark Level 1 requirement for internet-connected servers
3. Verify package integrity with dpkg --verify: `dpkg --verify` checks every installed package's files against the checksums and metadata recorded when the package was installed — a clean system produces no output; each line of output represents a modified file, with a field format: a code character per metadata attribute (5=checksum mismatch, M=permissions differ, U=user/owner differs, G=group differs, T=timestamp differs) followed by the file type (f=file, d=directory) and the path; `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^\.$"` suppresses permission-only changes on /var and similar expected administrative modifications; a line showing `5....T.. /usr/bin/passwd` means the binary's checksum and timestamp both differ from the package record — this is a finding requiring investigation, as it may indicate the binary was replaced by a trojanized version; run dpkg --verify monthly as part of routine maintenance, and run it immediately after any suspected compromise

**Skills Gained:** apt update (index refresh only), apt list --upgradable with security grep, apt upgrade (in-place), apt-get dist-upgrade (dependency resolution), /var/run/reboot-required check, apt autoremove --purge (dependency cleanup), dpkg -l rc-state cleanup, unattended-upgrades --dry-run --debug, /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/20auto-upgrades (auto-update/auto-upgrade toggles), /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/50unattended-upgrades (origin filter/reboot settings), unattended-upgrades log location, dpkg --verify (field code meanings: 5=checksum/M=perms/U=user/G=group/T=time), dpkg -V packagename (per-package check), checksum mismatch as binary tampering indicator
**AI Mentor:** The dpkg --verify technique is a fast triage tool during incident response: "When you suspect a server has been compromised, `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null` gives you a fast integrity sweep of every system binary and library. On a clean system, the output is empty. On a compromised system, a rootkit that replaces /usr/bin/passwd or /bin/su to capture credentials will show up as a checksum mismatch — unless the attacker was sophisticated enough to also update the dpkg database, which is rare. Run this as one of your first responder steps, alongside checking /proc/PID/exe for running processes with deleted binaries."

---

##### A3.C4.M4.L2 — Backup Strategies and Recovery Planning
- **Slug:** backup-strategies-recovery-planning | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M4.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (rsync -avz --delete /var/www/html/ /backup/html/, rsync -avz --checksum /backup/html/ /verify/html/, rsync -avz --dry-run --delete /var/www/ /backup/www/, rsync --stats -avz /var/www/html/ /backup/html/, tar -czf /backup/etc-$(date +%F).tar.gz /etc/, tar -tzf /backup/etc-2026-07-28.tar.gz | head -20, tar -xzf /backup/etc-2026-07-28.tar.gz -C /restore-test/, sha256sum /backup/etc-*.tar.gz, sha256sum /backup/etc-2026-07-28.tar.gz > /backup/checksums.sha256, sha256sum -c /backup/checksums.sha256, diff -rq /etc/ /restore-test/etc/ 2>/dev/null, rsync -avz /local/path/ user@backup-server:/remote/path/, crontab -l, cat /etc/cron.d/backup-job), ComparisonTable (backup approaches — rsync incremental / tar archive / LVM snapshot / cloud object storage — coverage/speed/storage/restore complexity/off-site suitability), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** rsync-delta-incremental-backup, tar-create-verify-extract-cycle, sha256-checksum-tamper-detection, three-two-one-rule-diagram, backup-test-restore-as-validation
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 104, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Site Reliability Engineer, Incident Responder, DevOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** Backup comparison table has proper column and row headers; 3-2-1 rule diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per component; tar command flags decoded in a plain-language reference table; checksum verification steps explained as a numbered sequence; diff output format described before the interactive demo; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Implement a backup strategy using rsync and tar: rsync is ideal for incremental synchronisation of live directories — `rsync -avz --delete /source/ /backup/` copies all changes, additions, and deletions; the trailing slash on the source path is significant (it means the contents of source/, not source/ itself); `--delete` removes files from the destination that were removed from the source, keeping the backup a true mirror; `--dry-run` previews what would change without executing it; `--checksum` recomputes checksums for all files rather than relying on size+timestamp, making the sync more thorough but slower; `--stats` prints transfer statistics including total bytes sent and received; for point-in-time archives, `tar -czf /backup/etc-$(date +%F).tar.gz /etc/` creates a gzip-compressed archive of /etc with a datestamped filename; verify archive integrity with `tar -tzf archive.tar.gz | head` to list contents and `sha256sum archive.tar.gz > archive.sha256` to record the checksum; a backup is only as good as its last verified restore — periodically run `tar -xzf archive.tar.gz -C /restore-test/ && diff -rq /etc/ /restore-test/etc/` to confirm the archive is valid and compare against the live files
2. Apply the 3-2-1 backup rule and plan for recovery: three copies of data (production + two backups), two different media types (local disk + network storage or tape), one copy offsite (rsync to a remote server, cloud object storage, or encrypted external drive stored offsite); the offsite copy protects against physical events (fire, flood, theft) that would destroy both the production system and a co-located local backup; schedule backups with cron or systemd timers: `0 2 * * * rsync -avz --delete /var/www/html/ /backup/html/ >> /var/log/backup.log 2>&1` runs a daily backup at 2 AM and logs output; always log backup output and monitor the log for failures — a backup that fails silently for three weeks provides no protection; document the Recovery Time Objective (RTO — how long restoration takes) and Recovery Point Objective (RPO — how much data loss is acceptable) for each system before an incident forces the question under pressure
3. Plan and test the restore process: a backup that has never been tested is a theory, not a plan; define the restore procedure as a runbook with exact commands: stop the service, clear the restore target, extract the archive, set correct permissions, start the service, verify functionality; test the runbook on a staging system quarterly; for configuration files: `tar -xzf /backup/etc-2026-07-28.tar.gz -C /restore-test/` extracts to a test directory; `diff -rq /etc/ /restore-test/etc/` identifies differences between the backup and current state; for web content: `rsync -avz --checksum /backup/html/ /verify/html/` with --checksum ensures a byte-for-byte comparison; time the restore process on a representative dataset and document the result — that is the actual RTO; ransomware incidents in particular require that backups are stored in a location that cannot be reached from the compromised system, because ransomware encrypts all accessible network shares as well as local storage

**Skills Gained:** rsync -avz --delete (incremental mirror), trailing slash significance (contents vs. directory), rsync --dry-run (preview), rsync --checksum (full verification mode), rsync --stats (transfer summary), rsync to remote host via SSH, tar -czf with date-stamped name (tar -czf /backup/etc-$(date +%F).tar.gz /etc/), tar -tzf (list archive contents), tar -xzf -C (extract to alternate directory), sha256sum archive + sha256sum -c verification, diff -rq (recursive quick difference), 3-2-1 backup rule (3 copies/2 media/1 offsite), cron backup scheduling with log redirection, RTO/RPO concept, backup isolation from production network (ransomware protection), restore runbook as a required deliverable
**AI Mentor:** The untested restore is the most common backup failure mode: "Organisations back up their systems every night for years and then discover during a ransomware incident that the restoration process has never been tested, the backup server has been offline for three months, or the backup contains empty archives because a cron job silently failed. Test your restore process at least quarterly. Time it. Document the exact commands. Store the runbook somewhere offline. The incident at 3 AM is the wrong time to discover that `tar -xzf` complains about a truncated archive."

---

##### A3.C4.M4.L3 — Hardening Checklist: Services, Mounts, and Kernel Parameters
- **Slug:** hardening-checklist-services-mounts-kernel | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L09, OS-L08 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M4.L1, A3.C4.M4.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (systemctl list-units --state=active --type=service, systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled, systemctl disable --now cups avahi-daemon bluetooth, systemctl mask cups, systemctl status cups, cat /etc/fstab, mount | grep "on / ", mount | grep "nosuid\|noexec\|nodev", sudo mount -o remount,noexec,nosuid,nodev /tmp, echo "tmpfs /tmp tmpfs defaults,noexec,nosuid,nodev,size=256M 0 0" | sudo tee -a /etc/fstab, sysctl -a | grep "net.ipv4", sysctl -a | grep "kernel", sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward, sysctl net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects, sysctl kernel.randomize_va_space, cat /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf, sysctl --system, sysctl -p /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf, journalctl --disk-usage, journalctl --vacuum-size=500M, cat /etc/systemd/journald.conf, grep "^Storage" /etc/systemd/journald.conf, ls -la /var/log/journal/), ClickableDiagram (hardening checklist categories: attack surface reduction → network hardening → filesystem hardening → kernel parameters → logging hardening — click each category for control list and verification command), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** service-minimisation-attack-surface-reduction, fstab-mount-option-noexec-effect, sysctl-network-parameter-packet-decision, aslr-randomize-va-space-effect, journal-vacuum-size-rotation
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3, CIS Benchmark Ubuntu 22.04 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, DevOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** Hardening category diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per category and verification commands; fstab mount option effects explained in a plain-language table; sysctl parameters listed with descriptions and recommended values; journal configuration directives annotated field by field; all command examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Minimise attack surface by disabling unnecessary services: `systemctl list-units --state=active --type=service` shows all currently running services; `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled` shows all services configured to start on boot; compare against a service whitelist (the minimal set required for the server's purpose) and disable anything not on the list with `systemctl disable --now servicename` (--now also stops it immediately without requiring a reboot); `systemctl mask servicename` goes further — it symlinks the unit file to /dev/null, preventing it from being started manually or as a dependency — use masking for services that should never run on this system (cups on a headless server, bluetooth on a VM, avahi-daemon on a server with no mDNS requirement); each unnecessary service is a potential attack vector through its listening port, its SUID binaries, or its configuration files — the rule is: if you cannot explain why a service is running on this specific server, disable it and see if anything breaks
2. Apply filesystem mount hardening options: the /etc/fstab options field can apply per-mount security restrictions: `noexec` prevents execution of any binary from the mounted filesystem (applicable to /tmp, /var/tmp, /home — prevents an attacker who can write files from executing them); `nosuid` prevents SUID and SGID bits from taking effect on the mounted filesystem (an SUID binary in /tmp gains no privileges); `nodev` prevents character and block device files from being interpreted as devices on the mounted filesystem; apply these options to /tmp — the most common malware staging location — with `tmpfs /tmp tmpfs defaults,noexec,nosuid,nodev,size=256M 0 0` in /etc/fstab, limiting it to 256 MB of RAM-backed storage with no executable, SUID, or device permissions; immediately apply the options to the currently mounted /tmp without rebooting with `mount -o remount,noexec,nosuid,nodev /tmp` and verify with `mount | grep "on /tmp"` confirming the options appear in the output
3. Apply kernel hardening parameters via sysctl: kernel behavior parameters are set at runtime with `sysctl -w parameter=value` (immediate, non-persistent) or persistently in /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf (applied on boot and with `sysctl --system`); the ten most impactful security parameters: `net.ipv4.ip_forward=0` (disable IP forwarding — unless this host is a router, it should not forward packets between interfaces); `net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects=0` and `net.ipv6.conf.all.accept_redirects=0` (reject ICMP redirect messages — a vector for route poisoning attacks); `net.ipv4.conf.all.send_redirects=0` (do not send ICMP redirects); `net.ipv4.conf.all.log_martians=1` (log packets with impossible source addresses — useful for detecting spoofing); `kernel.randomize_va_space=2` (enable full ASLR — Address Space Layout Randomisation makes memory exploit addresses unpredictable; 2 = full randomisation, the default on modern Ubuntu but explicit setting documents the intent); `kernel.core_dump_restrict=0` (disable core dumps for SUID programs to prevent memory disclosure); `fs.suid_dumpable=0` (equivalent restriction for all processes with extra privileges)

**Skills Gained:** systemctl list-units --state=active --type=service, systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled, systemctl disable --now (stop + disable), systemctl mask (symlink to /dev/null — unstart-able), service whitelist approach to attack surface reduction, /etc/fstab mount options (noexec/nosuid/nodev), tmpfs /tmp with size limit and security options, mount -o remount (live application without reboot), sysctl -w (immediate, non-persistent), /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf (persistent parameter file), sysctl --system (reload all sysctl.d files), key sysctl parameters (ip_forward/accept_redirects/send_redirects/log_martians/randomize_va_space=2/core_dump_restrict/suid_dumpable), journal --disk-usage and --vacuum-size for log rotation management, journald.conf Storage=persistent
**AI Mentor:** The noexec-on-/tmp control is one of the highest-leverage hardening steps for minimal effort: "The majority of Linux exploitation chains require the attacker to write an executable payload to a world-writable directory and then execute it. If /tmp has noexec, the attacker can write the file but cannot execute it — they must find a different execution vector, which significantly raises the cost of exploitation. This one fstab line has stopped real attacks. Combined with AppArmor profiling the web server, it makes code execution from a web application vulnerability much harder to complete even if the initial compromise succeeds."

---

##### A3.C4.M4.L4 — CIS Benchmark and Continuous Hardening Workflows
- **Slug:** cis-benchmark-continuous-hardening | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** evaluate | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L15, OS-L11 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M4.L1, A3.C4.M4.L2, A3.C4.M4.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (cat /opt/cis-check/cis-level1-results.txt, grep "FAIL" /opt/cis-check/cis-level1-results.txt | wc -l, grep "PASS" /opt/cis-check/cis-level1-results.txt | wc -l, cat /opt/cis-check/section-1-filesystem.txt, cat /opt/cis-check/section-5-ssh.txt, cat /opt/cis-check/section-6-users.txt, systemctl list-timers --all | grep hardening, cat /etc/systemd/system/hardening-check.timer, cat /etc/systemd/system/hardening-check.service, auditctl -l, ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | wc -l, aureport --summary, dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | wc -l, ufw status verbose, aa-status, getenforce 2>/dev/null || aa-status 2>/dev/null | head -5), ClickableDiagram (CIS Benchmark structure: benchmark document → Level 1 controls (minimal impact, widely applicable) → Level 2 controls (higher security, may affect functionality) — click each section for section title, control count, and example controls), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** cis-benchmark-section-structure-overview, automated-check-pass-fail-evidence-gathering, hardening-timeline-assess-implement-verify-reassess, compliance-gap-remediation-command-workflow
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domain 5, CIS Benchmark Ubuntu 22.04 LTS, LPIC-1 Topic 110 | **Careers:** Security Engineer, Linux Administrator, SOC Analyst, Compliance Analyst, DevSecOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** CIS structure diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per level and section; Level 1 vs. Level 2 comparison explained in plain-language table; automated check output decoded with PASS/FAIL format explained; remediation command table accessible with proper headers; all assessment criteria described in text before the evaluation exercise

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Understand the CIS Benchmark structure and apply it as an assessment framework: the Center for Internet Security (CIS) publishes benchmarks for major Linux distributions — the CIS Benchmark for Ubuntu Linux 22.04 LTS contains over 250 individual controls organised into sections (1. Initial Setup and Filesystem Configuration, 2. Services, 3. Network Configuration, 4. Host Based Firewalls, 5. Access Control and SSH, 6. System Maintenance and Auditing, etc.); each control is classified as Level 1 (recommended for all servers — minimal operational impact) or Level 2 (defence-in-depth — may restrict some functionality, more appropriate for high-security environments); each control specifies a rationale (why it matters), an audit command (how to check the current state), and a remediation command (how to fix it if the check fails); a CIS Level 1 compliant server passes all Level 1 checks — organisations pursuing PCI-DSS, ISO 27001, FedRAMP, and SOC 2 compliance commonly require CIS Level 1 as a baseline
2. Conduct a systematic hardening assessment and produce a gap report: read the pre-generated CIS assessment results at /opt/cis-check/cis-level1-results.txt; count passing and failing controls; for each FAIL entry identify the control number and title, the current observed state, the required state per the benchmark, and the remediation command; prioritise remediation by risk: critical findings (open network services, missing authentication controls, disabled audit logging) before moderate findings (filesystem configuration, account aging policies) before informational findings (banner text, log verbosity settings); produce a structured gap report with exactly four columns for each finding: Control ID, Finding, Current State, Remediation Command — the remediation commands must be tested commands that work on this system, not copied from the benchmark document without verification
3. Establish a continuous hardening workflow: hardening is not a one-time event — new vulnerabilities, configuration drift, new services, and new team members all erode a hardened baseline over time; implement continuous hardening with three mechanisms: (1) a scheduled hardening check using a systemd timer running a script monthly that checks the ten most critical controls and logs results to /var/log/hardening-check.log with `logger -t hardening-check`; (2) auditd rules from Module 3 that alert when critical configuration files (/etc/passwd, /etc/sudoers, /etc/ssh/sshd_config) are modified — any modification outside a known change window is a drift indicator; (3) quarterly manual reassessment using the full CIS benchmark audit commands to identify new gaps introduced since the last assessment; document the assessment date, assessor, findings count, and the target remediation date for each failing control in a hardening register that is reviewed at the start of each quarter

**Skills Gained:** CIS Benchmark structure (Level 1 vs. Level 2, section organisation), CIS control anatomy (rationale/audit command/remediation command), PASS/FAIL assessment output reading, gap report format (Control ID/Finding/Current State/Remediation), remediation prioritisation by risk (critical/moderate/informational), hardening as a continuous process vs. point-in-time event, three continuous hardening mechanisms (scheduled check/auditd drift detection/quarterly reassessment), hardening register concept (findings/dates/remediation tracking), compliance framework alignment (PCI-DSS/ISO 27001/FedRAMP/SOC 2 use CIS Level 1 as baseline)
**AI Mentor:** Ground the compliance connection in operational reality: "CIS Level 1 compliance is not just a paperwork exercise — it is a forcing function for implementing the controls that make attackers work harder. A server that passes CIS Level 1 has no unnecessary services running, no world-writable configuration files, a host firewall with a default-deny policy, SSH hardened with key-based authentication only, auditd logging privileged commands, and packages up to date. An attacker who lands on this server has a much smaller tool set and much less room to maneuver than on a default Ubuntu installation. The benchmark did not prevent the initial compromise — but it reduces the blast radius and increases the dwell-time before detection."

---

### Module A3.C4.M4 — Practical

**Title:** Perform a Complete Linux Hardening Assessment
**Type:** free-lab | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** Helix Corp's security team requires a hardening assessment of a newly provisioned Ubuntu 22.04 production server before it accepts traffic. The learner must assess the current state, apply required controls, and produce a written hardening report documenting each finding and the remediation applied.

**Tasks:**
1. **Service audit:** Run `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled` and compare against the provided baseline at /opt/baseline/required-services.txt. Identify and disable all services not in the baseline with `systemctl disable --now` and mask any that should never run with `systemctl mask`.
2. **Package updates:** Run `apt list --upgradable 2>/dev/null`. Apply all security updates with `apt upgrade -y`. Run `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null` and document any files with checksum mismatches.
3. **Filesystem hardening:** Add noexec, nosuid, and nodev options to /tmp in /etc/fstab. Apply immediately with `mount -o remount,noexec,nosuid,nodev /tmp`. Verify with `mount | grep /tmp`.
4. **Kernel parameters:** Create /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf with five parameters: `net.ipv4.ip_forward=0`, `net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects=0`, `net.ipv6.conf.all.accept_redirects=0`, `net.ipv4.conf.all.log_martians=1`, `kernel.randomize_va_space=2`. Apply: `sysctl --system`. Verify each: `sysctl parametername`.
5. **Assessment report:** Write a structured hardening report in /tmp/hardening-report.txt with four sections: (1) Executive Summary — server hostname, assessment date, total findings, critical/moderate/informational counts; (2) Findings Table — Control ID, Description, Current State, Remediation Applied; (3) Verification Evidence — the output of key verification commands (ufw status verbose, aa-status, auditctl -l, dpkg --verify output); (4) Remaining Gaps — any controls that could not be addressed in this session with the reason and a recommended remediation timeline.

**Assessment Criteria:** All non-baseline services disabled and masked (20%), security updates applied and dpkg --verify run (15%), /tmp hardening applied and verified (15%), sysctl parameters in 99-hardening.conf and verified (20%), hardening report complete with all four sections including evidence and remaining gaps (30%)

---

### Course A3.C4 — Capstone

**Title:** Operation Ironwall — Secure and Harden a Production Linux Web Server
**Type:** quiz-and-capstone | **Duration:** 90 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** Learner is a security engineer at Helix Corp. The company has taken over a web application server from a third-party contractor. The contractor's handover documentation is incomplete and the security team has identified multiple concerns: the SSH configuration is non-standard and potentially insecure, unnecessary network services are listening on public interfaces, security patches have not been applied in two months, the firewall has no default-deny policy, file permissions on web content directories are misconfigured, there have been suspicious login attempts in auth.log, and the backup process consists of a cron job that has not been verified since it was set up. The learner connects to the server via a TerminalSimulator and must assess, remediate, and document each issue in a complete hardening report — the deliverable that the security team will use to certify the server for production use.

1. **Initial assessment — establish the security baseline:** Execute the discovery sequence to understand the server's current state before changing anything: run `ss -tlnp` and record all listening services and their PIDs; run `ufw status` to determine whether a firewall is active; run `getenforce 2>/dev/null` and `aa-status 2>/dev/null | head -5` to determine the MAC enforcement state; run `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | grep -v disabled` to list all enabled services; run `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | head -20` to identify modified package files; run `apt list --upgradable 2>/dev/null | wc -l` to count pending updates; run `sudo grep -iE "failed password|invalid user|accepted" /var/log/auth.log | tail -30` to review recent authentication events; run `cat /etc/ssh/sshd_config | grep -v "^#" | grep -v "^$"` to read the current SSH configuration; document every finding from this sequence as the Assessment section of the hardening report — do not begin remediation until the assessment is complete

2. **SSH hardening — secure remote administration:** Back up the current SSH configuration: `sudo cp /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/ssh/sshd_config.contractor-backup`; read the backup with `sudo cat /etc/ssh/sshd_config.contractor-backup` and identify the non-compliant settings — the simulator has PermitRootLogin set to yes, PasswordAuthentication set to yes, and no MaxAuthTries or AllowUsers directive; apply the Helix Corp SSH hardening standard by editing /etc/ssh/sshd_config: set PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no, MaxAuthTries 3, LoginGraceTime 30, AllowUsers admin webapp-deploy, X11Forwarding no, and add a banner at /etc/ssh/sshd_config pointing to /etc/ssh/banner.txt with the text "Authorised use only. All activity on this system is monitored and logged. Unauthorised access is prohibited and will be reported to law enforcement."; validate: `sudo sshd -t`; reload: `sudo systemctl reload ssh`; verify by opening a new terminal (simulated) and confirming key-based login works, root login is rejected, and the banner appears; check whether Fail2Ban is installed (`systemctl status fail2ban`) — if not, install it, create /etc/fail2ban/jail.local with `[sshd]` section setting enabled=true, maxretry=5, findtime=5m, bantime=1h, and verify with `fail2ban-client status sshd`

3. **Firewall configuration — implement default-deny policy:** Run `ufw status verbose` — the simulator shows UFW is inactive with no rules configured; implement the Helix Corp firewall policy: `sudo ufw default deny incoming`, `sudo ufw default allow outgoing`, `sudo ufw allow from 10.0.0.0/8 to any port 22 comment "Admin SSH from internal"`, `sudo ufw allow 80/tcp comment "HTTP"`, `sudo ufw allow 443/tcp comment "HTTPS"`, `sudo ufw logging on`, `sudo ufw enable`; verify with `sudo ufw status verbose` and confirm exactly three allow rules exist; run `ss -tlnp` again and identify any service still listening on a public interface that is not covered by the firewall rules — the simulator has port 8080 listening with no corresponding UFW rule; investigate which service owns port 8080 with `ss -tlnp | grep 8080` and `systemctl status $(ss -tlnp | grep 8080 | awk '{print $NF}' | cut -d, -f2 | cut -d= -f2)`; if the service is not required per the server's stated purpose, disable it and remove the unused port from the running state

4. **Security patches and package audit:** Apply all pending security updates: `sudo apt update && sudo apt upgrade -y`; check whether a reboot is required: `test -f /var/run/reboot-required && echo "REBOOT REQUIRED AFTER MAINTENANCE WINDOW"` and record the result in the report; run `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null` after the upgrade and compare the output to the pre-upgrade output recorded in the assessment — any new checksum mismatches introduced by the upgrade require investigation; audit for unnecessary penetration testing tools: `dpkg -l | awk '$1=="ii" {print $2}' | grep -E "^(nmap|netcat|ncat|masscan|john|hydra|sqlmap|aircrack)"` — the simulator has one tool from this list installed; remove it with `sudo apt purge toolname -y` and verify removal; audit for packages in rc (removed but config remains) state: `dpkg -l | awk '$1=="rc"{print $2}'` and clean up with `sudo apt purge $(dpkg -l | awk '$1=="rc"{print $2}') -y 2>/dev/null`; verify that unattended-upgrades is configured and active: `systemctl status unattended-upgrades` and `cat /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/20auto-upgrades`

5. **File permissions and MAC enforcement:** Audit web content permissions: `ls -laR /var/www/html/ | head -30` — the simulator has /var/www/html/ owned by root with group-write enabled (chmod 664 on files, 775 on directories); remediate with `sudo chmod -R 644 /var/www/html/ && sudo chmod -R 755 /var/www/ && sudo chown -R www-data:www-data /var/www/html/`; run a SUID audit: `find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null` and compare against the CIS-compliant expected SUID list at /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt; identify and document the one unexpected SUID binary; remove its SUID bit with `sudo chmod u-s /path/to/binary`; run a world-writable file audit outside expected locations: `find / -perm -o+w -not -path "/proc/*" -not -path "/tmp/*" -not -path "/dev/*" -type f 2>/dev/null`; remediate any world-writable files in /etc or /var/www; verify AppArmor enforcement: `sudo aa-status | grep "profiles in enforce mode"` — if any web server profile is in complain mode, enforce it with `sudo aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/profilename`

6. **Audit and monitoring — deploy continuous visibility:** Deploy auditd rules persistently: create /etc/audit/rules.d/helix-hardening.rules with six rules: watch /etc/passwd (-p wa -k identity), watch /etc/shadow (-p rwa -k identity), watch /etc/sudoers (-p rwa -k sudoers-change), watch /etc/ssh/sshd_config (-p rwa -k ssh-config), watch /var/www/html/ (-p wa -k web-content-change), and a syscall rule for all execve by root (-F euid=0 -F arch=b64 -S execve -k privileged); load with `sudo augenrules --load` and verify with `auditctl -l`; verify the suspicious auth.log events from the assessment: `sudo grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10` to identify the top source IPs of failed SSH attempts; if any source IP appears more than 20 times and is not already banned by Fail2Ban, manually ban it: `sudo fail2ban-client set sshd banip IP`; configure persistent journal storage: add `Storage=persistent` to /etc/systemd/journald.conf and restart journald so journal survives reboots; schedule a monthly backup verification: create a cron entry `0 3 1 * * rsync -avz --checksum /var/www/html/ /backup/html/ >> /var/log/backup.log 2>&1` and verify the backup destination exists and is writable

7. **Hardening report — produce the security certification deliverable:** Write /root/helix-hardening-report.txt with seven sections: (1) Executive Summary — server hostname, IP address, assessment date, assessor name, overall security posture (Not Certified / Certified with Conditions / Certified), and counts of Critical/High/Medium/Low findings; (2) Pre-Assessment State — a table of every finding identified in Task 1 with current state documented; (3) SSH Hardening — configuration changes applied, before and after values for each directive, validation command output (sshd -t result), and Fail2Ban configuration; (4) Firewall Policy — complete UFW rule set with ufw status verbose output; (5) Patch and Package Audit — upgrade count applied, dpkg --verify findings, removed tools, auto-upgrade verification; (6) Permissions and MAC — SUID findings and remediation, world-writable findings and remediation, AppArmor enforcement status; (7) Ongoing Monitoring — auditd rules loaded (auditctl -l output), Fail2Ban IP bans, backup cron job, journal persistence; (8) Remaining Risks and Recommendations — any finding that could not be fully remediated in this session, the residual risk, the recommended remediation, and the target timeline; sign the report with `echo "Report completed: $(date)" >> /root/helix-hardening-report.txt && logger -t capstone "Hardening report complete for $(hostname)"` and verify the syslog entry with `grep "capstone" /var/log/syslog`

**Assessment Criteria:** Initial assessment completeness — all seven assessment commands executed and findings documented before remediation begins (15%), SSH hardening — all five sshd_config settings applied and validated with sshd -t, Fail2Ban active with jail.local settings (20%), firewall policy — UFW default-deny with three correct allow rules, unexpected port 8080 service identified and disabled (15%), patch management — upgrades applied, penetration tool removed, dpkg --verify run before and after (15%), permissions and MAC — web content permissions remediated, unexpected SUID found and bit removed, AppArmor in enforce mode (15%), audit and monitoring — all six auditd rules loaded, journal persistent, backup cron configured (10%), hardening report — all seven sections present, findings count accurate, residual risks identified with remediation timeline (10%)
**KG Validation:** OS-L05 (SSH architecture/hardening/key authentication), OS-L11 (AppArmor enforce mode verification and MAC enforcement), OS-L15 (auditd six-rule deployment and ausearch query), OS-L04 (package patching/dpkg --verify/tool removal), OS-L03 (service account audit/sudo verification), OS-L02 (SUID audit/world-writable remediation), NET-20 (UFW host firewall configuration), OS-L09 (journal persistence/syslog evidence trail) — all exercised and integrated in a realistic production hardening scenario
**Mastery Gate:** ≥80% to unlock Course A3.C5

---

## PART 5: COURSE A3.C5 — LINUX FOR CYBERSECURITY OPERATIONS

**Purpose:** This is the integration course. Courses 1–4 built the skills; this course applies them together in the context of real cybersecurity work. Learners stop administering Linux and start operating as cybersecurity practitioners who use Linux as their primary investigative and defensive platform. Log analysis, incident investigation, Bash automation for security operations, enterprise Linux security workflows, threat hunting, privilege escalation response, and end-to-end blue team operations — each topic draws on every previous course simultaneously. A learner who completes this course has demonstrated that they can use Linux not just as a system administrator but as a security analyst, incident responder, and defender. This is the competency that unlocks every downstream academy's applied security work.

**Learning Outcomes:**
- Conduct a structured log investigation using journalctl, /var/log/auth.log, /var/log/syslog, and auditd logs — extracting a coherent incident narrative from raw log data
- Build grep, awk, sed, cut, sort, and uniq pipelines to extract, transform, and count security-relevant log entries at analyst speed
- Identify indicators of compromise on a live Linux system: unauthorized users, SUID/SGID escalation paths, LD_PRELOAD injections, cron and systemd persistence, and unauthorized listening services
- Build and correlate a multi-source incident timeline with precise timestamps and causal relationships
- Write production-quality Bash security automation scripts using arrays, functions, set -euo pipefail, and structured output with exit codes
- Automate user auditing, integrity verification, and daily security health reporting as scheduled system jobs
- Explain Linux's role in enterprise security architecture and apply secure change management and configuration management principles
- Perform proactive threat hunting using MITRE ATT&CK as a hypothesis framework on a Linux target
- Detect and respond to privilege escalation attempts using SUID, capabilities, and LD_PRELOAD as attacker techniques
- Complete an end-to-end blue team investigation: initial alert → triage → containment → eradication → recovery → incident report

**Estimated Hours:** 10–13 hours | **Difficulty:** intermediate–advanced | **Prerequisites:** A3.C4 Capstone (≥80%)
**Career Relevance:** SOC Analyst (direct), Incident Responder, Blue Team Analyst, Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, Forensics Analyst, DevSecOps Engineer, Junior Penetration Tester
**Certification Alignment:** CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domains 2–4 (~35%), CompTIA CySA+ CS0-003 Domains 1–4 (~40%), CompTIA Linux+ XK0-005 Domain 4 (~60%), LPIC-1 Topics 108–110 (~50%), CEH (Linux exploitation and defense chapters, ~30%), OSCP preparation (Linux host enumeration and post-exploitation defense, ~25%)

---

### Module A3.C5.M1 — Log Analysis and Incident Investigation

**Module Objectives:**
1. Conduct a structured journalctl-based investigation workflow covering authentication, service, and boot logs
2. Build efficient log analysis pipelines using grep, awk, sed, cut, sort, and uniq to extract and quantify security events at scale
3. Identify indicators of compromise from system logs: unauthorized access, persistence mechanisms, privilege escalation, and suspicious processes
4. Build a multi-source incident timeline, correlate events across log files, preserve evidence, and produce investigation documentation

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L09 (Syslog/journald — primary node for this entire module), OS-L15 (Auditd — ausearch and aureport for incident investigation), OS-L08 (Process management — forensic process analysis from log artifacts), SEC-17 (Kill Chain — structuring the timeline around kill chain phases), SEC-18 (MITRE ATT&CK — mapping findings to ATT&CK techniques)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner extracts a coherent narrative from a seeded TerminalSimulator representing a compromised host — identifying the attacker's initial access timestamp from auth.log, lateral movement indicators from journalctl, persistence mechanism from syslog, and command execution from auditd; produces a structured incident timeline with six entries and maps each to a MITRE ATT&CK technique
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (journalctl -u ssh --since "2026-07-20" --until "2026-07-28" -n 100, journalctl -p err --since "24 hours ago" -n 50, journalctl _SYSTEMD_UNIT=nginx.service -n 30, journalctl -b -1, journalctl --list-boots, journalctl -b 0, journalctl -k --since "boot", grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log, grep "Accepted" /var/log/auth.log, awk '/Failed password/{ip=$11; count[ip]++} END{for(i in count) print count[i], i}' /var/log/auth.log | sort -rn, grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $1, $2, $3}' | sort | uniq -c, sed -n '/Jul 20 02:/,/Jul 20 04:/p' /var/log/auth.log, cut -d' ' -f1-3 /var/log/auth.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn, find / -mtime -3 -type f -not -path "/proc/*" -not -path "/sys/*" 2>/dev/null, find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null, awk -F: '$3==0{print}' /etc/passwd, awk -F: '$7=="/bin/bash"' /etc/passwd, sudo cat /etc/shadow | grep -v "^[^:]*:!" | grep -v "^[^:]*:\*", for u in $(ls /var/spool/cron/crontabs/ 2>/dev/null); do echo "=== $u ==="; sudo cat "/var/spool/cron/crontabs/$u"; done, systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled, ausearch -k privileged -ts "07/20/2026 02:00:00" -te "07/20/2026 04:00:00" -i, ausearch -k identity -ts recent -i, aureport -l --start 07/20/2026 --end 07/28/2026, ss -tnp, ls -la /tmp/ /var/tmp/, ls -la /root/.ssh/authorized_keys, cat ~/.bash_history, cat /proc/$(pgrep suspicious-name)/cmdline 2>/dev/null | tr '\0' ' '), ClickableDiagram (incident investigation workflow: alert received → initial triage (9-command sequence) → log deep-dive → IOC identification → timeline construction → evidence preservation → report — click each phase for tools and commands), AttackTimeline (seeded incident timeline widget: six events with timestamps, source, technique, and finding), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** journalctl-time-filter-output-decode, auth-log-failed-vs-accepted-pattern, awk-counting-ip-frequency-pipeline, sed-time-window-extraction, incident-timeline-causal-chain, mitre-attack-technique-mapping-linux
**Simulations:** Incident log explorer (pre-seeded /var/log/auth.log with a realistic brute-force and compromise sequence — learner uses grep/awk commands to extract the attacker IP, first successful login timestamp, and post-compromise commands); timeline builder (learner places findings in chronological order on an interactive timeline and labels each with the MITRE ATT&CK technique)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L2; quiz-and-challenge for L3 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Run `journalctl -u ssh -n 30` and identify: the service state changes, the last five authentication events, and any error-level entries; explain what "session opened for user root by (uid=0)" in auth.log indicates about privilege level
- **Intermediate:** Construct a single-pipeline command using grep, awk, sort, and uniq -c to count the number of failed SSH login attempts per source IP from /var/log/auth.log, sorted by frequency descending
- **Advanced:** Given a simulated auth.log with a brute-force attack followed by a successful login, extract: (1) the attacker's source IP, (2) the exact timestamp of the first successful login, (3) the username used — then map each finding to its MITRE ATT&CK technique (T1110.001 Password Guessing, T1078 Valid Accounts)
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level
