##### A3.C5.M1.L1 — journalctl Investigation Workflow
- **Slug:** journalctl-investigation-workflow | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L09, OS-L15 | **Prereqs:** A3.C4.M4.L4 (A3.C4 Capstone ≥80%)
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (journalctl --list-boots, journalctl -b -1 -n 50, journalctl -b 0 -p err, journalctl -k --since "boot" | head -30, journalctl -u ssh --since "2026-07-20 00:00:00" --until "2026-07-20 06:00:00", journalctl -u ssh -n 50 -r, journalctl _SYSTEMD_UNIT=nginx.service --since "1 hour ago", journalctl -p warning --since "today", journalctl --disk-usage, journalctl -F _SYSTEMD_UNIT, journalctl -o json-pretty -u ssh -n 3, journalctl --no-pager -u ssh --since "2026-07-20" | grep -iE "accepted|failed|invalid"), ClickableDiagram (journalctl filter taxonomy: time filters (--since/--until/-b) / unit filters (-u/unit=/_SYSTEMD_UNIT) / priority filters (-p) / output format (-o) / kernel (-k) — click each branch for the filter syntax and a security investigation use case), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** journalctl-boot-index-b-0-vs-b-minus1, journal-priority-levels-0-through-7, journal-time-filter-since-until-syntax, journalctl-json-output-for-siem-ingestion
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, LPIC-1 Topic 108, CompTIA Security+ Domain 3, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 1 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Linux Administrator, Forensics Analyst | **Accessibility:** journalctl filter taxonomy diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per branch; priority level table has both numeric (0–7) and name columns with plain-language descriptions; time filter syntax examples in accessible code blocks with date format decoded; JSON output format described before demo; keyboard navigation throughout TerminalSimulator

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Conduct a systematic journalctl investigation starting from boot context: `journalctl --list-boots` shows all boots in the journal with their index number, boot ID, and the time range covered — a system that rebooted unexpectedly during a suspected incident warrants investigating the prior-boot journal with `journalctl -b -1` (the boot before the current one); `journalctl -b 0` covers the current boot; `journalctl -k` (or `journalctl --dmesg`) shows only kernel messages for the current boot — essential for identifying kernel-level errors, OOM kill events, and hardware failures that might explain anomalous behavior; prioritise investigation by severity with `journalctl -p err` (error and above) or `journalctl -p warning` (warning and above) — on a clean server these return very few entries; a sudden spike in error-level journal entries is an investigation trigger
2. Filter the journal by service, time window, and field: `journalctl -u servicename` filters to a specific systemd unit — the most useful investigation target is `journalctl -u ssh` which shows all SSH daemon events including authentication attempts, session opens, and disconnects; narrow by time with `--since "2026-07-20 02:00:00" --until "2026-07-20 04:00:00"` to focus on a suspected incident window; add `-r` to reverse-sort (most recent first — preferred during active incident triage); combine filters: `journalctl -u nginx --since "today" -p err` shows only nginx errors since midnight; use `journalctl -F _SYSTEMD_UNIT` to list all units that have produced journal entries — useful for discovering unexpected daemons that logged output; extract structured fields with `journalctl -o json-pretty -u ssh -n 3` to inspect the full journal record including _PID, _UID, _COMM, _EXE, and MESSAGE fields — this is the format used by SIEM ingestion pipelines
3. Correlate authentication events and service events using journal timestamps: `journalctl -u ssh --since "window-start" --until "window-end"` produces a chronological record of all SSH events in the window — look for the pattern: repeated "Failed password for invalid user X from IP" entries followed by "Accepted password for Y from same-IP" (brute-force then success) or "Accepted publickey for Z from unexpected-IP" (compromised key or unauthorized key deployment); cross-reference with `journalctl _SYSTEMD_UNIT=cron.service` during the same window to catch scheduled tasks that ran immediately after a login event — a post-compromise cron installation typically triggers a reload of the cron daemon within minutes of the attacker's successful authentication; the combined journal timeline is the primary evidence source for Phase 1 of any Linux incident investigation

**Skills Gained:** journalctl --list-boots (boot index), journalctl -b N (specific boot), journalctl -k (kernel messages), journalctl -p level (priority filter: emerg/alert/crit/err/warning/notice/info/debug), journalctl -u unit (service filter), journalctl --since/--until (time window), journalctl -r (reverse/most-recent-first), journalctl -n N (last N lines), journalctl -F field (list unique values), journalctl -o json-pretty (structured output for SIEM), journalctl --disk-usage, SSH auth pattern recognition (Failed password/Accepted password/Accepted publickey/session opened for user root), brute-force-then-success timeline pattern, cross-service time correlation for incident reconstruction
**AI Mentor:** Frame journalctl as the first stop in any investigation: "When you receive an incident alert on a Linux system, your first two commands are `uptime` (establish how long the system has been in its current state) and `journalctl -p err --since "24 hours ago" -n 100` (establish what the system has been reporting as errors). Everything else branches from there. On a clean system, journalctl -p err returns almost nothing. On a compromised system, you often find the attack trace in the first 20 lines — a failed service, an unexpected process error, or a cron job that tried to run something that did not exist."

##### A3.C5.M1.L2 — Syslog Analysis: grep, awk, sed, cut, sort, and uniq
- **Slug:** syslog-analysis-grep-awk-sed-cut-sort-uniq | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L09 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M1.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | wc -l, grep -c "Accepted" /var/log/auth.log, grep -iE "failed|invalid|error" /var/log/syslog | tail -20, grep -v "CRON" /var/log/syslog | grep -v "systemd" | tail -30, awk '{print $1, $2, $3}' /var/log/auth.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10, awk '/Failed password/{ip=$11; count[ip]++} END{for(i in count) print count[i], i}' /var/log/auth.log | sort -rn | head -10, awk '/Accepted/{print $9, $11}' /var/log/auth.log, awk -F: '{print $1}' /etc/passwd | sort, awk '{print $5}' /var/log/auth.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5, sed -n '/Jul 20 02:00/,/Jul 20 04:00/p' /var/log/auth.log, sed 's/203\.0\.113\.[0-9]*/ATTACKER_IP/g' /var/log/auth.log | grep ATTACKER_IP | head -10, cut -d' ' -f1-3 /var/log/auth.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10, cut -d: -f1,3 /etc/passwd | sort -t: -k2 -n, sort -k5 -n /var/log/auth.log | head -10, grep "Accepted" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn, cat /var/log/auth.log | grep "$(date +"%b %e")" | grep "Failed" | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5), ClickableDiagram (log analysis pipeline anatomy: source → filter (grep) → extract (awk/cut) → transform (sed) → sort → deduplicate (uniq) → summarise (wc/head) — click each stage for the command and its role in the pipeline), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** grep-count-c-vs-wc-l-distinction, awk-field-separator-default-whitespace, awk-pattern-action-ip-counting, sed-address-range-time-window-extraction, sort-uniq-c-frequency-counting-pattern
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 103, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 1 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Forensics Analyst, Security Analyst | **Accessibility:** Pipeline anatomy diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per stage; all awk patterns explained in plain English before the command; sed address range notation decoded field by field; sort key flag (-k N) explained with column numbering; uniq -c output format described in text; all log examples in accessible code blocks with field positions labeled

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Build grep-based log filters for rapid security event extraction: `grep -c "pattern" file` counts matching lines without printing them — faster than `grep "pattern" file | wc -l` for count-only queries; `grep -iE "failed|invalid|error" file` is case-insensitive (-i) and uses extended regex (-E) to match any of several patterns in a single pass; `grep -v "pattern" file` inverts the match — essential for removing noise: `grep -v "CRON" /var/log/syslog | grep -v "systemd-logind" | tail -30` strips the two most common sources of syslog noise to reveal unusual entries; chain grep with a time filter using `grep "$(date +"%b %e")" /var/log/auth.log` to restrict to today's entries (format: "Jul 28"); for multi-file log analysis `grep -r "pattern" /var/log/` searches all files in /var/log/ — useful when an application writes to a non-standard log path
2. Extract and aggregate fields with awk for quantitative analysis: awk processes each line by splitting it on whitespace (or a specified delimiter with -F) and assigning fields to $1, $2, ... $NF; the auth.log format is `Month Day HH:MM:SS hostname process[PID]: message` — `$1 $2 $3` is the timestamp, $11 is the source IP for "Failed password" events (always verify field positions with `head -5 /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print NR, NF}'` to count fields); the canonical security awk idiom for frequency counting is `awk '/pattern/{key=$field; count[key]++} END{for(k in count) print count[k], k}'` — this pattern counts occurrences of any field value across a file in a single pass with no intermediate sort; `awk '/Accepted/{print $9, $11}' /var/log/auth.log` extracts username ($9) and source IP ($11) for every successful authentication — the complete list of who logged in from where during the investigation window
3. Apply sed and cut for transformation and extraction: `sed -n '/timestamp-start/,/timestamp-end/p'` extracts log lines within a time window without knowing exact line numbers — the -n flag suppresses default output, /pattern1/,/pattern2/ defines the address range, and p prints matching lines; `sed 's/known-attacker-IP/ATTACKER_IP/g'` normalises a known attacker IP for readability and allows consistent grep across redacted reports; `cut -d' ' -f1-3` extracts the first three space-delimited fields (the syslog timestamp) for frequency analysis; the complete analyst pipeline `grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10` is the single most used security pipeline in Linux log analysis — it produces a frequency-ranked table of source IPs generating failed SSH logins in under one second regardless of log size

**Skills Gained:** grep -c (count), grep -iE (case-insensitive + extended regex), grep -v (invert for noise removal), grep -r (recursive), grep with date expansion ($(date +"%b %e")), awk field splitting ($1..$NF), awk pattern-action blocks, awk associative array frequency counting (count[key]++), awk END block for final output, /var/log/auth.log field positions for common event types, sed -n '/start/,/end/p' time window extraction, sed s/pattern/replacement/g normalization, cut -d delimiter -f fields, sort -rn (reverse numeric), uniq -c (prefix count), sort | uniq -c | sort -rn full frequency pipeline, canonical security analysis pipeline (grep → awk → sort → uniq -c → sort -rn → head)
**AI Mentor:** Build the analyst's muscle memory: "Every SOC analyst working on Linux needs one pipeline memorised so deeply they can type it without thinking: `grep 'pattern' /var/log/file | awk '{print $FIELD}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -20`. This pipeline turns any log file into a frequency table for any field in under two seconds. Which source IPs generated the most failed logins? Which usernames are being brute-forced? Which processes are generating the most errors? Same pipeline, different field number. Master this one construct and you will be faster at log triage than most tools with GUIs."

##### A3.C5.M1.L3 — Finding Indicators of Compromise on a Linux System
- **Slug:** finding-indicators-of-compromise-linux | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** analyse | **Ethical Content:** true
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L06, OS-L09, OS-L13, OS-L15 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M1.L1, A3.C5.M1.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (awk -F: '($3==0){print}' /etc/passwd, awk -F: '($7=="/bin/bash"){print $1}' /etc/passwd, sudo awk -F: '($2!~/^\$/ && $2!="*" && $2!="!" && $2!="!!"){print $1, $2}' /etc/shadow 2>/dev/null, last | head -20, lastb | head -20, who, w, for u in $(ls /var/spool/cron/crontabs/ 2>/dev/null); do echo "=== $u ==="; sudo cat "/var/spool/cron/crontabs/$u"; done, sudo cat /etc/cron.d/* 2>/dev/null, ls -la /etc/cron.daily/ /etc/cron.weekly/, systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/enabled-services.txt 2>/dev/null, find /tmp /var/tmp /dev/shm -type f 2>/dev/null, find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt 2>/dev/null, find / -perm -2000 -type f 2>/dev/null, ls -la /proc/*/exe 2>/dev/null | grep deleted, ps aux | awk '{print $11}' | sort -u, ss -tnp | grep -v "127.0.0.1", cat /proc/$(pgrep -f suspicious)/cmdline 2>/dev/null | tr '\0' ' ', sudo cat /root/.bash_history 2>/dev/null | tail -30, sudo cat /home/*/.bash_history 2>/dev/null | grep -iE "wget|curl|nc|python|perl|bash -i|/dev/tcp" | head -20, sudo find / -name "*.php" -newer /var/log/dpkg.log -type f 2>/dev/null, echo $LD_PRELOAD, sudo cat /etc/ld.so.preload 2>/dev/null, ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | grep -iE "wget|curl|bash|python|sh -i" | head -20), ClickableDiagram (Linux IOC categories: account anomalies → persistence mechanisms → process anomalies → network anomalies → filesystem anomalies → log anomalies — click each category for detection commands and ATT&CK technique mapping), AttackTimeline (MITRE ATT&CK technique reference: T1078/T1136/T1053.005/T1543.002/T1059.004/T1574.006/T1036/T1562), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** deleted-binary-proc-exe-detection, ld-preload-injection-attack-vector, cron-persistence-event-trigger, suid-binary-unexpected-location-risk
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Security+ Domain 4, CompTIA CySA+ Domains 2 and 4, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Threat Hunter, Forensics Analyst | **Accessibility:** IOC category diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per category; ATT&CK technique IDs paired with plain-language names; all detection commands documented with field-by-field explanations; LD_PRELOAD concept explained in text before animation; deleted binary detection steps in numbered sequence; all commands in accessible code blocks; ethical framing applied explicitly before offensive technique discussion

**Ethical Content Note:** This lesson teaches attacker techniques (SUID abuse, LD_PRELOAD injection, cron persistence) from a defensive detection perspective. The context is always: "this is what attackers do — here is how to detect it." No operational offensive tooling is provided or referenced. All techniques described are oriented toward building detection capability, not exploitation capability. Learners are reminded that applying these techniques to systems they do not own or have explicit written permission to test is a criminal offence in most jurisdictions.

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Detect account and authentication anomalies: `awk -F: '($3==0){print}' /etc/passwd` identifies any account with UID 0 beyond root (T1136 — Create Account, T1078 — Valid Accounts); `awk -F: '($7=="/bin/bash"){print $1}' /etc/passwd` lists all accounts with an interactive shell — any account that should be a service account with /usr/sbin/nologin but has /bin/bash is a finding; `sudo awk -F: '($2!~/^\$/ && $2!="*" && $2!="!" && $2!="!!"){print $1}' /etc/shadow` identifies accounts with no password or a plaintext-like shadow entry; `last` shows historical login records (utmp-based — can be tampered with, but tampering is itself an IOC); `lastb` shows failed login attempts from the btmp log; `sudo cat /root/.bash_history` and all user bash_history files — look for wget/curl to external IPs, nc -e, python -c reverse shell one-liners, and bash -i /dev/tcp patterns (T1059.004 — Unix Shell)
2. Detect persistence mechanisms across all five locations: (1) cron — audit all five locations as covered in A3.C2; any entry downloading from the internet or executing from /tmp is critical; (2) systemd — `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/enabled-services.txt` reveals enabled units not in the approved baseline (T1543.002 — Create or Modify System Process: Systemd Service); (3) /tmp and /var/tmp — `find /tmp /var/tmp /dev/shm -type f` locates all files in world-writable directories; executable files here with no known owner are high-priority findings; (4) SUID binaries not in the baseline — unexpected SUID executables, especially in /tmp or /var/tmp, are T1548.001 — Abuse Elevation Control Mechanism: Setuid; (5) /etc/ld.so.preload — `sudo cat /etc/ld.so.preload` reads the global LD_PRELOAD file; any entry here means the listed library is injected into every process at startup — a rootkit-level persistence mechanism (T1574.006 — Hijack Execution Flow: LD_PRELOAD); also check `echo $LD_PRELOAD` and `sudo cat /proc/1/environ | tr '\0' '\n' | grep LD_PRELOAD` to detect per-session or per-process injection
3. Detect process and network anomalies: `ls -la /proc/*/exe 2>/dev/null | grep deleted` finds all processes whose binary has been deleted from disk (T1036 — Masquerading) — a common anti-forensic technique where malware deletes its own binary immediately after launch; `ps aux` with an eye for processes with names that resemble system processes but run from /tmp, /var, or home directories; `ss -tnp | grep -v "127.0.0.1"` shows all non-loopback established TCP connections with owning processes — an unexpected outbound connection from a web server process to an external IP on a high port is a command-and-control (C2) indicator (T1095); `sudo find / -name "*.php" -newer /var/log/dpkg.log -type f 2>/dev/null` finds PHP files modified or created more recently than the last package operation — web shells are nearly always newer than the surrounding application files; `ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i` from auditd (when rules from A3.C4.M3.L4 are in place) extracts all privileged command executions today — filter for wget/curl/bash/python executing as root without a known scheduled job to explain them

**Skills Gained:** UID 0 account detection, interactive shell account audit, shadow file plaintext-password detection, last/lastb login history, bash_history wget/curl/nc/reverse-shell pattern search, cron persistence (all five locations), systemd persistence (unit file baseline comparison), /tmp /var/tmp /dev/shm executable file detection, SUID unexpected-location detection, /etc/ld.so.preload LD_PRELOAD global injection, per-process LD_PRELOAD via /proc/1/environ, deleted binary detection via /proc/PID/exe symlink, process anomaly via ps with location check, ss -tnp non-loopback connection detection, PHP web shell detection (newer than dpkg.log), auditd privileged command extraction with tool filter, MITRE ATT&CK technique mapping (T1078/T1136/T1543.002/T1548.001/T1574.006/T1036/T1059.004/T1095)
**AI Mentor:** Lead with the defensive framing: "Understanding how attackers establish persistence on Linux makes you better at finding it — not better at doing it. The techniques in this lesson (cron persistence, LD_PRELOAD injection, SUID abuse, deleted binary execution) are all in MITRE ATT&CK because defenders observed them in real incidents and documented them so other defenders could detect them faster. Your job is to know these techniques well enough that when you see the artifacts they leave behind, you recognise them immediately. A SOC analyst who has never heard of LD_PRELOAD will miss a rootkit. A SOC analyst who understands it will find `cat /etc/ld.so.preload` in their investigation checklist."

##### A3.C5.M1.L4 — Building an Incident Timeline and Preserving Evidence
- **Slug:** incident-timeline-evidence-preservation | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** evaluate | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L09, OS-L15 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M1.L1, A3.C5.M1.L2, A3.C5.M1.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (date -u, timedatectl, ls -la --full-time /var/log/auth.log, stat /var/log/auth.log, stat /tmp/suspicious-file, sudo dd if=/dev/sda of=/dev/null bs=512 count=1 2>&1 | grep "records", sudo tar -czf /mnt/evidence/auth.log-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).tar.gz /var/log/auth.log, sha256sum /var/log/auth.log, sha256sum /mnt/evidence/auth.log-*.tar.gz, journalctl --since "2026-07-20 00:00:00" --until "2026-07-20 06:00:00" --no-pager -o short-iso > /mnt/evidence/journal-window.txt, ausearch -ts "07/20/2026 00:00:00" -te "07/20/2026 06:00:00" -i > /mnt/evidence/audit-window.txt, grep "Jul 20" /var/log/auth.log > /mnt/evidence/auth-jul20.txt, grep "Jul 20" /var/log/syslog > /mnt/evidence/syslog-jul20.txt, ps auxf > /mnt/evidence/processes-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).txt, ss -tnp > /mnt/evidence/connections-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).txt, netstat -rn > /mnt/evidence/routes-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).txt, ls -laR /tmp > /mnt/evidence/tmp-listing.txt, sha256sum /mnt/evidence/* > /mnt/evidence/MANIFEST.sha256, cat /mnt/evidence/MANIFEST.sha256), ClickableDiagram (evidence volatility order: CPU registers → running processes → network connections → routing table → ARP cache → open files → RAM contents → disk data → remote logs — click each tier for the collection command and why it degrades fastest), AttackTimeline (interactive timeline widget pre-loaded with blank entries — learner places six events from the investigation into the timeline, labels each with timestamp/source/event/ATT&CK technique), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** locard-exchange-principle-digital, evidence-volatility-tier-collection-order, sha256-chain-of-custody-hash-sequence, timeline-causal-chain-construction
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Security+ Domain 4, CompTIA CySA+ Domains 2 and 4, EC-Council CEH | **Careers:** Incident Responder, Forensics Analyst, SOC Analyst, Blue Team Analyst | **Accessibility:** Evidence volatility diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per tier and urgency level; SHA-256 chain of custody steps in numbered sequence; timeline widget keyboard-navigable with Tab for event placement; all evidence collection commands documented as a numbered runbook; MANIFEST format explained field by field

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Collect volatile evidence in volatility order before it is lost: digital evidence degrades at different rates — CPU registers and running memory (seconds to minutes), active network connections (minutes — ended by attacker disconnect or system cleanup), routing table and ARP cache (minutes to hours), open file descriptors (minutes to hours), RAM contents (lost at power off), disk contents (persistent until overwritten) — collect in this order; for a live Linux system: capture process list with `ps auxf > /mnt/evidence/processes-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).txt`, active connections with `ss -tnp > /mnt/evidence/connections-timestamp.txt`, routing table with `netstat -rn > /mnt/evidence/routes-timestamp.txt`, and world-writable directory contents with `ls -laR /tmp > /mnt/evidence/tmp-listing.txt` — all within the first five minutes; use UTC timestamps consistently in all filenames and log entries with `date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ` — local time zones cause ordering confusion when correlating across systems and are a common cause of incorrect incident timelines
2. Preserve log evidence with cryptographic integrity: evidence that cannot be proven unmodified is inadmissible and unreliable; archive log files with datestamped names: `sudo tar -czf /mnt/evidence/auth.log-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).tar.gz /var/log/auth.log`; generate a SHA-256 hash immediately after collection: `sha256sum /var/log/auth.log > /mnt/evidence/auth.log.sha256`; after the archive is created, hash the archive: `sha256sum /mnt/evidence/auth.log-*.tar.gz >> /mnt/evidence/auth.log.sha256`; create a MANIFEST of all collected evidence files: `sha256sum /mnt/evidence/* > /mnt/evidence/MANIFEST.sha256` — this manifest allows any future reviewer to verify that none of the evidence files were modified after collection; write the evidence to read-only media (USB drive or evidence server) before analysis — analysis must always be performed on a copy, never on original evidence
3. Build a structured incident timeline from multiple sources: a timeline entry has exactly five fields: UTC timestamp, source (which log or tool), event description, affected entity (user, process, IP, file), and ATT&CK technique; extract the relevant window from each source: `journalctl --since "2026-07-20 00:00:00" --until "2026-07-20 06:00:00" -o short-iso > /mnt/evidence/journal-window.txt`, `ausearch -ts "07/20/2026 00:00:00" -te "07/20/2026 06:00:00" -i > /mnt/evidence/audit-window.txt`, and timestamp-filtered copies of auth.log and syslog; read each source and extract events where the timestamp, entity, or action pattern is common between sources — a "Failed password for root from 203.0.113.45" in auth.log at 02:14:07 followed 47 seconds later by "Accepted password for alice from 203.0.113.45" in auth.log is a timeline entry pair; the causal relationship (brute-force leading to success) is the investigative finding, not the individual events in isolation; the completed timeline is the primary deliverable of Phase 1 of incident response

**Skills Gained:** evidence volatility order (processes → connections → routes → ARP → files → RAM → disk), UTC timestamp format for evidence files (date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ), ps auxf/ss -tnp/netstat -rn volatile collection commands, /mnt/evidence/ evidence directory convention, tar -czf datestamped archive, sha256sum for hash generation and verification, MANIFEST.sha256 chain of custody, write-to-read-only-media principle, analysis-on-copy-not-original, journalctl -o short-iso for ISO 8601 timestamps in journal export, ausearch -ts/-te time range for auditd export, five-field timeline entry format (UTC timestamp/source/event/entity/ATT&CK technique), causal relationship identification across multiple log sources
**AI Mentor:** The chain of custody is often skipped because investigations feel urgent: "In a live incident, collecting evidence feels slower than acting. But a hash you forgot to record, an evidence file you modified during analysis, or a timeline built on notes rather than log files can make your incident report contestable and reduce its value to legal proceedings. Evidence integrity takes three minutes per file. Do it before analysis, every time. The MANIFEST.sha256 file is the proof that nothing changed between collection and reporting. Without it, any competent attorney can challenge the integrity of your entire evidence set."

---

### Module A3.C5.M1 — Practical

**Title:** Investigate a Compromised Linux Server Using System Logs
**Type:** free-lab | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** Helix Corp's security operations team has received an alert: a production Linux server generated an unusual spike in outbound network connections at 02:30 AM, then went quiet. The server hosts a customer-facing web application. The learner has SSH access to a TerminalSimulator representing the live server and must conduct a full log investigation, identify the attack chain, preserve evidence, and deliver a concise incident timeline.

**Tasks:**
1. **Initial triage:** Execute the 9-command diagnostic sequence. Then run `journalctl -p err --since "2026-07-20 00:00:00" -n 50` and `ss -tnp`. Document every finding before touching anything. No remediation in this task.
2. **Authentication log analysis:** Run `grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10` to identify the top brute-force source IP. Run `grep "Accepted" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $9, $11, $1, $2, $3}'` to identify all successful logins with username, source IP, and timestamp. Identify the first successful login from the attacker IP.
3. **Persistence detection:** Audit all five cron locations. Run `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled`. Check `/tmp` and `/var/tmp` for executables. Check `/etc/ld.so.preload`. Run `ls -la /proc/*/exe 2>/dev/null | grep deleted`. Document every persistence artifact found.
4. **Evidence collection:** Create `/mnt/evidence/`. Archive auth.log with a UTC datestamped filename. Generate SHA-256 hashes. Export the journal window covering the attack to a text file. Export auditd records for the window. Create a MANIFEST.sha256.
5. **Timeline:** Build a six-entry timeline table with columns: UTC Timestamp | Source | Event | Affected Entity | ATT&CK Technique. Write it to `/mnt/evidence/incident-timeline.txt`.

**Assessment Criteria:** Correct attacker IP and first successful login timestamp identified (25%), all persistence mechanisms found and documented (30%), evidence archive and MANIFEST created with valid SHA-256 hashes (25%), six-entry timeline with correct ATT&CK mappings (20%)

### Module A3.C5.M2 — Automation for Security Operations

**Module Objectives:**
1. Write production-quality Bash security scripts using set -euo pipefail, typed arrays, functions with local variables, and structured exit codes that integrate with external monitoring systems
2. Build a modular Linux security audit script that checks user accounts, file permissions, running services, and listening ports and produces a severity-ranked formatted report
3. Implement automated file integrity monitoring using SHA-256 baselines, scheduled verification, and tamper-detection logic that exits non-zero when any monitored file changes
4. Schedule recurring security jobs with cron and systemd timers, apply correct file ownership and permissions to cron entries, and retrieve job output via the systemd journal

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L10 (Bash scripting and automation — primary node for this entire module), OS-L09 (Syslog/journald — integrating log analysis into automated scripts and reading scheduled job output), OS-L08 (Process management — scripted service and process auditing within check functions)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner produces a three-component security automation suite: a modular security audit script that passes shellcheck with zero warnings and exits with a severity-coded exit code; a file integrity monitor that detects a deliberate change to a monitored file with a FAILED entry in its output and exits non-zero; and a systemd timer unit with Persistent=true that schedules the audit script nightly and captures output to the journal — all components deployed to /usr/local/bin/ with executable permissions and verified with systemctl list-timers and journalctl
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (set -euo pipefail, bash -n script.sh, bash -x script.sh 2>&1 | head -30, shellcheck /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh, declare -a FINDINGS=(), FINDINGS+=("CRITICAL: extra UID-0 account detected"), printf '%s\n' "${FINDINGS[@]}", echo "Count: ${#FINDINGS[@]}", declare -A CONFIG=([ssh_port]=22 [max_auth_tries]=3 [log_file]=/var/log/security-audit.log), echo "${CONFIG[log_file]}", function check_uid0() { local accounts; accounts=$(awk -F: '($3==0){print $1}' /etc/passwd | grep -v "^root$"); [[ -n "$accounts" ]] && FINDINGS+=("CRITICAL: UID-0 accounts beyond root: $accounts"); }, function check_world_writable() { find /etc -perm -o+w -type f 2>/dev/null | while read -r f; do FINDINGS+=("HIGH: world-writable /etc file: $f"); done; }, function generate_report() { printf '\n=== SECURITY AUDIT REPORT ===\n'; printf 'Host: %s\n' "$(hostname -f)"; printf 'Generated: %s\n' "$(date -u)"; printf 'Findings: %d\n' "${#FINDINGS[@]}"; printf '%s\n' "${FINDINGS[@]}"; }, readonly EXIT_OK=0 EXIT_WARN=1 EXIT_CRITICAL=2, trap 'echo "Interrupted at line $LINENO" >&2; exit 130' INT TERM, sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/hosts /etc/fstab /etc/ld.so.preload > /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, cat /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED, echo "backdoor:x:0:0::/root:/bin/bash" >> /etc/passwd, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256; echo "Exit: $?", find /etc -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum > /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256, wc -l /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED, find /usr/bin /usr/sbin /bin /sbin -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum > /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED | wc -l, dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$", crontab -l, cat /etc/cron.d/security-automation, 0 2 * * * root /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh >> /var/log/security-audit.log 2>&1, run-parts --test /etc/cron.daily, cat /etc/systemd/system/security-audit.service, cat /etc/systemd/system/security-audit.timer, systemctl daemon-reload, systemctl enable --now security-audit.timer, systemctl list-timers, journalctl -u security-audit.service -n 20), CodeDiffViewer (naive bash script with no error handling and unquoted variables vs. production script with set -euo pipefail, local variables, FINDINGS array, and structured exit code — diff view with one-sentence annotation per changed line explaining the bug each fix prevents), ClickableDiagram (security automation architecture: set flags and constants → FINDINGS array → check function library → generate_report → exit code decision → cron/systemd scheduler → journal/log output — click each component for the code pattern and failure mode it prevents), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** set-euo-pipefail-three-flags-demonstrated, bash-array-append-iterate-printf-safe, bash-function-local-variable-scope-isolation, sha256-baseline-generation-and-verify-flow, sha256sum-c-failed-exit-code-scriptable, systemd-timer-persistent-true-missed-run-recovery, shellcheck-common-warning-codes-sc2086-sc2048
**Simulations:** Script builder (learner assembles a security check script from modular function blocks — selecting correct set flags, function patterns, array append operations, and exit codes — then runs shellcheck; errors are highlighted with the warning code and a plain-language fix); FIM demonstrator (learner generates a SHA-256 baseline, a tampered file is silently introduced, learner runs sha256sum -c and identifies the FAILED entry, then maps it to MITRE ATT&CK T1565 Data Manipulation)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1; quiz-and-challenge for L2, L3, and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Add `set -euo pipefail` to a provided script; explain what each of the three flags does (errexit, nounset, pipefail) and describe one specific class of bug each flag prevents in a security script context
- **Intermediate:** Write a Bash function called `check_listening_ports` that runs `ss -tlnp`, extracts port numbers using awk and cut, stores them in a local indexed array, and prints each entry prefixed with `[PORT]`; the function must use local for all variables and double-quote all variable expansions
- **Advanced:** Write a script that generates a SHA-256 baseline of /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow, /etc/sudoers, /etc/ssh/sshd_config, and /etc/hosts to /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, then implements a verify function that runs sha256sum -c on the baseline, prints only FAILED lines, and exits with code 2 if any failures are found
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level

##### A3.C5.M2.L1 — Production Bash Scripting Patterns for Security
- **Slug:** production-bash-scripting-patterns-security | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L10 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M1.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (set -e, set -u, set -o pipefail, set -euo pipefail, bash -n script.sh, bash -x script.sh 2>&1 | head -30, shellcheck script.sh, declare -a FINDINGS=(), FINDINGS+=("HIGH: world-writable /etc/shadow detected"), FINDINGS+=("LOW: no failed SSH logins in last 24 hours"), printf '%s\n' "${FINDINGS[@]}", echo "Count: ${#FINDINGS[@]}", declare -A CONFIG=([ssh_port]=22 [max_auth_tries]=3 [log_file]=/var/log/security-audit.log), echo "Port: ${CONFIG[ssh_port]}", function check_uid0_accounts() { local accounts; accounts=$(awk -F: '($3==0){print $1}' /etc/passwd | grep -v "^root$"); echo "$accounts"; }, result=$(check_uid0_accounts), [[ -n "$result" ]] && FINDINGS+=("CRITICAL: extra UID-0 account: $result"), function log_finding() { local severity="$1" message="$2"; printf '[%s] %s %s\n' "$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ)" "$severity" "$message" | tee -a "${LOG_FILE:-/dev/stderr}"; }, log_finding "HIGH" "unexpected SUID binary at /tmp/rootkit", trap 'echo "Script interrupted at line $LINENO" >&2; exit 130' INT TERM, readonly EXIT_OK=0 EXIT_WARN=1 EXIT_CRITICAL=2, [[ "${#FINDINGS[@]}" -eq 0 ]] && exit $EXIT_OK || exit $EXIT_CRITICAL), CodeDiffViewer (before: script.sh with no set flags, unquoted $VAR, and no exit code vs. after: set -euo pipefail, "${VAR}" everywhere, and exit $EXIT_CRITICAL — diff annotated with one sentence per change explaining the bug prevented), ClickableDiagram (production Bash script anatomy for security tools: shebang and set flags → readonly constants and CONFIG array → FINDINGS indexed array → check function library → main execution block → exit code decision — click each section for the pattern and the failure mode it prevents), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** set-e-silent-failure-without-vs-immediate-exit-with, set-u-rm-rf-empty-var-catastrophic-demonstration, set-o-pipefail-midpipe-grep-failure-masked, bash-trap-cleanup-on-int-term, shellcheck-sc2086-unquoted-variable-fix
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, LPIC-1 Topic 105, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Security Engineer, DevSecOps Engineer, Linux Administrator | **Accessibility:** Bash script anatomy diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per section; set flags explained in a three-column table (flag / full name / failure class prevented) with plain-language descriptions; CodeDiffViewer before/after annotated with one sentence per line explaining the bug each fix prevents; trap syntax decoded field by field in prose; exit code contract in a three-row table (code / constant name / when to use); all code examples in accessible code blocks with surrounding explanatory text

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Apply the three Bash safety flags to eliminate silent failures in security scripts: `set -e` (errexit) exits the script immediately when any command returns a non-zero exit code — without it, a failed command (such as a grep that cannot read /var/log/auth.log due to permissions) is silently ignored and execution continues to a conclusion it cannot validate; `set -u` (nounset) exits with an error if any undefined variable is referenced — this prevents the scenario where a typo in a variable name produces an empty string, turning `rm -rf "$TMPDIR/"` into `rm -rf /` when $TMPDIR was never set; `set -o pipefail` makes a pipeline exit non-zero if any command within it fails, not just the last one — without pipefail, `grep "pattern" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c` returns exit code 0 even when grep finds nothing, creating a false-clean pipeline that silently produces empty output; place `set -euo pipefail` on the second line of every security script immediately after the shebang; verify script syntax without execution with `bash -n script.sh` and trace each executed command with `bash -x script.sh 2>&1 | head -30` during development and debugging
2. Structure security scripts with typed arrays, associative arrays, functions, and readonly constants: `declare -a FINDINGS=()` creates an empty indexed array for accumulating security findings throughout the script; append with `FINDINGS+=("SEVERITY: description")` — the severity prefix (CRITICAL/HIGH/WARN/LOW) makes the array self-documenting and enables severity-based filtering at report time; check array length with `${#FINDINGS[@]}` (zero means no findings); `declare -A CONFIG=([ssh_port]=22 [max_auth_tries]=3 [log_file]=/var/log/security-audit.log)` creates an associative array that centralises all tunable values and avoids magic numbers scattered through the code; define named exit codes with `readonly EXIT_OK=0 EXIT_WARN=1 EXIT_CRITICAL=2` — readonly prevents accidental reassignment and names make the exit code semantics self-documenting; `function check_name() { local var; var=$(command); echo "$var"; }` is the correct function pattern — `local` restricts variables to the function scope, a critical correctness requirement when multiple check functions run sequentially and might use similar variable names; capture function output with `result=$(function_name)` and test with `[[ -n "$result" ]]` (non-empty output means a finding was detected)
3. Implement structured logging, trap cleanup, and shellcheck compliance: a reusable log function `function log_finding() { local severity="$1" message="$2"; printf '[%s] %s %s\n' "$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ)" "$severity" "$message" | tee -a "$LOG_FILE"; }` writes ISO 8601 timestamped entries to both stdout and a persistent log file simultaneously; `trap 'echo "Interrupted at line $LINENO" >&2; exit 130' INT TERM` registers a cleanup handler that fires when the script receives SIGINT (Ctrl+C) or SIGTERM — this prevents partial audit state in the log when the script is stopped mid-run; shellcheck is a static analysis tool that catches the four most common Bash bugs: SC2086 (unquoted variable — `$VAR` must be `"$VAR"` to prevent word splitting), SC2048 (unquoted array expansion — `${ARRAY[@]}` must be `"${ARRAY[@]}"`), SC2034 (assigned variable never read), and SC2046 (unquoted command substitution causing word splitting); passing `shellcheck script.sh` with zero warnings before deploying any security script to production is a baseline quality gate — an unattended script with a shellcheck warning is a latent reliability bug waiting to appear at 2 AM

**Skills Gained:** set -e (errexit), set -u (nounset), set -o pipefail (pipefail), bash -n (syntax check), bash -x (execution trace), declare -a FINDINGS=() (indexed array), FINDINGS+=() (append), printf '%s\n' "${FINDINGS[@]}" (safe iteration), ${#FINDINGS[@]} (array length), declare -A CONFIG=() (associative array), ${CONFIG[key]} (associative access), readonly EXIT_OK/EXIT_WARN/EXIT_CRITICAL, function with local variables, $(function_name) output capture, [[ -n "$var" ]] non-empty test, log_finding() with tee -a dual output, date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ (UTC timestamp), trap INT TERM cleanup, $LINENO in trap messages, shellcheck (SC2086/SC2048/SC2034/SC2046)
**AI Mentor:** Make the case for production standards from the first script: "Security scripts run unattended, as root, on production systems. A naive script with an unquoted variable, a silent pipeline failure, or no exit code is not poor style — it is a reliability hazard. When your audit script fails silently because /var/log/auth.log was temporarily locked, you receive a false-clean report. When your cleanup function runs `rm -rf $TMPDIR/` and $TMPDIR was never set, you lose files. `set -euo pipefail` converts silent failures into visible exits. shellcheck converts syntax errors into readable warnings before the script touches production. Every security script you write will run dozens or hundreds of times across your career. Write it right once."

---

##### A3.C5.M2.L2 — Building a Linux Security Audit Script
- **Slug:** linux-security-audit-script | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** create | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L10 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M2.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (cat /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh, chmod +x /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh, /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh, /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh 2>&1 | tee /var/log/security-audit-$(date -u +%Y%m%d).log, /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh; echo "Exit code: $?", shellcheck /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh, function check_uid0_accounts() { local accounts; accounts=$(awk -F: '($3==0){print $1}' /etc/passwd | grep -v "^root$"); [[ -n "$accounts" ]] && FINDINGS+=("CRITICAL: extra UID-0 accounts: $accounts"); }, function check_empty_passwords() { local accts; accts=$(sudo awk -F: '($2==""){print $1}' /etc/shadow 2>/dev/null); [[ -n "$accts" ]] && FINDINGS+=("CRITICAL: accounts with empty passwords: $accts"); }, function check_world_writable_etc() { find /etc -perm -o+w -type f 2>/dev/null | while read -r f; do FINDINGS+=("HIGH: world-writable /etc file: $f"); done; }, function check_suid_against_baseline() { local unexpected; unexpected=$(diff <(find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | sort) /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt 2>/dev/null | grep "^<" | sed 's/^< //'); [[ -n "$unexpected" ]] && while IFS= read -r bin; do FINDINGS+=("HIGH: unexpected SUID binary: $bin"); done <<< "$unexpected"; }, function check_failed_login_count() { local count; count=$(grep -c "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log 2>/dev/null || echo 0); [[ "$count" -gt 100 ]] && FINDINGS+=("WARN: $count failed login attempts in auth.log"); }, function generate_report() { printf '\n=== SECURITY AUDIT REPORT ===\n'; printf 'Host:      %s\n' "$(hostname -f)"; printf 'Generated: %s\n' "$(date -u)"; printf 'Kernel:    %s\n' "$(uname -r)"; printf 'Findings:  %d\n' "${#FINDINGS[@]}"; printf '%s\n' "${FINDINGS[@]}"; }, grep -c "^CRITICAL" <<< "$(printf '%s\n' "${FINDINGS[@]}")"), ClickableDiagram (security audit script module map: account anomaly checks / permission and filesystem checks / service and network checks / log analysis checks / report generator — click each module for the function names it contains and the Linux detection commands each function runs), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** bash-function-three-step-detect-test-append-pattern, while-read-r-safe-filename-loop-vs-for-in-find, diff-process-substitution-baseline-comparison, tee-dual-stdout-and-logfile-output, printf-over-echo-cross-platform-consistency
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA CySA+ Domain 3, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 1, CompTIA Security+ Domain 4 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Security Engineer, DevSecOps Engineer, Blue Team Analyst | **Accessibility:** Script module map diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per module; each function's purpose listed in a plain-language two-column table (function name / what it detects) before any code appears; diff process substitution explained step by step in prose before the command; while read -r safe pattern contrasted with the unsafe for-in-find pattern in a side-by-side accessible code block; report generator output fields listed with field names in a table; all code examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Structure a security audit script as a collection of independent check functions that append to a shared FINDINGS array: each function follows a strict three-step pattern — run the detection command, test whether the result indicates a problem, append to FINDINGS only when a problem exists; `function check_uid0_accounts() { local accounts; accounts=$(awk -F: '($3==0){print $1}' /etc/passwd | grep -v "^root$"); [[ -n "$accounts" ]] && FINDINGS+=("CRITICAL: extra UID-0 accounts: $accounts"); }` — the function name is self-documenting, `local accounts` prevents scope leakage between functions, the detection runs as a command substitution, `[[ -n "$accounts" ]]` tests for non-empty output meaning the check found something unexpected, and FINDINGS+=() appends a severity-prefixed string; the main() function calls all check functions in sequence, then calls generate_report(), then sets the exit code based on the highest severity finding: any CRITICAL or HIGH in FINDINGS exits $EXIT_CRITICAL (2), WARN-only exits $EXIT_WARN (1), empty FINDINGS exits $EXIT_OK (0); this exit code contract allows any external monitoring system that invokes the script — Nagios, Zabbix, or a systemd OnFailure= unit — to receive machine-readable audit status without parsing text
2. Implement safe filesystem and permission check functions: `function check_world_writable_etc() { find /etc -perm -o+w -type f 2>/dev/null | while read -r f; do FINDINGS+=("HIGH: world-writable /etc file: $f"); done; }` — the `while read -r f` construct processes filenames one at a time from find's output, safely handling names that contain spaces or special characters; the `for f in $(find ...)` pattern is unsafe for production because it breaks on any filename containing spaces, IFS characters, or glob metacharacters; `function check_suid_against_baseline() { local unexpected; unexpected=$(diff <(find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | sort) /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt 2>/dev/null | grep "^<" | sed 's/^< //'); [[ -n "$unexpected" ]] && while IFS= read -r bin; do FINDINGS+=("HIGH: unexpected SUID binary: $bin"); done <<< "$unexpected"; }` — process substitution `<(command)` passes command output as a file descriptor to diff eliminating a temporary file; `grep "^<"` isolates lines present in the current scan but absent from the baseline; `sed 's/^< //'` strips the diff prefix; the `<<< "$unexpected"` here-string feeds multiline output into while-read line by line
3. Build the report generator and output pipeline: `function generate_report()` uses printf throughout — printf's escape handling is consistent across POSIX systems and it does not interpret backslashes in data strings the way echo -e does; `${#FINDINGS[@]}` expands to the total count of findings — print it in the report header so an analyst scanning a log immediately knows the scale without reading every entry; the complete output pipeline `security-audit.sh 2>&1 | tee /var/log/security-audit-$(date -u +%Y%m%d).log` routes both stdout and stderr through tee which writes to the dated log file while simultaneously displaying on screen; `2>&1` must appear before the pipe so stderr is merged into stdout before tee receives the combined stream; the datestamped log filename means daily runs create separate archive files rather than overwriting a single log; after deployment, filter for critical items with `grep "^CRITICAL\|^HIGH" /var/log/security-audit-$(date -u +%Y%m%d).log`

**Skills Gained:** three-step check function pattern (detect / test / append to FINDINGS), severity-prefixed findings (CRITICAL/HIGH/WARN/LOW), ${#FINDINGS[@]} findings count, main() as orchestrator, exit code contract (EXIT_OK/EXIT_WARN/EXIT_CRITICAL), while read -r safe filename loop, for-in-find unsafe pattern (never use), diff <(command) process substitution for baseline comparison, grep "^<" to isolate new diff lines, sed 's/^< //' prefix strip, <<< here-string for while-read, printf over echo, 2>&1 before pipe, tee datestamped log, security-audit.sh deployment (/usr/local/bin/ chmod +x), grep severity-prefix filter on log output
**AI Mentor:** Position the audit script as a living tool: "The script you build here is not a homework exercise — it is the beginning of a tool you will extend for every environment you defend. Each new check function encodes something you learned: a finding that surprised you in an incident, a control your organisation is required to enforce, a service specific to your stack that has known hardening requirements. Functions you write once run on every system you touch after that. The exit code contract means any monitoring platform can consume your script without modification. Write one check function per finding type, keep them independent, and the script becomes a compounding asset."

##### A3.C5.M2.L3 — Automated File Integrity Monitoring
- **Slug:** automated-file-integrity-monitoring | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** analyse | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L10 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M2.L1, A3.C5.M2.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/hosts /etc/fstab /etc/ld.so.preload > /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, cat /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED, echo "backdoor:x:0:0::/root:/bin/bash" >> /etc/passwd, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256; echo "Exit: $?", diff <(sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers) /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256 | grep "^[<>]", find /etc -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum > /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256, wc -l /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED, find /usr/bin /usr/sbin /bin /sbin -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum > /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED | wc -l, dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$", dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | awk '$1~/5/{print "MD5 mismatch:", $NF}', cat /usr/local/bin/fim-check.sh, chmod +x /usr/local/bin/fim-check.sh, /usr/local/bin/fim-check.sh, /usr/local/bin/fim-check.sh; echo "Exit: $?"), ClickableDiagram (file integrity monitoring lifecycle: establish baseline on Day 0 → store on read-only or remote media → schedule nightly verification → detect FAILED output and non-zero exit → alert and investigate → update baseline only after approved change — click each phase for the commands, storage requirements, and security controls at that phase), AttackTimeline (tampered-file investigation: baseline created at OS deployment → attacker gains access and appends a UID-0 backdoor account to /etc/passwd → scheduled FIM check runs → FAILED line generated with /etc/passwd filename → analyst diffs current vs. baseline → backdoor account confirmed → ATT&CK T1136 Create Account mapped), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** sha256sum-output-format-hash-spaces-mode-filename, sha256sum-c-ok-vs-failed-exit-code, dpkg-verify-flag-column-position-meanings, fim-baseline-remote-storage-tamper-protection-rationale, find-sort-xargs-sha256-directory-manifest-generation
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Security+ Domain 3, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 2, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, PCI DSS Requirement 11.5 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Security Engineer, Forensics Analyst | **Accessibility:** SHA-256 output format decoded in a three-column table (hash / mode-indicator / filename) with plain-language descriptions; FAILED vs. OK output contrasted in an accessible side-by-side block; dpkg --verify flag positions in a two-column table (character position / meaning); FIM lifecycle diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per phase and urgency rating; AttackTimeline widget keyboard-navigable with Tab for event navigation; all commands in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Generate and verify SHA-256 baselines for critical system files: `sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/hosts /etc/fstab /etc/ld.so.preload > /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256` generates the baseline — each line contains the SHA-256 hash, two spaces, and the absolute filename; `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256` reads the baseline and recomputes the hash for each listed file, printing `filename: OK` for unchanged files and `filename: FAILED` for modified ones; sha256sum -c exits non-zero if any file fails — this exit code property makes the check scriptable without parsing output; `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED` isolates only the failing filenames for triage; the baseline critical-files list must cover at minimum: /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow, /etc/sudoers, /etc/ssh/sshd_config, /etc/hosts, /etc/fstab, and /etc/ld.so.preload; the baseline file itself must be stored on read-only media or a remote server — an attacker with write access can update the baseline to match their modified files, negating the check entirely; an append-only SIEM, an S3 bucket with object lock, or a write-once evidence server provides tamper-evident baseline storage
2. Extend FIM coverage to directories and system binaries: `find /etc -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum > /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256` generates a manifest of all files under /etc — `sort` is mandatory because find's directory traversal order is not guaranteed between runs, and an inconsistent file order produces false FAILED entries on the next verification; `xargs sha256sum` passes the sorted list to sha256sum in batches that respect the system's argument length limit; `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED` extracts failing files from the full manifest; building a binaries manifest with `find /usr/bin /usr/sbin /bin /sbin -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum > /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256` provides integrity coverage for core system utilities — a rootkit that replaces ps, ss, or ls with modified versions that hide attacker activity will generate FAILED entries in this check; the fim-check.sh script wraps all three checks: run sha256sum -c on critical-files and etc-manifest, capture FAILED lines into the FINDINGS array, set EXIT_CRITICAL if any were found, then generate a report — the script's exit code is the machine-readable signal consumed by scheduling and monitoring systems
3. Use package manager integrity verification as a supplementary check: `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null` (Debian/Ubuntu) re-reads the MD5 checksums recorded when each package was installed and compares them to the current files on disk — the output line flag field uses character positions to indicate mismatch type: position 3 (5) means MD5 or SHA-256 content mismatch (the file was modified), S means file size mismatch, M means file mode or permissions changed, U means file owner changed, G means file group changed, T means modification timestamp changed; a fully clean system produces no output from dpkg --verify; `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$"` filters to significant deviations (lines containing only a dot represent expected configuration file changes tracked by dpkg as conffiles and can be ignored); `rpm -Va 2>/dev/null | grep "^..5"` is the RPM-based equivalent — the third character in the verification flags output is the digest mismatch indicator; both package manager checks complement the custom SHA-256 baseline by covering all installed packages, catching modified libraries and helper binaries not explicitly listed in the critical-files baseline

**Skills Gained:** sha256sum file-list to baseline file, sha256sum -c (verification), sha256sum -c exit code (scriptable tamper detection), sha256sum -c | grep FAILED (failing-file extraction), critical-files baseline list (/etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/hosts /etc/fstab /etc/ld.so.preload), baseline remote/read-only storage requirement, find -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum (directory manifest), sort for deterministic find ordering, sha256sum -c manifest | grep FAILED, binaries manifest (/usr/bin /usr/sbin /bin /sbin), fim-check.sh structure (three-check wrapper + FINDINGS + exit code), dpkg --verify modification flag positions (5=MD5/S=size/M=mode/U=owner/G=group/T=mtime), dpkg --verify | grep -v "^.$" conffile filter, rpm -Va | grep "^..5" RPM equivalent, package-manager verification vs. custom baseline (complementary coverage)
**AI Mentor:** Ground FIM in real compromise context: "File integrity monitoring exists because attackers modify files after gaining access — they add a backdoor account to /etc/passwd, replace ss with a version that hides their listening socket, or write to /etc/ld.so.preload to inject a library into every process at startup. A SHA-256 baseline built before the attack and verified on a schedule is the difference between 'we know exactly which file changed and when' and 'we cannot confirm what the attacker modified.' Run `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256` on any system you are investigating. If anything returns FAILED — and you did not authorise that change — that is your starting point."

##### A3.C5.M2.L4 — Scheduling Security Jobs with cron and systemd Timers
- **Slug:** scheduling-security-jobs-cron-systemd | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 45 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L10 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M2.L1, A3.C5.M2.L2, A3.C5.M2.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (crontab -l, crontab -l -u root, cat /etc/crontab, ls -la /etc/cron.d/, cat /etc/cron.d/security-automation, 0 2 * * * root /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh >> /var/log/security-audit.log 2>&1, 30 3 * * * root /usr/local/bin/fim-check.sh >> /var/log/fim-check.log 2>&1, */5 * * * * root /usr/local/bin/brute-force-watcher.sh >> /var/log/brute-force.log 2>&1, run-parts --test /etc/cron.daily, ls -la /etc/cron.d/security-automation, find /etc/cron.d -not -user root 2>/dev/null, find /etc/cron.d -perm /022 2>/dev/null, cat /etc/systemd/system/security-audit.service, cat /etc/systemd/system/security-audit.timer, systemctl daemon-reload, systemctl enable --now security-audit.timer, systemctl list-timers, systemctl list-timers --all, systemctl status security-audit.timer, systemctl status security-audit.service, journalctl -u security-audit.service -n 20, journalctl -u security-audit.service --since "today", systemctl cat security-audit.timer), ComparisonTable (cron vs. systemd timer — missed-run handling / logging and output capture / dependency support / activation model / audit trail / permission model / syntax format), ClickableDiagram (cron entry anatomy for /etc/cron.d/ files: minute field / hour field / day-of-month / month / day-of-week / user field / command with redirection — click each field for allowed values, special characters, and a security scheduling example), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** cron-five-fields-star-slash-range-list-examples, cron-d-sixth-user-field-vs-user-crontab, systemd-timer-oncalendar-persistent-true-activation, systemd-service-oneshot-journal-capture, missed-run-cron-silent-skip-vs-systemd-persistent-recovery
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, LPIC-1 Topic 107, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 3 | **Careers:** SOC Analyst, Security Engineer, DevSecOps Engineer, Linux Administrator | **Accessibility:** Cron field anatomy diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per field; cron time expression table with five example expressions and their plain-English equivalents side by side; systemd timer and service unit files explained line by line in prose before the animation; cron vs. systemd comparison table with labelled row and column headers for screen readers; journalctl output retrieval documented in a numbered sequence; all examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Schedule security scripts with cron using the correct file location and format: the five cron time fields are minute (0–59), hour (0–23), day-of-month (1–31), month (1–12), and day-of-week (0–7, where both 0 and 7 are Sunday); `0 2 * * *` means "at 02:00 AM every day", `*/5 * * * *` means "every five minutes", `30 3 * * 1` means "at 03:30 AM every Monday"; for system-wide security scripts that must run as a specific user, use /etc/cron.d/ files rather than the root personal crontab — /etc/cron.d/ files use a six-field format with the username before the command: `0 2 * * * root /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh >> /var/log/security-audit.log 2>&1`; the `>> logfile 2>&1` redirect is mandatory — without it, cron attempts to email output to root via sendmail, which is rarely configured on modern servers and causes output to be silently discarded; for scripts that should run daily, weekly, or monthly, drop executable scripts into /etc/cron.daily/, /etc/cron.weekly/, or /etc/cron.monthly/ and they will be executed by run-parts at times defined in /etc/crontab; verify what run-parts would execute without running it with `run-parts --test /etc/cron.daily`; set `MAILTO=security@example.com` at the top of a /etc/cron.d/ file to receive non-empty script output by email — useful for scripts that print only when they find something, making email a finding notification
2. Create systemd timer units as the modern replacement for cron on systemd-based systems: a systemd timer requires two unit files — a .service file defining what to run and a .timer file defining when; the service file: `[Unit] Description=Nightly Security Audit / [Service] Type=oneshot / ExecStart=/usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh / StandardOutput=journal / StandardError=journal` — Type=oneshot tells systemd to wait for the command to complete before marking the job done, and both StandardOutput and StandardError set to journal route all script output to the systemd journal automatically without any manual redirection; the timer file: `[Unit] Description=Nightly Security Audit Timer / [Timer] OnCalendar=*-*-* 02:00:00 / Persistent=true / [Install] WantedBy=timers.target` — OnCalendar uses systemd's calendar time expression syntax; `Persistent=true` is the key advantage over cron: when the scheduled time passes while the system is offline (during patching, a cloud instance stop/start, or a reboot window), the timer fires at the next startup rather than silently skipping the run; enable and start with `systemctl enable --now security-audit.timer`; view all scheduled timers with `systemctl list-timers` which shows each timer's next and last activation time; retrieve job output with `journalctl -u security-audit.service --since "today"` — all stdout and stderr is automatically captured, timestamped, and queryable without any redirection
3. Apply ownership and permission security to cron and timer files: cron files in /etc/cron.d/ must be owned by root and must not be group-writable or world-writable — if any non-root user can write to a cron file, they can inject commands that run as root, making world-writable cron entries a local privilege escalation vector; enforce permissions with `chmod 644 /etc/cron.d/security-automation` and verify with `ls -la /etc/cron.d/`; cron silently ignores /etc/cron.d/ files that have group-write or world-write bits set (a security feature — if cron executed world-writable files it would be trivially exploitable); audit for violations with `find /etc/cron.d -perm /022 -o -not -user root 2>/dev/null` — any result is a finding; systemd service and timer unit files in /etc/systemd/system/ should likewise be root-owned with 644 permissions; verify the active timer is firing as expected by checking `systemctl status security-audit.timer` for the last trigger time and `systemctl status security-audit.service` for the exit code of the most recent run

**Skills Gained:** cron five-field time syntax (minute/hour/dom/month/dow), common cron expressions (0 2 * * * daily / */5 every-5-min / 30 3 * * 1 weekly-Monday), /etc/cron.d/ six-field format with user field, >> log 2>&1 mandatory output redirect, MAILTO= for email delivery, /etc/cron.daily-weekly-monthly drop-in directories, run-parts --test dry-run, systemd .service unit (Type=oneshot / ExecStart / StandardOutput=journal / StandardError=journal), systemd .timer unit (OnCalendar / Persistent=true / WantedBy=timers.target), OnCalendar *-*-* HH:MM:SS syntax, Persistent=true missed-run recovery behaviour, systemctl enable --now timer, systemctl list-timers (next/last activation), journalctl -u service --since today (output retrieval), /etc/cron.d/ file permission requirement (root-owned 644), find /etc/cron.d -perm /022 permission audit, world-writable cron file as privilege escalation vector
**AI Mentor:** Make the Persistent=true argument concrete: "A cron job scheduled for 02:00 AM does nothing if the server was rebooted at 01:50 for a patch window. You find out two days later that your FIM checks have a gap. A systemd timer with `Persistent=true` fires at the next boot, closing the gap automatically. For any security monitoring job where a missed run creates a monitoring blind spot, Persistent=true is a correctness requirement, not a convenience. The journal output capture is equally important: cron output disappears unless you remember to redirect it; systemd timer output is captured, timestamped, and queryable with journalctl for as long as the journal retains data."

### Module A3.C5.M2 — Practical

**Title:** Build and Deploy a Security Automation Suite
**Type:** free-lab | **Duration:** 60 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** Helix Corp's security team has completed the initial incident investigation from Module 1. To prevent the same class of compromise from going undetected in the future, the learner must build and deploy three automation components: a modular security audit script, a file integrity monitor, and a scheduled execution framework. All components must pass quality gates before deployment.

**Tasks:**
1. **Write security-audit.sh:** Create /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh with `set -euo pipefail`, a FINDINGS indexed array, readonly exit code constants (EXIT_OK=0, EXIT_WARN=1, EXIT_CRITICAL=2), and a trap handler for INT and TERM. Implement five check functions: check_uid0_accounts, check_empty_passwords, check_world_writable_etc, check_suid_against_baseline (using diff <() against /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt), and check_failed_login_count. Add generate_report() printing host, UTC timestamp, kernel version, and all FINDINGS with their count. Set exit code based on highest severity. Run `shellcheck /usr/local/bin/security-audit.sh` and resolve all warnings before proceeding.
2. **Create the FIM baseline:** Run `sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/hosts /etc/fstab /etc/ld.so.preload > /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256`. Verify clean: `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256`. Generate the /etc directory manifest: `find /etc -type f | sort | xargs sha256sum > /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256`. Record the file count with `wc -l /opt/baseline/etc-manifest.sha256`.
3. **Write fim-check.sh:** Create /usr/local/bin/fim-check.sh with set -euo pipefail. The script must run `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED`, capture any FAILED entries, print them to stdout, and exit with code 2 if any are found. Test: append a line to /etc/hosts, run fim-check.sh, confirm the FAILED entry appears and exit code is 2. Restore /etc/hosts and confirm exit code returns to 0.
4. **Schedule with cron:** Create /etc/cron.d/security-automation with two entries: security-audit.sh at 02:00 daily and fim-check.sh at 03:30 daily, both with correct user field and `>> /var/log/ 2>&1` redirects. Verify permissions: `ls -la /etc/cron.d/security-automation` must show root ownership and 644 mode.
5. **Deploy a systemd timer:** Write /etc/systemd/system/security-audit.service (Type=oneshot, ExecStart pointing to security-audit.sh, StandardOutput=journal, StandardError=journal) and /etc/systemd/system/security-audit.timer (OnCalendar=*-*-* 02:00:00, Persistent=true, WantedBy=timers.target). Run `systemctl daemon-reload` then `systemctl enable --now security-audit.timer`. Verify with `systemctl list-timers | grep security-audit`. Check output with `journalctl -u security-audit.service -n 20` if the timer has already fired.

**Assessment Criteria:** security-audit.sh passes shellcheck with zero warnings and produces a correctly formatted report with severity-coded exit code (30%), FIM baseline created for all seven files and fim-check.sh correctly detects the /etc/hosts tampering with exit code 2 and clears on restore (25%), systemd timer enabled with Persistent=true and verified with list-timers (25%), cron entries correctly formatted in /etc/cron.d/ with root:644 permissions confirmed (20%)

### Module A3.C5.M3 — Linux in Enterprise Security

**Module Objectives:**
1. Apply advanced SSH hardening for enterprise environments: restrict access with AllowUsers and Match blocks, eliminate unnecessary features, configure ForceCommand for privileged access management, and verify the hardened configuration with sshd -T and ssh-audit
2. Configure PAM authentication policy: enforce password quality with pam_pwquality, implement account lockout with pam_faillock, and restrict login access with pam_access
3. Establish a configuration integrity baseline with AIDE, detect configuration drift against the database, and apply change management discipline before updating the baseline
4. Apply CIS Benchmark Level 1 hardening controls to a Linux server, score the implementation with lynis, and produce a gap analysis with prioritised remediation commands for each failing control

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L13 (enterprise SSH hardening, PAM authentication policy, and configuration management — primary node for this module), OS-L11 (MAC enforcement — SELinux/AppArmor as a component of the enterprise security baseline), OS-L15 (Auditd — auditing SSH authentication, PAM events, and configuration change events in the enterprise audit trail)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner applies a complete enterprise SSH hardening configuration and verifies each control is active using sshd -T output; configures pam_faillock with deny=3 and unlock_time=600 and confirms a test lockout with faillock --user; initialises an AIDE database, introduces a deliberate configuration change, runs aide --check and identifies the modified file in the report; runs lynis audit system and produces a scored gap analysis with five controls, their current state, and the specific remediation command for each
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (cat /etc/ssh/sshd_config, cp /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/ssh/sshd_config.bak, sshd -t, sshd -T | grep -iE "permitrootlogin|passwordauthentication|maxauthtries|x11forwarding|allowagentforwarding|permittunnel|logLevel", systemctl reload sshd, ssh -o BatchMode=yes -o ConnectTimeout=5 localhost echo ok 2>&1, cat /etc/pam.d/common-auth, cat /etc/pam.d/common-password, cat /etc/security/pwquality.conf, faillock --user alice, faillock --user alice --reset, cat /etc/security/faillock.conf, su -l badpass 2>&1, cat /etc/security/access.conf, cat /etc/pam.d/login, sudo aide --init, ls -la /var/lib/aide/, sudo aide --check, sudo aide --check 2>&1 | grep -iE "changed|added|removed", sudo aide --check 2>&1 | tail -20, sudo lynis audit system, sudo lynis audit system --quick 2>&1 | tail -40, grep "Suggestion" /var/log/lynis.log | head -20, grep "Warning" /var/log/lynis.log | head -10, lynis show controls | grep "SSH" | head -10, cat /var/log/lynis-report.dat | grep "hardening_index", dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$", debsums -c 2>/dev/null | head -10, sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward, sysctl kernel.randomize_va_space, cat /etc/sysctl.d/99-hardening.conf), ClickableDiagram (enterprise Linux security layers: SSH access control → PAM authentication and lockout → configuration management baseline → MAC enforcement → audit and compliance assessment — click each layer for the controls, tools, and commands at that layer), ComparisonTable (SSH control methods — AllowUsers / AllowGroups / Match block / ForceCommand — with use case, scope, and configuration example for each), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** sshd-t-effective-config-vs-default-compiled-value, pam-stack-required-sufficient-optional-module-control, pam-faillock-counter-increment-reset-lockout-flow, aide-init-database-vs-check-comparison-workflow, lynis-hardening-index-score-components-and-weighting, cis-benchmark-level-1-vs-level-2-applicability-criteria
**Simulations:** SSH hardening tester (learner modifies sshd_config settings one at a time — PermitRootLogin yes → warning flag; PermitRootLogin no → pass; each saved change updates a running hardening score and shows the sshd -T effective value); PAM lockout simulator (learner writes faillock.conf parameters, fails three consecutive logins, sees the account enter locked state via faillock --user output, then resets with faillock --user --reset and watches the counter clear to zero)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L3; quiz-and-challenge for L2 and L4

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Run `sshd -T | grep -iE "permitrootlogin|passwordauthentication|maxauthtries|x11forwarding"` and explain the current effective value of each setting and whether it represents a security risk on a production server
- **Intermediate:** Write the /etc/security/pwquality.conf settings that enforce: minimum 14 characters, at least 1 uppercase, 1 lowercase, 1 digit, and 1 special character; then write the /etc/security/faillock.conf settings that implement deny=3, unlock_time=600, fail_interval=900, and even_deny_root; explain why even_deny_root is controversial but necessary for compliance frameworks
- **Advanced:** Run `sudo lynis audit system --quick` and identify three controls from the Suggestions section of /var/log/lynis.log; for each control, write the specific remediation command and state whether it is a CIS Benchmark Level 1 or Level 2 control and why
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level
