##### A3.C5.M3.L1 — SSH Hardening for Enterprise Environments
- **Slug:** ssh-hardening-enterprise | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L13 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M2.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (cat /etc/ssh/sshd_config, cp /etc/ssh/sshd_config /etc/ssh/sshd_config.bak, sshd -t, sshd -T, sshd -T | grep -iE "permitrootlogin|passwordauthentication|maxauthtries|logingracetime|x11forwarding|allowagentforwarding|permittunnel|maxsessions|loglevel", systemctl reload sshd, systemctl status sshd, echo "Port 22" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "PermitRootLogin no" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "PasswordAuthentication no" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "MaxAuthTries 3" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "LoginGraceTime 30" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "X11Forwarding no" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "AllowAgentForwarding no" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "PermitTunnel no" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "ClientAliveInterval 300" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "ClientAliveCountMax 2" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "MaxSessions 4" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "LogLevel VERBOSE" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "Banner /etc/ssh/banner" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, echo "AllowUsers alice bob" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, cat /etc/ssh/banner, sshd -t && systemctl reload sshd, sshd -T | grep -i allowusers, grep "Accepted\|Failed\|Invalid" /var/log/auth.log | tail -10), ClickableDiagram (sshd_config hardening controls: authentication controls (PermitRootLogin/PasswordAuthentication/MaxAuthTries) / session controls (LoginGraceTime/ClientAliveInterval/MaxSessions) / feature reduction (X11Forwarding/AllowAgentForwarding/PermitTunnel) / access controls (AllowUsers/Banner/LogLevel) — click each group for the directive, its hardened value, and the attack surface it eliminates), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** sshd-t-effective-value-vs-compiled-default-contrast, permit-root-login-no-vs-prohibit-password-vs-yes, client-alive-interval-count-max-session-timeout-calculation, allow-users-allowlist-effect-on-other-accounts, loglevel-verbose-fingerprint-logging-in-auth-log
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** articulation
- **Certs:** CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domain 3, CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 2, CIS Benchmark Ubuntu 22.04 Section 5 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, DevSecOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** sshd_config control groups diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per group; each directive explained in a three-column table (directive / hardened value / attack surface eliminated) with plain-language descriptions; sshd -t validation step highlighted before every reload; ClientAliveInterval timeout calculation shown as arithmetic in text; AllowUsers effect on unlisted accounts explained explicitly; all configuration examples in accessible code blocks

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Apply the core authentication and access restriction directives: `PermitRootLogin no` eliminates direct root SSH access — an attacker who obtains valid root credentials cannot log in directly and must first compromise a regular user account, adding a detection step; `PasswordAuthentication no` disables all password-based SSH authentication and requires key-based authentication — this eliminates the entire class of brute-force and credential-stuffing attacks against SSH; `MaxAuthTries 3` limits the number of authentication attempts per connection to three before the connection is dropped — without this limit, a single connection can attempt hundreds of key pairs or password guesses before disconnecting; `LoginGraceTime 30` gives unauthenticated connections 30 seconds to complete authentication before being terminated — the default 120 seconds allows attackers to hold connections open during slow brute-force attempts; `AllowUsers alice bob svc-deploy` restricts SSH access to an explicit allowlist — any user account not on this list cannot log in via SSH regardless of valid credentials or a valid key, making lateral movement between accounts much harder; always validate sshd_config syntax with `sshd -t` before reloading — a syntax error in sshd_config causes the sshd reload to fail, but the existing running sshd process continues with the old configuration, creating a confusing state where changes appear applied but are not; verify the effective configuration with `sshd -T` which shows the compiled defaults merged with all active configuration files
2. Reduce the SSH feature attack surface: `X11Forwarding no` disables X11 display forwarding — enabling X11 forwarding opens a reverse channel from the server to the client that can be used to inject X events or redirect displays; `AllowAgentForwarding no` disables SSH agent forwarding — agent forwarding allows the remote server to use your local SSH agent to authenticate onward connections, which means a compromised server can use your agent to authenticate to other servers you have access to (T1021.004 — Remote Services: SSH); `PermitTunnel no` disables SSH tunnel (VPN over SSH) capability — attackers use SSH tunnels to exfiltrate data or create covert channels through SSH; `MaxSessions 4` limits the number of simultaneous sessions per connection — the default (10) allows a single authenticated connection to open many multiplexed sessions; `ClientAliveInterval 300` and `ClientAliveCountMax 2` together create an idle timeout: the server sends a keepalive after 300 seconds of inactivity and terminates the connection if 2 consecutive keepalives receive no response (300 × 2 = 600 seconds maximum idle before termination) — this prevents abandoned sessions from remaining open indefinitely
3. Configure logging, banners, and VERBOSE-level fingerprint recording: `LogLevel VERBOSE` causes sshd to log the fingerprint (SHA-256 hash) of every SSH key used for authentication in /var/log/auth.log — at the default INFO level, accepted key authentications log the username and source IP but not the key fingerprint; at VERBOSE level, the log entry includes the fingerprint, which allows investigators to determine exactly which key was used during an authentication event, enabling detection of unauthorised keys; `Banner /etc/ssh/banner` displays a legal warning banner to all connecting users before authentication — the banner text should include a statement that the system is for authorised users only and that all activity is monitored; banners serve both a legal notice function (establishing that unauthorised access is prohibited) and a deterrent function; create the banner file with appropriate jurisdiction-specific legal language and reload sshd; verify the banner is served with `ssh -o BatchMode=yes hostname 2>&1 | head -5`; for enterprise environments with many servers, manage sshd_config through a configuration management tool (Ansible, Puppet, Chef) so that all servers have identical hardened configurations and drift is detected automatically

**Skills Gained:** PermitRootLogin no (eliminates direct root access), PasswordAuthentication no (key-only auth, eliminates brute-force class), MaxAuthTries 3 (per-connection attempt limit), LoginGraceTime 30 (unauthenticated connection timeout), AllowUsers explicit allowlist (blocks all unlisted accounts from SSH), sshd -t (syntax validation before reload), sshd -T (effective compiled configuration), systemctl reload sshd (reload without dropping connections), X11Forwarding no (eliminates X11 reverse channel), AllowAgentForwarding no (eliminates agent hijack risk — T1021.004), PermitTunnel no (eliminates covert channel), MaxSessions 4 (multiplexed session limit), ClientAliveInterval/ClientAliveCountMax idle timeout calculation (interval × count = max idle seconds), LogLevel VERBOSE (key fingerprint logging in auth.log), Banner /etc/ssh/banner (legal notice before auth), sshd_config change management (validate → reload → verify with sshd -T)
**AI Mentor:** Lead with the AllowUsers argument: "The single most impactful SSH hardening directive is AllowUsers. PermitRootLogin no stops direct root login but leaves every other account on the system as a potential SSH entry point. AllowUsers creates an explicit allowlist — if an attacker creates a backdoor account or compromises a service account, they still cannot use it for SSH unless it appears on the list. On a server with 30 local accounts, AllowUsers might list three. That eliminates 27 accounts as SSH attack vectors with one line. Verify it took effect with `sshd -T | grep -i allowusers` after every reload."

##### A3.C5.M3.L2 — PAM Authentication Policy and Account Lockout
- **Slug:** pam-authentication-policy-account-lockout | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** apply | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L13 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M3.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (cat /etc/pam.d/common-auth, cat /etc/pam.d/common-password, cat /etc/pam.d/common-account, cat /etc/pam.d/sshd, cat /etc/pam.d/login, cat /etc/pam.d/sudo, cat /etc/security/pwquality.conf, cat /etc/security/faillock.conf, cat /etc/security/access.conf, cat /etc/security/limits.conf, faillock --user alice, faillock --user alice --reset, passwd alice, chage -l alice, chage -M 90 -W 14 -I 7 alice, chage -l alice, su -l testuser 2>&1, sudo pam-auth-update, grep "pam_faillock" /etc/pam.d/common-auth, grep "pam_pwquality" /etc/pam.d/common-password, grep "pam_access" /etc/pam.d/sshd, grep "pam_limits" /etc/pam.d/common-session, cat /etc/login.defs, grep "PASS_MAX_DAYS\|PASS_MIN_DAYS\|PASS_WARN_AGE" /etc/login.defs), ClickableDiagram (PAM stack execution flow for authentication: pam_faillock preauth (check lockout) → pam_unix (verify credentials) → pam_faillock authfail (record failure) / pam_faillock authsucc (reset counter on success) — click each module for the control flag, what it checks, and what happens when it fails or succeeds), ComparisonTable (PAM module control flags — required / requisite / sufficient / optional — with what happens on success, what happens on failure, and when to use each), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** pam-stack-module-control-flags-execution-flow, pam-faillock-preauth-authfail-authsucc-counter, pam-pwquality-password-check-sequence, chage-password-aging-fields-explained, pam-access-positive-negative-rule-evaluation
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Linux+ Domain 4, LPIC-1 Topic 110, CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domain 3, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 2, CIS Benchmark Ubuntu 22.04 Section 5 | **Careers:** Linux Administrator, Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, Identity and Access Management Engineer | **Accessibility:** PAM stack execution flow diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per module and control flag outcome; module control flag comparison table with labelled columns (flag / on-success / on-failure / typical use case); faillock --user output fields explained in a named-field table; chage field meanings in a two-column table (flag / meaning) with password aging timeline illustration described in text; all PAM file examples in accessible code blocks with module purpose explained in surrounding prose

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Understand the PAM stack execution model and configure pam_faillock for account lockout: PAM (Pluggable Authentication Modules) processes authentication through an ordered stack of modules for each service — each module in the stack is listed in /etc/pam.d/servicename and has a module control flag that determines what happens if the module succeeds or fails; control flags are: `required` (failure is remembered but the stack continues — all modules run before returning failure), `requisite` (failure immediately halts the stack and returns failure), `sufficient` (success immediately halts the stack and returns success if no earlier required module failed), and `optional` (success or failure has no effect unless it is the only module in the stack); pam_faillock implements account lockout by maintaining a per-user failure counter in /var/run/faillock/ — the configuration in /etc/security/faillock.conf sets `deny=3` (lock after 3 failures), `unlock_time=600` (automatic unlock after 600 seconds), `fail_interval=900` (failures older than 900 seconds do not count toward the lockout threshold), and `even_deny_root` (root is also subject to lockout — required by CIS Benchmark but creates a risk of locking out the only privileged account; mitigate by ensuring console access is available); pam_faillock requires two entries in /etc/pam.d/common-auth: a `preauth` entry before pam_unix (checks whether the account is already locked before attempting authentication) and an `authfail` or `authsucc` entry after pam_unix (records failure or resets the counter on success); verify with `faillock --user username` and reset with `faillock --user username --reset`
2. Enforce password quality policy with pam_pwquality and password aging with chage: pam_pwquality enforces password complexity requirements when users change their passwords — configuration in /etc/security/pwquality.conf sets: `minlen=14` (minimum 14 characters), `dcredit=-1` (at least 1 digit — negative values specify minimums), `ucredit=-1` (at least 1 uppercase letter), `lcredit=-1` (at least 1 lowercase letter), `ocredit=-1` (at least 1 special character), `maxrepeat=3` (no character repeated more than 3 times in sequence), and `gecoscheck=1` (password cannot contain username or GECOS field information); pam_pwquality is invoked by the `password` stack in /etc/pam.d/common-password as a `requisite` module so that a password failing quality checks immediately returns failure without proceeding to hash storage; password aging adds a time-based enforcement layer independent of PAM: `chage -M 90` sets the maximum number of days before a password must be changed, `-W 14` sets the warning period (user warned 14 days before expiry), and `-I 7` sets the inactivity period (account locked if not logged in within 7 days of password expiry); `chage -l username` shows all aging parameters for a user; defaults for new accounts are set in /etc/login.defs via PASS_MAX_DAYS, PASS_MIN_DAYS, and PASS_WARN_AGE
3. Restrict login access with pam_access and apply resource limits with pam_limits: pam_access implements host-based and time-based access control using /etc/security/access.conf — each rule has three colon-separated fields: permission (+ for allow, - for deny), user or group, and origin (hostname, IP, LOCAL for console, or ALL); rules are evaluated top-to-bottom and the first match wins; `-:ALL EXCEPT admin wheel:ALL` denies all users except members of the admin and wheel groups from all origins — a simple deny-all except admins policy; `+:svc-deploy:192.168.1.0/24` allows the svc-deploy account only from the admin network; pam_access is added to /etc/pam.d/sshd and /etc/pam.d/login as an `account` module so it is evaluated after authentication succeeds but before the session is opened; pam_limits enforces per-user resource limits defined in /etc/security/limits.conf — security-relevant limits include `nofile` (maximum open file descriptors — prevents file descriptor exhaustion), `nproc` (maximum number of processes — prevents fork bombs), and `maxlogins` (maximum simultaneous logins for a user); pam_limits is added to the `session` stack in /etc/pam.d/common-session

**Skills Gained:** PAM stack execution model (service → /etc/pam.d/service → module stack → per-module control flag), module control flags (required/requisite/sufficient/optional — success and failure behaviour for each), pam_faillock preauth/authfail/authsucc stack placement, /etc/security/faillock.conf parameters (deny/unlock_time/fail_interval/even_deny_root), faillock --user (view counter) and --reset (unlock), pam_pwquality and /etc/security/pwquality.conf (minlen/dcredit/ucredit/lcredit/ocredit/maxrepeat/gecoscheck), password requisite module placement in common-password, chage -M/-W/-I password aging, chage -l (view aging), /etc/login.defs PASS_MAX_DAYS/PASS_MIN_DAYS/PASS_WARN_AGE for new accounts, pam_access /etc/security/access.conf three-field rule syntax (permit/user/origin), +/- permission symbols, pam_access in account stack, pam_limits /etc/security/limits.conf (nofile/nproc/maxlogins), pam_limits in session stack
**AI Mentor:** Frame lockout policy as a layered control: "pam_faillock is not a substitute for key-based SSH authentication — it is a complementary control. With PasswordAuthentication no in sshd_config, password brute-force attacks against SSH are already impossible. pam_faillock matters for console logins, sudo authentication, and any service that uses PAM password authentication. The even_deny_root parameter is contentious because it risks locking out root at the console — mitigate this by ensuring you always have an alternative privileged access path (a separate root console session, a recovery boot, or an out-of-band management interface) before enabling it in production."

##### A3.C5.M3.L3 — Configuration Baseline and Drift Detection with AIDE
- **Slug:** configuration-baseline-drift-detection-aide | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** analyse | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L11, OS-L13 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M3.L1, A3.C5.M3.L2
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (apt install aide -y, cat /etc/aide/aide.conf, grep -v "^#" /etc/aide/aide.conf | grep -v "^$" | head -30, sudo aide --init, ls -la /var/lib/aide/, sudo mv /var/lib/aide/aide.db.new /var/lib/aide/aide.db, sudo aide --check, sudo aide --check 2>&1 | grep -E "changed|added|removed|AIDE", echo "config_drift=1" >> /etc/ssh/sshd_config, sudo aide --check 2>&1 | grep -E "changed|added|f:", sudo aide --check 2>&1 | tail -30, sudo aide --update, sudo mv /var/lib/aide/aide.db.new /var/lib/aide/aide.db, sudo aide --check 2>&1 | grep "AIDE found no differences", debsums -c 2>/dev/null | head -10, debsums -l 2>/dev/null | head -10, dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$", cat /etc/aide/aide.conf | grep "^/etc", cat /etc/aide/aide.conf | grep "^/usr/bin", grep "NORMAL\|PERMS\|SHA512" /etc/aide/aide.conf | head -10), ClickableDiagram (AIDE workflow: install and configure scope → init (aide --init generates aide.db.new) → promote database (mv aide.db.new aide.db) → schedule nightly check (aide --check compares current state to aide.db) → review report → for approved changes: aide --update to regenerate baseline → promote again — click each phase for the commands and the security controls at that phase), AttackTimeline (AIDE drift detection scenario: baseline built at deployment → authorised SSH config change applied but not documented → AIDE nightly check reports /etc/ssh/sshd_config changed → security team investigates → change was authorised but undocumented → change management process violation confirmed → baseline updated after approval), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** aide-init-hash-all-monitored-files-to-database, aide-check-current-vs-database-comparison, aide-report-changed-added-removed-interpretation, aide-update-new-database-promotion-workflow, debsums-c-changed-package-file-detection
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA Security+ Domain 3, CompTIA CySA+ Domain 2, PCI DSS Requirement 11.5, NIST SP 800-53 SI-7 | **Careers:** Security Engineer, SOC Analyst, Incident Responder, Compliance Analyst | **Accessibility:** AIDE workflow diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per phase with an urgency indicator for the investigation phase; aide --check report output fields explained in a named-field table (File/Changed attributes/Before/After); AttackTimeline widget keyboard-navigable with Tab for event navigation; debsums -c output format decoded in text before the command; all AIDE commands documented with expected output described in prose; aide.conf rule format explained field by field

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Initialise and use AIDE to establish a configuration baseline: AIDE (Advanced Intrusion Detection Environment) generates cryptographic checksums for every file it monitors and stores them in a binary database; configuration is in /etc/aide/aide.conf — each rule specifies a path and a set of attributes to monitor using named groups: NORMAL monitors file content (SHA-256/SHA-512), permissions (mode), owner, group, and size; PERMS monitors only permissions and ownership without hashing the file content; the default configuration monitors /etc, /usr/bin, /usr/sbin, /bin, /sbin, and /lib with appropriate attribute sets; after installation and configuration, run `sudo aide --init` to generate the initial database (written to /var/lib/aide/aide.db.new rather than aide.db to allow review before promotion); promote the database to active status with `sudo mv /var/lib/aide/aide.db.new /var/lib/aide/aide.db`; run a check with `sudo aide --check` which compares the current state of all monitored files against the stored database — on a clean unchanged system the report ends with "AIDE found no differences"; keep the aide.db file on read-only or remote media where it cannot be modified by an attacker — an attacker who can write to aide.db can update the baseline to match their modifications, negating the check entirely
2. Interpret AIDE check output and manage the baseline update workflow: when a monitored file changes, aide --check reports it with the filename, the attribute that changed (content, permissions, mtime, owner, group, size), and the before and after values; a changed /etc/ssh/sshd_config entry after an authorised SSH hardening change is expected — but the same entry appearing on a system that should not have had any SSH changes is an investigation trigger; the key discipline is change management: every change to a monitored file should be documented in a change request before it is applied, so that when AIDE reports the change, the security team can cross-reference it against the approved change log rather than treating every AIDE finding as a potential incident; after a set of approved changes has been applied and verified, update the baseline with `sudo aide --update` (which generates a new aide.db.new from the current state of all monitored files) and then promote it with `sudo mv /var/lib/aide/aide.db.new /var/lib/aide/aide.db`; any change that appears in AIDE output without a corresponding approved change record is treated as an unauthorised modification — even if the change appears benign, the absence of documentation is itself a finding
3. Use debsums and dpkg --verify as complementary package integrity checks: `debsums -c` (debian-specific, requires the debsums package) checks the MD5 sums of all installed package files against the values stored in /var/lib/dpkg/info/*.md5sums — output lists files whose content does not match the package-recorded checksum; `debsums -l` lists packages that have no md5sums file (common for manually installed or older packages); `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null` performs the same check using dpkg's own verification mechanism and uses a flag field to indicate the type of mismatch (5 for content, S for size, M for permissions); debsums and dpkg --verify complement AIDE by covering all installed package files rather than only the paths explicitly listed in aide.conf, catching modified libraries, kernel modules, and helper binaries that the AIDE configuration might not cover; the three tools together — AIDE for monitored paths, debsums/dpkg --verify for package integrity, and the SHA-256 custom baseline from Module 2 for critical files — provide overlapping coverage that a sophisticated attacker would need to defeat simultaneously to avoid detection

**Skills Gained:** AIDE purpose (cryptographic baseline for file integrity monitoring), /etc/aide/aide.conf path and attribute rule syntax, NORMAL/PERMS named attribute groups, aide --init (generate aide.db.new), mv aide.db.new aide.db (promote baseline), aide --check (compare current state to database), aide --check FAILED/changed/added/removed report interpretation, aide --update (regenerate database from current state), aide.db remote/read-only storage requirement, change management discipline (document before apply), cross-reference AIDE output against change log, AIDE as unauthorised-change vs. approved-change classifier, debsums -c (package file content check), debsums -l (no md5sums packages), dpkg --verify flag field (5=content/S=size/M=mode), three-tool coverage (AIDE + debsums/dpkg --verify + SHA-256 custom baseline)
**AI Mentor:** Position AIDE within the change management process: "AIDE does not distinguish between authorised and unauthorised changes on its own — it only reports what changed. The discipline that makes AIDE useful is change management: every change to a monitored file must be documented in a change record before it is applied. When AIDE reports a change, the security team checks the change log. If there is a matching approved record, the change is expected and the baseline is updated. If there is no matching record, the change is unauthorised and the investigation begins. Without the change log, AIDE output is noise. With it, any finding that does not match a record is a signal."

##### A3.C5.M3.L4 — CIS Benchmark Application and lynis Audit
- **Slug:** cis-benchmark-lynis-audit | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** evaluate | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L11, OS-L13 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M3.L1, A3.C5.M3.L2, A3.C5.M3.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (apt install lynis -y, sudo lynis audit system, sudo lynis audit system --quick, cat /var/log/lynis.log | grep "Suggestion" | head -20, cat /var/log/lynis.log | grep "Warning" | head -10, cat /var/log/lynis-report.dat | grep "hardening_index", lynis show controls | head -20, lynis show controls | grep -i "ssh" | head -10, lynis show controls | grep -i "pam" | head -10, lynis show details AUTH-9264, lynis show details SSH-7408, sudo lynis audit system --tests-from-group ssh, sudo lynis audit system --tests-from-group authentication, sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward, sysctl net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects, sysctl net.ipv4.conf.all.send_redirects, sysctl kernel.randomize_va_space, sysctl kernel.dmesg_restrict, sysctl fs.suid_dumpable, cat /etc/sysctl.d/99-cis-hardening.conf, sysctl --system, grep "TMOUT" /etc/profile /etc/bashrc /etc/profile.d/*.sh 2>/dev/null, grep "umask" /etc/profile /etc/bashrc 2>/dev/null, cat /proc/mounts | grep -E "nosuid|nodev|noexec"), ClickableDiagram (CIS Benchmark control domains for Linux: filesystem configuration / software updates / special purpose services / network configuration / logging and auditing / system access and authentication / user accounts and environment / warning banners / system maintenance — click each domain for the representative Level 1 and Level 2 controls and the verification command for each), ComparisonTable (CIS Benchmark Level 1 vs. Level 2 — scope / risk tolerance / server availability impact / compliance frameworks that require each / example controls from each level), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** lynis-audit-scan-phase-progression, lynis-hardening-index-score-calculation, cis-benchmark-level-1-server-control-examples, sysctl-parameter-hardening-immediate-persistent-apply, mount-option-nosuid-nodev-noexec-effect
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA Security+ SY0-701 Domain 3, CompTIA CySA+ Domains 2 and 3, CIS Controls V8, NIST SP 800-53, PCI DSS Requirement 2 | **Careers:** Security Engineer, Compliance Analyst, SOC Analyst, Linux Administrator, DevSecOps Engineer | **Accessibility:** CIS Benchmark domain diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per domain and level; lynis output sections (OK/Warning/Suggestion/Found) explained in a four-row table with plain-language descriptions; hardening_index score explained as a percentage in prose; sysctl parameter table with parameter name, default value, hardened value, and security effect; all commands in accessible code blocks with expected output format described

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Run lynis and interpret its output for a gap analysis: lynis is an open-source security auditing tool that tests the system against a large set of hardening controls grouped by category (SSH, authentication, filesystem, networking, logging, etc.); `sudo lynis audit system` runs all checks and produces three output files: /var/log/lynis.log (full detail of every check), /var/log/lynis-report.dat (machine-readable key=value pairs for automated processing), and the terminal summary; the terminal summary shows tests grouped as: green [OK] (control passed), yellow [WARNING] (security concern requiring action), yellow [SUGGESTION] (improvement opportunity), and [FOUND]/[NOT FOUND] (informational); the hardening index (retrieved from /var/log/lynis-report.dat with `grep "hardening_index"`) is a score from 0 to 100 representing the percentage of applicable controls that passed — a freshly installed server typically scores 45–60 before hardening; `lynis show details CONTROL-ID` shows the specific test logic, what it checks, and a remediation reference for each control; `lynis audit system --tests-from-group ssh` runs only SSH-related checks — useful for verifying that SSH hardening from L1 satisfied all applicable lynis controls; the CIS Benchmark for Ubuntu Linux documents the specific configuration values that correspond to each lynis control, enabling mapping between lynis output and the formal compliance standard
2. Apply CIS Benchmark Level 1 kernel and network hardening controls: CIS Level 1 controls are recommendations that can be implemented with minimal service disruption and are applicable to virtually all server deployments; Level 2 controls are stricter but may affect functionality and are intended for high-security environments; core Level 1 kernel hardening parameters applied via sysctl: `net.ipv4.ip_forward=0` (disable IP forwarding — the server is not a router), `net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects=0` (do not accept ICMP redirects that could modify routing), `net.ipv4.conf.all.send_redirects=0` (do not send ICMP redirects), `net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_source_route=0` (do not accept source-routed packets), `kernel.randomize_va_space=2` (full ASLR — address space layout randomisation), `kernel.dmesg_restrict=1` (restrict kernel log to privileged users), `fs.suid_dumpable=0` (prevent SUID processes from creating core dumps that may contain sensitive data); persist these with a file in /etc/sysctl.d/99-cis-hardening.conf and apply with `sysctl --system`; Level 1 filesystem controls include mounting /tmp with noexec and nodev options (prevents code execution and device files in world-writable directories), setting default umask to 027 in /etc/profile and /etc/bashrc (new files default to permissions 640 rather than 644), and setting TMOUT=900 (auto-logout after 15 minutes of shell inactivity)
3. Produce a structured gap analysis and apply the remediation workflow: a gap analysis for CIS compliance documents five fields for each failing control: control ID (lynis test ID and CIS benchmark reference), current state (what lynis found), required state (what the benchmark specifies), remediation command (the exact command that brings the system into compliance), and priority (based on exploitability and exposure); process: run `sudo lynis audit system --quick`, extract Warnings first then Suggestions from /var/log/lynis.log, look up each finding with `lynis show details CONTROL-ID` to get the formal remediation guidance, apply the change, then run lynis again for that group with `--tests-from-group groupname` to verify the finding is resolved before documenting as complete; example gap entries: SSH-7408 (PermitRootLogin not disabled — remediation: set PermitRootLogin no in sshd_config, reload sshd), AUTH-9264 (no password hashing rounds configured — remediation: set SHA_CRYPT_MIN_ROUNDS 50000 in /etc/login.defs), KRNL-5830 (ASLR not fully enabled — remediation: sysctl -w kernel.randomize_va_space=2), FILE-6310 (/tmp not mounted separately or without noexec — remediation: add tmpfs /tmp line to /etc/fstab with nodev,nosuid,noexec options and remount), LOGG-2190 (syslog daemon not running — remediation: systemctl enable --now rsyslog); document each gap with before/after verification commands and the control ID so the gap analysis can be used as an audit artefact

**Skills Gained:** lynis audit system (full scan), lynis audit system --quick (abbreviated scan), /var/log/lynis.log (full detail log), /var/log/lynis-report.dat | grep hardening_index (score), lynis output categories (OK/WARNING/SUGGESTION), lynis show details CONTROL-ID (remediation guidance), lynis audit --tests-from-group groupname (targeted scan), CIS Benchmark Level 1 vs. Level 2 distinction, sysctl hardening parameters (net.ipv4.ip_forward=0 / accept_redirects=0 / send_redirects=0 / accept_source_route=0 / kernel.randomize_va_space=2 / kernel.dmesg_restrict=1 / fs.suid_dumpable=0), /etc/sysctl.d/99-cis-hardening.conf persistent parameter file, sysctl --system (apply all drop-in files), umask 027 in /etc/profile and /etc/bashrc, TMOUT=900 shell idle timeout, /tmp noexec/nodev/nosuid mount options, gap analysis five-field structure (control ID / current state / required state / remediation command / priority), verify-before-document workflow
**AI Mentor:** Frame the hardening index as a starting point, not a target: "lynis gives you a score because scores are motivating — but the hardening index is not a compliance standard. A score of 75 with SSH-7408 (PermitRootLogin) still failing is worse than a score of 65 with SSH-7408 passing, because not all controls have equal security value. Work through Warnings before Suggestions, and within each category prioritise controls that reduce remote attack surface (SSH, network parameters, service exposure) over controls that improve local security posture (umask, core dump settings). The gap analysis is the deliverable — the score is just a progress indicator."

### Module A3.C5.M3 — Practical

**Title:** Apply an Enterprise Security Baseline to a Linux Server
**Type:** free-lab | **Duration:** 60 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate

**Scenario:** A new Helix Corp application server has passed initial network review and has the security automation suite from Module 2 deployed. Before the server is cleared for production traffic, it must pass an enterprise security baseline review. The learner must harden SSH, configure PAM lockout and password policy, establish an AIDE baseline, and run a lynis audit to identify and remediate the top gaps.

**Tasks:**
1. **SSH hardening:** Back up /etc/ssh/sshd_config. Apply the following directives: PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no, MaxAuthTries 3, LoginGraceTime 30, X11Forwarding no, AllowAgentForwarding no, PermitTunnel no, ClientAliveInterval 300, ClientAliveCountMax 2, MaxSessions 4, LogLevel VERBOSE, AllowUsers alice svc-deploy. Create /etc/ssh/banner with a legal notice. Validate syntax with `sshd -t`, then reload. Verify each hardened setting is active using `sshd -T | grep -iE "permitrootlogin|passwordauthentication|maxauthtries|x11forwarding|loglevel|allowusers"`.
2. **PAM policy:** Write /etc/security/pwquality.conf enforcing minlen=14, dcredit=-1, ucredit=-1, lcredit=-1, ocredit=-1, and maxrepeat=3. Write /etc/security/faillock.conf with deny=3, unlock_time=600, fail_interval=900, and even_deny_root. Verify pam_faillock is present in /etc/pam.d/common-auth with `grep pam_faillock /etc/pam.d/common-auth`. Test: attempt three failed logins for the testuser account, then confirm `faillock --user testuser` shows the locked state. Reset with `faillock --user testuser --reset`.
3. **AIDE baseline:** Run `sudo aide --init` and wait for completion. Promote the database: `sudo mv /var/lib/aide/aide.db.new /var/lib/aide/aide.db`. Run `sudo aide --check` and confirm "AIDE found no differences." Introduce a change: append `LogLevel DEBUG` to /etc/ssh/sshd_config. Run `sudo aide --check` and identify the changed file in the output. Document what attributes changed. Run `sshd -t` (the change makes the config invalid — revert it), restore sshd_config to the hardened version, and run `sudo aide --update` followed by the db.new promotion.
4. **lynis gap analysis:** Run `sudo lynis audit system --quick`. Extract the top five Warnings from /var/log/lynis.log. For each Warning: record the lynis control ID, run `lynis show details CONTROL-ID`, write the exact remediation command, and apply it. After applying all five remediations, run lynis again and confirm each Warning is resolved. Record the initial and final hardening_index scores from /var/log/lynis-report.dat.
5. **Kernel hardening:** Write /etc/sysctl.d/99-cis-hardening.conf with at least five Level 1 parameters (net.ipv4.ip_forward=0, net.ipv4.conf.all.accept_redirects=0, net.ipv4.conf.all.send_redirects=0, kernel.randomize_va_space=2, fs.suid_dumpable=0). Apply with `sysctl --system`. Verify each parameter with `sysctl parametername`. Confirm lynis KRNL-5830 is now passing with `sudo lynis audit system --tests-from-group kernel`.

**Assessment Criteria:** All nine SSH directives active and verified with sshd -T (25%), pam_faillock lockout triggered and confirmed with faillock --user and successfully reset (20%), AIDE baseline established and /etc/ssh/sshd_config change detected in aide --check output (25%), lynis gap analysis documents five Warnings with control IDs, remediation commands, and before/after hardening_index scores (20%), five sysctl kernel parameters applied and verified (10%)

### Module A3.C5.M4 — Blue Team Operations on Linux

**Module Objectives:**
1. Conduct proactive threat hunting on a live Linux host using MITRE ATT&CK as a hypothesis framework: develop hunt hypotheses from TTPs, establish a behavioural baseline, and distinguish anomalies from expected activity using process, network, authentication, and log sources
2. Detect all six classes of Linux persistence mechanism: cron abuse, systemd persistence, SSH backdoors, login shell startup file injection, shared library hijacking via /etc/ld.so.preload, and PAM module backdoors
3. Respond to an active privilege escalation attempt: identify the escalation path, contain the attacker, preserve volatile evidence in the correct order, eradicate all attacker artefacts, and complete a structured recovery workflow
4. Produce a professional incident response report following the PICERL framework and implement post-incident continuous improvement measures that reduce mean time to detect for the same attack pattern

**KG Domain Coverage:** OS-L06 (blue team operations — privilege escalation detection, persistence hunting, and containment and eradication response — primary node for this module), OS-L15 (Auditd — real-time detection and continuous evidence trail during active incident response), SEC-17 (Kill Chain — structuring the hunt and response around the seven kill chain phases), SEC-18 (MITRE ATT&CK — hypothesis-driven hunting using technique IDs as structured search targets)
**Practical Outcomes:** Learner develops five ATT&CK-derived hunt hypotheses and identifies at least three true positives from a seeded compromised TerminalSimulator; documents all persistence mechanisms present — cron, systemd, and SSH layers simultaneously active; responds to a simulated active privilege escalation by executing the containment and eradication sequence in correct order without destroying evidence; produces a six-section PICERL incident response report with an accurate eight-entry timeline, complete IOC list with ATT&CK mappings, and at least three actionable post-incident recommendations
**Required Interactive Components:** TerminalSimulator (ps aux --forest, ps aux | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10, ss -tnp | grep -v "127.0.0.1", ss -tlnp, lsof -i -P -n | grep LISTEN, w, last | head -20, lastb | head -10, who, for u in $(awk -F: '$3>=1000{print $1}' /etc/passwd); do echo "=== $u cron ==="; sudo crontab -l -u "$u" 2>/dev/null; done, sudo cat /etc/cron.d/* 2>/dev/null, ls -la /etc/cron.daily/ /etc/cron.weekly/, systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/enabled-services.txt 2>/dev/null, find /home -name ".bash_profile" -o -name ".bashrc" 2>/dev/null | xargs grep -l "nc\|wget\|curl\|bash -i\|/dev/tcp" 2>/dev/null, find /etc/systemd/system -name "*.service" -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp -type f 2>/dev/null, cat /root/.ssh/authorized_keys 2>/dev/null, find /home -name "authorized_keys" 2>/dev/null | xargs cat 2>/dev/null, getcap -r / 2>/dev/null, find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | sort, cat /etc/ld.so.preload 2>/dev/null, ls -la /proc/*/exe 2>/dev/null | grep deleted, ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | grep -v "^----$" | head -40, ausearch -k identity -ts today -i | head -30, journalctl -u cron --since "2026-07-28 00:00:00" -n 30, journalctl -u sshd --since "today" -n 50 -r, grep "sudo\|su -\|COMMAND" /var/log/auth.log | tail -30, sudo getcap -r /usr/bin /usr/sbin /bin /sbin 2>/dev/null, kill -9 $(pgrep -f "bash -i" 2>/dev/null), usermod -L compromised-user, iptables -A OUTPUT -d 203.0.113.0/24 -j DROP, systemctl stop suspicious.service, systemctl disable suspicious.service, rm /etc/systemd/system/suspicious.service, crontab -r -u compromised-user, sed -i '/backdoor-key/d' /root/.ssh/authorized_keys), AttackTimeline (blue team response timeline: alert received → initial triage → persistence confirmed → privilege escalation identified → containment executed → eradication completed → recovery verified → report submitted — learner populates each event from investigation findings), ClickableDiagram (PICERL framework: Preparation → Identification → Containment → Eradication → Recovery → Lessons Learned — click each phase for the blue team actions, Linux commands, and deliverables at that phase), QuizWidget
**Animation Categories:** mitre-attack-tactic-to-technique-hunt-hypothesis, threat-hunt-baseline-vs-anomaly-process-comparison, linux-six-persistence-mechanisms-overview, privilege-escalation-suid-capability-ldpreload-detection, picerl-phase-actions-and-outputs, containment-action-ordering-evidence-preservation
**Simulations:** Threat hunt simulator (learner receives a baseline and current state snapshot of a compromised host; formulates five ATT&CK hypotheses; runs search commands for each; three return true positives that the learner must label with a technique ID and severity; two return benign baseline activity that the learner must mark as false positive with an explanation); containment drill (learner receives an active attacker session alert; must execute containment commands in the correct order within a constraint; out-of-order actions display a consequence message about evidence destruction or inadvertent service disruption)
**Assessment Strategy:** Quiz-only for L1 and L4; quiz-and-challenge for L2 and L3

**Practical Progression:**
- **Beginner:** Given ps aux output with one suspicious entry — a python3 process running from /tmp with root effective UID — identify the ATT&CK technique it most likely represents, write the command to read its command line from /proc, and explain what you would look for in /var/log/auth.log to understand when and how it started
- **Intermediate:** List all six Linux persistence mechanism categories and write the single most important detection command for each; explain why /etc/ld.so.preload persistence is more dangerous than cron persistence from a defender's perspective
- **Advanced:** You discover that www-data ran `/usr/bin/python3 -c 'import socket,subprocess,os;...'` at 03:14 UTC. Write the containment sequence in correct order — preserve volatile evidence, terminate the attacker session, block the C2 IP, lock the compromised account — and explain the specific evidence or operational risk created by performing each step out of order
- **Capstone:** Not applicable at module level

##### A3.C5.M4.L1 — Threat Hunting on Linux
- **Slug:** threat-hunting-linux | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** advanced | **Bloom's:** analyse | **Ethical Content:** true
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L06, OS-L15 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M3.L4
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (ps aux --forest, ps aux | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -20, ss -tnp | grep -v "127.0.0.1", lsof -i -P -n | grep LISTEN, w, last | head -20, who, journalctl -p err --since "24 hours ago" -n 50, grep "Failed password\|Accepted" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $1,$2,$3,$9,$11}' | tail -30, ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | head -40, find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt, getcap -r / 2>/dev/null, ls -la /proc/*/exe 2>/dev/null | grep deleted, cat /etc/ld.so.preload 2>/dev/null, find /tmp /var/tmp /dev/shm -executable -type f 2>/dev/null, diff <(ss -tlnp | awk 'NR>1{print $4}' | sort) /opt/baseline/listening-ports.txt 2>/dev/null, diff <(systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | awk 'NR>1{print $1}' | sort) /opt/baseline/enabled-services.txt 2>/dev/null, awk -F: '($3>=1000 && $7!="/usr/sbin/nologin" && $7!="/bin/false"){print $1,$7}' /etc/passwd, sudo ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | grep -v "root\|systemd\|cron" | head -20), ClickableDiagram (ATT&CK-hypothesis-driven hunt cycle: select tactic → identify likely techniques → formulate detection hypothesis → write search command → run against log/system sources → classify findings as TP/FP/TNP → document and escalate TPs — click each step for the Linux commands and data sources at that step), AttackTimeline (seeded compromise timeline: the learner sees five events in random order and must arrange them into a causal kill chain sequence, labelling each with its ATT&CK technique), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** mitre-attack-tactic-discovery-technique-T1057-process-discovery, threat-hunting-hypothesis-formulation-three-part-structure, baseline-vs-anomaly-process-list-comparison, ss-tnp-unexpected-outbound-connection-detection, hunt-true-positive-vs-false-positive-classification
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA CySA+ CS0-003 Domains 1 and 2, CompTIA Security+ Domain 4, EC-Council CEH, GCIA | **Careers:** Threat Hunter, SOC Analyst (Tier 2/3), Incident Responder, Blue Team Analyst | **Accessibility:** ATT&CK hunt cycle diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per step and a plain-language description of what each step produces; hypothesis structure explained in a numbered three-part template before any example; TP/FP/TNP classification defined in a three-row table with plain-language descriptions; seeded timeline events accessible as individual labelled cards; all commands in accessible code blocks with output format described in surrounding prose

**Ethical Content Note:** This lesson teaches attacker techniques from the detection perspective. Hunt hypotheses are derived from documented real-world TTPs. The context at every step is: "this is what attackers do — here is how to detect that they did it." No tools or techniques for conducting the attack are provided; only the detection-side analysis commands are taught.

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Develop ATT&CK-driven hunt hypotheses for a Linux target: threat hunting is proactive — a hunter does not wait for an alert but instead forms structured hypotheses about attacker behaviour and searches for evidence that those behaviours occurred or are occurring; a hunt hypothesis has three components: the assumed attacker objective (e.g., "attacker has established persistent access"), the ATT&CK technique expected to implement that objective (T1053.005 — Scheduled Task: Cron), and the observable artifact the technique produces (unexpected cron entry executing from /tmp); the MITRE ATT&CK for Linux matrix organises techniques under tactics: Initial Access, Execution, Persistence, Privilege Escalation, Defense Evasion, Credential Access, Discovery, Lateral Movement, Collection, Command and Control, Exfiltration, Impact; a structured hunt against a Linux host starts with Persistence and Privilege Escalation as the highest-yield tactics (most attacker actions eventually require one or both); for each candidate technique, identify the data source that would contain evidence: T1053.005 (Cron) → crontab files and /var/log/syslog cron entries; T1543.002 (Systemd Service) → systemctl list-unit-files and /etc/systemd/system/; T1574.006 (LD_PRELOAD) → /etc/ld.so.preload and process environment; T1548.001 (SUID abuse) → find -perm -4000; T1098 (Account Manipulation) → /etc/passwd and auditd identity key
2. Establish a behavioural baseline and classify deviations as true or false positives: threat hunting without a baseline produces noise, not signal — the baseline documents what is normal for this specific system and is the reference against which anomalies are measured; process baseline: `ps aux | awk '{print $11}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn` on a known-clean system shows which processes are expected and how many instances are normal; network baseline: `ss -tlnp` shows which ports should be listening; service baseline: `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled` shows which services are expected; account baseline: the set of interactive-shell accounts from /etc/passwd; for each hunt hypothesis, run the detection command and compare results against the baseline using diff — a listening port not in the baseline or a process running from /tmp with no corresponding installed package are high-confidence anomalies (true positive candidates); a SUID binary on the baseline is expected (false positive); a SUID binary not on the baseline requires investigation; document each finding as TP (true positive — anomaly confirmed as malicious), FP (false positive — anomaly explained by known benign activity), or TNP (true negative positive — expected baseline activity confirmed)
3. Correlate findings across log sources to build an investigative narrative: a single IOC is an observation; two correlated IOCs from independent sources are evidence; threat hunting produces value by connecting disparate signals into a causal chain — `ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | grep -v "^root$"` shows all privileged command executions by non-root today; compare the timestamps of hits against the `last` login records for the same user — if a non-root user ran a privileged command 3 minutes after logging in from an external IP, and that user does not normally log in from that IP, the correlation is a high-confidence finding; cross-source correlation pattern: find a timestamp in one source, extract the user or process, search for that entity in a second source around the same time window, repeat across auth.log, journalctl, auditd, and /proc; the hunt concludes with a written hunt report: hypotheses tested, data sources queried, findings classified, and any escalation actions taken; a hunt that finds nothing is not a failed hunt — absence of evidence for a specific hypothesis increases confidence that the system is clean for that TTP

**Skills Gained:** ATT&CK-driven hunt hypothesis structure (objective / technique ID / observable artifact), ATT&CK for Linux tactic overview (Persistence/Privilege Escalation as highest-yield hunt start), technique-to-data-source mapping (T1053.005→crontab / T1543.002→systemctl / T1574.006→ld.so.preload / T1548.001→find -perm -4000 / T1098→/etc/passwd), baseline generation (ps/ss/systemctl/passwd), diff <(command) baseline comparison, TP/FP/TNP classification framework, cross-source timestamp correlation (ausearch + last + auth.log + journalctl), hunt report structure (hypotheses tested / data sources / findings / escalations), absence of evidence as confidence increase for clean hypothesis
**AI Mentor:** Frame hunting as a discipline distinct from monitoring: "Monitoring waits for alerts. Hunting assumes breach and goes looking. The difference is posture: a SOC monitoring dashboards reacts to what the SIEM surfaces; a threat hunter picks an ATT&CK technique that is known to work against Linux, formulates a hypothesis about what evidence it would leave, and actively searches for that evidence whether or not any alert fired. The most dangerous attackers never trigger alerts — they use legitimate tools (bash, python, cron), move slowly, and stay within baseline noise. Hypothesis-driven hunting finds them by knowing what 'normal' looks like so precisely that any deviation is visible."

##### A3.C5.M4.L2 — Detecting Persistence Mechanisms
- **Slug:** detecting-persistence-mechanisms-linux | **Type:** discovery | **Duration:** 55 min | **Difficulty:** advanced | **Bloom's:** analyse | **Ethical Content:** true
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L06, OS-L13, OS-L15 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M4.L1
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (for u in root $(awk -F: '$3>=1000{print $1}' /etc/passwd); do echo "=== $u ==="; sudo crontab -l -u "$u" 2>/dev/null; done, sudo cat /etc/cron.d/* 2>/dev/null, ls -la /etc/cron.daily/ /etc/cron.weekly/ /etc/cron.hourly/, sudo cat /etc/crontab, journalctl -u cron --since "2026-07-27 00:00:00" --until "2026-07-28 00:00:00" -n 50, grep "CRON.*CMD" /var/log/syslog | tail -30, systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | sort, find /etc/systemd/system -name "*.service" -type f 2>/dev/null, find /etc/systemd/system -name "*.timer" -type f 2>/dev/null, find /home -maxdepth 3 -path "*/.config/systemd/user/*.service" 2>/dev/null, cat /root/.ssh/authorized_keys 2>/dev/null, find /home -name "authorized_keys" 2>/dev/null | while read -r f; do echo "=== $f ==="; cat "$f"; done, sshd -T | grep -iE "authorizedkeysfile|port|listenaddress|acceptenv", find /home -name ".bash_profile" -o -name ".bashrc" -o -name ".bash_logout" -o -name ".profile" 2>/dev/null | xargs grep -l "nc\|wget\|curl\|bash -i\|/dev/tcp\|python.*socket\|exec" 2>/dev/null, cat /etc/profile.d/*.sh 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^#" | grep -v "^$", cat /etc/environment, cat /etc/rc.local 2>/dev/null, ls -la /etc/init.d/ | grep -v "^total", sudo cat /etc/ld.so.preload 2>/dev/null, sudo cat /proc/1/environ | tr '\0' '\n' | grep LD_PRELOAD, ls -la /etc/pam.d/ | grep -v "^total", grep -r "pam_unix\|pam_ldap\|pam_exec" /etc/pam.d/ | grep -v "^#" | head -20, find /lib/security /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/security -name "*.so" 2>/dev/null | sort, find /lib/security -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp -type f 2>/dev/null, ausearch -k identity -ts today -i | head -30), ClickableDiagram (six Linux persistence layers: cron (5 locations) / systemd units and timers / SSH authorized_keys and sshd_config / shell startup files (6 locations) / shared library injection (ld.so.preload / ld.so.conf.d) / PAM module insertion — click each layer for the detection commands and ATT&CK technique ID mapping), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** cron-five-location-audit-all-user-root-system, systemd-user-level-service-home-config-path, ssh-authorized-keys-location-root-vs-user, bashrc-profile-bash_logout-persistence-execution-trigger, ld-so-preload-global-injection-all-processes, pam-exec-module-backdoor-insertion
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** metacognitive
- **Certs:** CompTIA CySA+ Domains 2 and 4, CompTIA Security+ Domain 4, EC-Council CEH, GREM | **Careers:** Threat Hunter, Incident Responder, SOC Analyst, Forensics Analyst | **Accessibility:** Six persistence layers diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per layer and an urgency rating (rootkit-level/high/medium); cron location list in a numbered table; systemd user-level path explained in text before the find command; authorized_keys location table with root path vs. user path; shell startup file execution order explained in a numbered sequence; all detection commands in accessible code blocks with output interpretation described in prose; ethical framing applied explicitly before all offensive technique descriptions

**Ethical Content Note:** This lesson teaches all six Linux persistence mechanisms from the defensive detection perspective. The context at every step is: "attackers use this technique — here is exactly how to detect and remove it." The detection commands shown are the analyst's tools; no installation or setup instructions for any persistence mechanism are provided. All ATT&CK technique references are for classification and detection, not implementation. Learners are reminded that applying these techniques to systems they do not own or have explicit written authorisation to test is a criminal offence.

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Detect cron and systemd persistence across all locations: cron has five audit locations — user crontabs (`crontab -l -u username` for each user including root), /etc/crontab (system crontab with user field), /etc/cron.d/ (package-managed and admin-managed entries), and the run-parts directories /etc/cron.daily/, /etc/cron.weekly/, /etc/cron.hourly/, /etc/cron.monthly/ (script files run on schedule); hunt for cron entries that: execute from /tmp, /var/tmp, or /dev/shm (world-writable execution locations), download content via wget or curl, run base64 or python -c or bash -i (one-liner execution), or use unusual timing patterns (e.g., `*/1 * * * *` every-minute execution is unusual for a production server); for systemd, `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | sort` combined with a diff against the service baseline identifies new services; `find /etc/systemd/system -name "*.service" -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp` finds service files created after the known-good timestamp; critically, attackers also install persistence at the user level — `find /home -maxdepth 3 -path "*/.config/systemd/user/*.service"` finds user-scope systemd units that execute without root privileges and persist across the user's logins without appearing in system-level `systemctl list-unit-files`; `journalctl -u cron --since yesterday` shows what cron actually executed, not just what is configured — a cron entry that ran a command producing errors is visible here even if the crontab entry has since been deleted (T1053.005)
2. Detect SSH backdoors and shell startup file injection: SSH backdoors take three forms — unauthorised keys in authorized_keys files, modifications to sshd_config, and rogue sshd processes; audit all authorized_keys files: `find / -name "authorized_keys" -type f 2>/dev/null | while read -r f; do echo "=== $f ==="; cat "$f"; done` — any key whose comment does not match a known administrator or service account is a finding; compare key fingerprints against the known-good key inventory; audit sshd_config for backdoor settings: AcceptEnv with PATH or LD_PRELOAD in the environment variable allowlist (allows environment injection), non-standard Port or ListenAddress directives, ForceCommand that is not in the approved list, or AuthorizedKeysFile pointing to an unexpected path; shell startup file injection (T1546.004) uses the execution of login shell startup files to run attacker code on every interactive login: for bash, execution order is /etc/profile → /etc/profile.d/*.sh → ~/.bash_profile (login shells) and ~/.bashrc (interactive non-login shells); `find /home -name ".bashrc" -o -name ".bash_profile" -o -name ".profile" 2>/dev/null | xargs grep -l "nc\|wget\|curl\|bash -i\|/dev/tcp"` finds startup files containing reverse shell patterns; /etc/profile.d/ scripts run for all users — a malicious script placed here affects every user who opens a shell; audit with `cat /etc/profile.d/*.sh | grep -v "^#" | grep -v "^$"`; /etc/rc.local runs at boot as root — check it exists and contains only expected content
3. Detect shared library injection and PAM backdoors: /etc/ld.so.preload is the highest-privilege persistence mechanism available without modifying binaries — any library listed in this file is injected into every dynamically linked process on the system at startup, including init, sshd, sudo, and cron; `sudo cat /etc/ld.so.preload` should return empty on a clean system — any entry is a critical finding (T1574.006); also check `sudo cat /proc/1/environ | tr '\0' '\n' | grep LD_PRELOAD` for environment-level injection in the init process; `find /lib/security -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp -type f` finds PAM modules installed more recently than the system's known-good timestamp — PAM modules in /lib/security/ (or /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/security/ on 64-bit systems) are loaded by any PAM-aware service; a malicious PAM module listed in /etc/pam.d/common-auth runs for every authentication attempt on the system and can silently accept any password, log credentials, or spawn reverse shells; audit PAM module files: `find /lib/security -name "*.so" | sort` and compare against a package-manager-verified list using `dpkg -S /lib/security/*.so | awk -F: '{print $1}' | sort -u`; any .so file in /lib/security that is not owned by an installed package is a critical finding — remove it and run `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/pam-modules.sha256` to verify all legitimate modules are unmodified

**Skills Gained:** cron five-location audit (user crontabs/root crontab/etc-crontab/cron.d/run-parts directories), cron IOC patterns (tmp execution/wget-curl/base64/every-minute timing), systemctl baseline diff for new services, find -newer timestamp for recently created systemd units, user-level systemd path (~/.config/systemd/user/), journalctl -u cron for executed-not-just-configured evidence, SSH authorized_keys full audit (find + while-read-cat), sshd_config backdoor indicators (AcceptEnv PATH/non-standard Port/unexpected ForceCommand), shell startup file execution order (profile/profile.d/bash_profile/bashrc), find + xargs grep -l for reverse shell patterns in startup files, /etc/profile.d/*.sh all-users execution risk, /etc/rc.local boot persistence, /etc/ld.so.preload (critical — any entry = finding), /proc/1/environ LD_PRELOAD environment injection check, PAM module audit (/lib/security/ + dpkg -S ownership check), non-packaged PAM .so = critical finding
**AI Mentor:** Ground the ld.so.preload finding in severity: "Of all six persistence mechanisms, /etc/ld.so.preload is the hardest to detect without specifically looking for it and the hardest to remove without understanding exactly what the injected library does. A rootkit installed via ld.so.preload runs in the address space of every process on the system — including ps, ls, ss, and netstat — which means those tools can be made to hide the rootkit from their own output. If `cat /etc/ld.so.preload` is not empty and you did not put it there, your first action is to capture the hash of the listed library, boot from external media, and analyse it offline. Do not run any more commands trusting the output of userspace tools on that system."
