/mnt/evidence/processes-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).txt, ss -tnp > /mnt/evidence/connections-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).txt, ls -laR /tmp /var/tmp > /mnt/evidence/tmp-listing.txt, sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers > /mnt/evidence/critical-hashes.sha256, kill -9 $(pgrep -f "bash -i"), usermod -L compromised-user, iptables -A OUTPUT -d 203.0.113.45 -j DROP, iptables -A INPUT -s 203.0.113.45 -j DROP, chmod u-s /usr/bin/unexpected-suid, setcap -r /usr/bin/unexpected-cap 2>/dev/null, sed -i '/compromised-user/d' /etc/sudoers.d/compromised-grants 2>/dev/null, echo "" > /etc/ld.so.preload, ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | grep "compromised-user" | head -20, ausearch -k identity -ts today -i | tail -10), ClickableDiagram (privilege escalation response workflow: detect escalation path → preserve volatile evidence FIRST → terminate attacker session → block C2 network → lock compromised account → eradicate escalation vector (SUID/capability/LD_PRELOAD/sudo) → verify escalation path is closed → document all actions with timestamps — click each step for the commands and the consequence of skipping or reordering), AttackTimeline (privilege escalation response drill: analyst receives alert → timeline shows five events the learner must place in the correct response order: evidence preservation / session kill / network block / account lock / vector eradication), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** suid-privilege-escalation-mechanism-detection-removal, linux-capability-getcap-setcap-r-removal, sudo-misconfiguration-nopasswd-all-exploitation, ldpreload-injection-containment-eradication, containment-action-ordering-evidence-destruction-risk
- **Practical:** free-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-and-challenge | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA CySA+ Domains 2, 3, and 4, CompTIA Security+ Domain 4, EC-Council CEH, GCIH | **Careers:** Incident Responder, SOC Analyst (Tier 2/3), Threat Hunter, Blue Team Analyst | **Accessibility:** Privilege escalation response workflow diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per step and an explicit "consequence of skipping" label per node; containment action ordering explained in a numbered sequence with rationale for each position; SUID removal commands documented with pre/post verification; capability removal syntax explained field by field; all commands in accessible code blocks; LD_PRELOAD eradication steps in a numbered runbook; ethical framing applied before all offensive technique descriptions

**Ethical Content Note:** This lesson teaches privilege escalation techniques (SUID abuse, Linux capability abuse, LD_PRELOAD injection, sudo misconfiguration exploitation) exclusively from the detection and response perspective. The orientation at every step is: "an attacker used this technique — here is how to detect that it was used, contain the attacker, and remove the vector." No attack-execution instructions are provided. All techniques discussed are in MITRE ATT&CK and are taught so defenders can recognise and remediate them.

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Identify the active privilege escalation vector from system artifacts: privilege escalation on Linux follows one of five primary vectors — SUID binary abuse (T1548.001), Linux capability abuse (T1548.001 variant), LD_PRELOAD injection (T1574.006), sudo misconfiguration exploitation (T1548.003), and kernel exploit (T1068); `getcap -r /usr/bin /usr/sbin /bin /sbin 2>/dev/null` lists all files with Linux capabilities set — capabilities grant specific root-like privileges without full root (e.g., cap_net_raw allows raw sockets, cap_dac_override bypasses file permission checks entirely, cap_setuid allows setting any user ID including root); any capability-set binary not in the authorised baseline is a privilege escalation vector; `find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt` identifies unexpected SUID binaries beyond the baseline; `sudo -l -U username` shows what commands a specific user can run as root — `(ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL` means any command as root without a password; `cat /etc/sudoers` and `ls -la /etc/sudoers.d/` identify all sudo grants including package-installed entries that may be overly permissive; auditd logs (ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i) show which commands the suspected attacker actually executed as root — the combination of the sudo grant and the executed command history establishes the escalation event with full evidentiary weight
2. Execute the containment sequence in volatility-preserving order: containment sequencing matters because each action destroys evidence from the next — killing a process removes it from the process table (volatile), blocking a network connection closes the socket entry in /proc/net (volatile), locking an account leaves the account record intact (non-volatile); the correct order is: (1) capture volatile evidence first — `ps auxf > /mnt/evidence/processes-timestamp.txt`, `ss -tnp > /mnt/evidence/connections-timestamp.txt`, `ls -laR /tmp /var/tmp > /mnt/evidence/tmp-listing.txt` — all within two minutes of alert; (2) terminate the attacker's active session — `kill -9 $(pgrep -f "bash -i")` ends the interactive reverse shell; (3) block the C2 network address — `iptables -A OUTPUT -d ATTACKER_IP -j DROP && iptables -A INPUT -s ATTACKER_IP -j DROP` — this prevents the attacker from reconnecting without destroying evidence; (4) lock the compromised account — `usermod -L username` (the -L flag prefixes the password hash with ! rendering it invalid for authentication, but the account record and its cron jobs remain intact for forensic analysis); do not delete the compromised account until the investigation is complete — deletion destroys the audit trail of that user's activity; hash the critical files immediately after containment: `sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers > /mnt/evidence/critical-hashes.sha256`
3. Eradicate the escalation vector and verify closure: eradication targets the specific mechanism that enabled or maintained the privilege escalation — removing it is distinct from containment (which stopped the active attacker) and must happen before recovery; for SUID abuse: `chmod u-s /path/to/unexpected-binary` removes the SUID bit; verify with `ls -la /path/to/binary` (no 's' in the permissions field); for capability abuse: `setcap -r /path/to/binary` removes all capabilities from the binary; verify with `getcap /path/to/binary` (empty output means no capabilities); for LD_PRELOAD: `echo "" > /etc/ld.so.preload` clears the global injection file; then identify and hash the injected library before deleting it — it is forensic evidence and should be preserved in /mnt/evidence/ before removal; for sudo misconfiguration: remove the overly permissive grant from /etc/sudoers.d/ and verify with `sudo visudo -c` (syntax validation) followed by `sudo -l -U username` to confirm the escalation path is closed; verify eradication with a second run of getcap, find -perm -4000, cat /etc/ld.so.preload, and sudo -l for the affected account — all should now return clean or match the authorised baseline; document each eradication action with a UTC timestamp and the command used; these timestamps belong in the incident timeline

**Skills Gained:** privilege escalation vector identification (SUID/capability/LD_PRELOAD/sudo/kernel), getcap -r (recursive capability scan), setcap -r (capability removal and verification), find -perm -4000 vs. suid-baseline diff, sudo -l -U username (user sudo grants), /etc/sudoers.d/ grant audit, ausearch -k privileged for attacker command history, containment action ordering rationale (volatile-evidence-first discipline), ps auxf / ss -tnp / ls -laR /tmp evidence capture commands with UTC timestamps, kill -9 $(pgrep -f pattern) session termination, iptables -A OUTPUT/INPUT -j DROP for C2 block, usermod -L (lock without delete — preserves forensic record), sha256sum critical-hashes post-containment, chmod u-s SUID removal, echo "" > /etc/ld.so.preload LD_PRELOAD eradication, sudoers.d grant removal and visudo -c validation, eradication verification sequence (getcap/find-perm-4000/ld.so.preload/sudo-l all clean)
**AI Mentor:** Make the containment ordering argument visceral: "The most common mistake in privilege escalation response is killing the attacker's session before capturing volatile evidence. Once you run `kill -9`, the process entry vanishes from /proc, the socket entry closes, and the command line argument that told you exactly what the attacker was running is gone. You have two to five minutes from alert acknowledgement to capture ps, ss, and /tmp contents before the attacker or an automated reconnection script destroys that state. Evidence first, containment second — every time, regardless of how urgent it feels to close the session."

---

##### A3.C5.M4.L4 — Recovery, Reporting, and Continuous Improvement
- **Slug:** recovery-reporting-continuous-improvement | **Type:** expository | **Duration:** 50 min | **Difficulty:** intermediate | **Bloom's:** create | **Ethical Content:** false
- **KG Nodes:** OS-L06, OS-L15 | **Prereqs:** A3.C5.M4.L1, A3.C5.M4.L2, A3.C5.M4.L3
- **Interactive:** TerminalSimulator (systemctl start nginx, systemctl status nginx, systemctl status webapp.service, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256, sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256, sudo aide --check, dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$", ss -tlnp, ufw status verbose, auditctl -l, systemctl list-timers | grep security, journalctl -u security-audit.service -n 10, cat /mnt/evidence/incident-report.md, grep "hardening_index" /var/log/lynis-report.dat, sudo lynis audit system --quick 2>&1 | tail -20, ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i | wc -l, cat /etc/audit/rules.d/post-incident-additions.rules, aide --init, sha256sum /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers > /opt/baseline/critical-files-updated.sha256, diff <(crontab -l -u root 2>/dev/null) /opt/baseline/root-crontab.txt 2>/dev/null, logger -t incident-recovery "Recovery verified for $(hostname) at $(date -u)"), ClickableDiagram (PICERL incident response framework: Preparation (policies/tools/baselines) → Identification (detection/triage/scoping) → Containment (isolation/account-lock/C2-block) → Eradication (vector removal/persistence cleanup/account audit) → Recovery (service restoration/integrity verification/monitoring confirmation) → Lessons Learned (root cause/detection gaps/improvement actions) — click each phase for the checklist items and the artefacts produced), ProcessFlow (incident report structure: Title block → Executive Summary → Timeline → Technical Narrative → IOC List → Recommendations → Appendices — click each section for the content requirements and word-count guidance), QuizWidget
- **Animations:** picerl-six-phases-with-output-artefacts, recovery-verify-sequence-integrity-before-service-start, incident-report-executive-summary-vs-technical-narrative-audience, lessons-learned-detection-gap-to-control-improvement, continuous-improvement-feedback-loop-alert-rule-fim-aide
- **Practical:** guided-lab | **Assessment:** quiz-only | **Reflection:** transfer
- **Certs:** CompTIA CySA+ Domains 3 and 4, CompTIA Security+ Domain 4, GCIH, EC-Council CHFI | **Careers:** Incident Responder, SOC Analyst, Security Engineer, CISO-track | **Accessibility:** PICERL diagram text-labeled with aria descriptions per phase and checklist items listed in a plain-language table; incident report structure explained in a seven-row table with section name, audience, and content requirements; recovery verification checklist in a numbered sequence; lessons learned template provided as a five-field form with field names and descriptions; all commands in accessible code blocks; ProcessFlow widget keyboard-navigable

**Learning Objectives:**
1. Execute the recovery phase with integrity verification before service restoration: recovery is not simply restarting services — it is restoring the system to a verified-clean known-good state before any affected service returns to production traffic; the recovery sequence is: (1) verify all eradication actions are complete (getcap, find -perm -4000, cat /etc/ld.so.preload, sudo -l for the compromised account all return clean); (2) run integrity verification — `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256` and `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256` must return no FAILED entries, meaning no additional files were modified beyond what was investigated; (3) run aide --check against the AIDE database and review every changed entry — any unexpected entry requires investigation before recovery proceeds; (4) run dpkg --verify to confirm no package files were modified; (5) only after all integrity checks pass, restart affected services with `systemctl start servicename` and verify each reaches the active (running) state with systemctl status; (6) verify monitoring systems are active: `systemctl list-timers | grep security` (FIM and audit scripts scheduled), `auditctl -l` (all audit rules loaded), `ufw status verbose` (firewall policy intact); if any integrity check returns a FAILED entry for a file that was not part of the investigated incident, treat it as a new finding and return to the Identification phase — do not proceed to recovery with unresolved integrity failures
2. Write a professional incident response report following the PICERL structure: the incident report is the primary deliverable of the incident response process — it serves three audiences simultaneously: executives (who need to understand business impact and resource requirements), technical staff (who need enough detail to reproduce the investigation and verify remediation), and legal/compliance (who need a defensible record with timestamps and chain of custody); a complete incident report has six sections: (1) Title block — incident ID, affected system, incident classification, dates of detection and resolution, lead responder; (2) Executive Summary — 200 words maximum, business impact, root cause in one sentence, remediation status (resolved/ongoing/monitoring), and cost estimate; (3) Timeline — all events in the five-field format (UTC timestamp / source / event / affected entity / ATT&CK technique) ordered chronologically; (4) Technical Narrative — detailed walkthrough of what happened, in the order it was discovered during the investigation; (5) IOC List — all attacker IPs, filenames, hashes, usernames, and ATT&CK technique IDs with confidence level (high/medium/low); (6) Recommendations — at least three specific, actionable items with the control ID, the current gap, and the recommended change with an estimated implementation effort; recommendations must address the detection gap (what monitoring would have caught this earlier), the hardening gap (what configuration would have prevented or limited the attack), and the process gap (what procedure should have been followed but was not)
3. Implement post-incident continuous improvement to close detection gaps: the lessons learned phase converts incident findings into durable system improvements that reduce mean time to detect for the same attack class; the continuous improvement cycle for each finding from the incident: identify the detection gap (what evidence was available that no monitoring alert fired on) → design a control to close it → implement the control → verify it works → add it to the baseline → schedule reassessment; concrete improvements from a typical Linux compromise: add an auditd rule watching the specific path the attacker used for persistence (`auditctl -w /path/to/attacker-file -p wa -k post-incident`), add the new persistence path to the SHA-256 FIM baseline (`sha256sum /previously-unmonitored-file >> /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256`), add a new check function to security-audit.sh for the specific IOC class that was missed, update the AIDE configuration to monitor any directory that the attacker exploited but AIDE did not cover, and run lynis after all changes to verify the hardening index increased; document each improvement in the hardening register with the incident ID that motivated it, the date implemented, and the verification method — this register is the evidence that lessons learned actually translate into system changes

**Skills Gained:** recovery verification sequence (eradication complete / integrity checks / AIDE check / dpkg verify / service restart / monitoring active), recovery precondition (all integrity checks pass before service-start), aide --check as final pre-recovery gate, systemctl start + status (service restoration and verification), monitoring confirmation (list-timers / auditctl -l / ufw status), PICERL six-phase framework (Preparation/Identification/Containment/Eradication/Recovery/Lessons Learned), incident report six-section structure (title/executive-summary/timeline/technical-narrative/IOC-list/recommendations), three-audience report (executive/technical/legal), executive summary 200-word constraint, IOC list format (IP/filename/hash/username/technique-ID/confidence), recommendation three-gap structure (detection/hardening/process), continuous improvement cycle (gap → control design → implement → verify → baseline → reassess), auditctl -w post-incident rule addition, sha256sum >> baseline append for new monitored files, security-audit.sh new check function per missed IOC class, AIDE configuration extension, lynis post-improvement verification, hardening register concept
**AI Mentor:** Close the improvement loop explicitly: "The lessons learned phase is the most skipped phase in incident response — teams resolve the incident, write the report, and move to the next alert without implementing any changes. The result is that the same attack succeeds again six months later. The discipline is: every detection gap identified in the lessons learned becomes a specific, scheduled implementation task with an owner and a due date. Not a recommendation document — an implemented control, verified with a test, added to the monitoring baseline. If you investigated a cron persistence backdoor and your audit script did not catch it, write the check function before you close the ticket. That function runs forever. That is how blue team capability compounds over time."

### Module A3.C5.M4 — Practical

**Title:** Conduct a Complete Blue Team Investigation on a Compromised Linux Host
**Type:** free-lab | **Duration:** 75 min | **Difficulty:** advanced

**Scenario:** Helix Corp's SOC has received a SIEM alert: unusual outbound network activity was detected from a production Linux application server at 04:17 UTC. The learner has been assigned as the lead incident responder. The server has the automation suite from Module 2 and the enterprise baseline from Module 3 in place. The learner must conduct a full blue team investigation from initial alert to final incident report, applying the complete PICERL workflow.

**Tasks:**
1. **Initial triage and scoping:** Execute the complete initial triage sequence: w, who, last -n 20, ss -tnp, ps auxf, journalctl -p err --since "today" -n 50, and grep "Accepted\|Failed" /var/log/auth.log | tail -30. Document every anomaly before taking any action. Identify the source IP of the suspicious outbound connection and the process owning it. Do not terminate any process or lock any account at this stage.
2. **Persistence hunting:** Run a systematic persistence check across all six mechanism categories: (a) all cron locations for all users, (b) systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled diff against baseline and find /etc/systemd/system -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp, (c) all authorized_keys files, (d) all shell startup files with grep for reverse shell patterns, (e) cat /etc/ld.so.preload, (f) find /lib/security -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp. The simulator has persistence present in exactly three of the six layers. Document each finding with the location, content, ATT&CK technique ID, and confidence level.
3. **Privilege escalation detection:** Run getcap -r /, find -perm -4000 against the SUID baseline, sudo -l for each interactive-shell account, and ausearch -k privileged -ts today -i. Identify the escalation vector the attacker used — the simulator has one active escalation path present. Map it to its ATT&CK technique. Document the evidence from each source that confirms the escalation.
4. **Evidence preservation and containment:** Before any eradication: capture ps auxf, ss -tnp, ls -laR /tmp, and netstat -rn to /mnt/evidence/ with UTC timestamps. Hash /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow, /etc/sudoers, and the attacker's malicious files. Then execute containment in the correct order: kill the attacker's session, block the C2 IP with iptables, lock the compromised user account. Do not delete any files at this stage.
5. **Eradication and recovery:** Remove all three persistence mechanisms found in Task 2. Remove the privilege escalation vector found in Task 3. Verify closure of each with the appropriate detection command. Run sha256sum -c against the FIM baseline to confirm no additional files were modified beyond those investigated. Run dpkg --verify. Restart any affected services and verify they reach active status. Confirm all monitoring systems are active with systemctl list-timers and auditctl -l.
6. **Incident report:** Write /root/incident-report.md with all six PICERL sections: Title block, Executive Summary (≤200 words, business impact, resolved status), Timeline (eight entries in five-field format), Technical Narrative (chronological investigation walkthrough), IOC List (all attacker artefacts with ATT&CK technique IDs and confidence levels), and Recommendations (three items addressing the detection gap, the hardening gap, and the process gap that allowed this incident to occur).

**Assessment Criteria:** Initial triage sequence completed and all anomalies documented before any action taken (15%), all three persistence mechanisms identified across the correct layers with ATT&CK mapping (25%), privilege escalation vector identified with multi-source evidence from auditd and sudo -l (15%), evidence captured in correct volatility order before containment, containment executed in correct sequence (20%), eradication verified clean with detection commands and FIM baseline passes (15%), incident report contains all six sections with eight-entry timeline and three-gap recommendations (10%)

---

### Course A3.C5 — Capstone

**Title:** Operation Night Sentinel
**Type:** quiz-and-capstone | **Duration:** 120 min | **Difficulty:** advanced

**Scenario:** A medium-sized financial services company — Meridian Financial — has contacted Helix Corp's incident response team after detecting anomalous behaviour on a critical Linux application server that handles internal payment processing. The server hosts a Python web application running under the webapp service account, with nginx as the reverse proxy. The server is in a hardened network segment with restricted external access. The SOC analyst on call noticed an unusual spike in outbound connections at 23:47 UTC followed by several failed sudo attempts by an account that should not have sudo access. The server is live and processing transactions — the business unit has stated it cannot afford a full outage during business hours. The learner has been assigned as the lead incident responder with SSH access to a TerminalSimulator representing the compromised host. All data from Modules 1–4 is applicable. The investigation must be completed without unnecessarily disrupting services.

1. **Investigate the incident — establish the full scope of compromise:** Execute the 12-command initial triage sequence in this exact order, documenting every finding before taking any action: `uptime` (establish system state and reboot history), `w` (active sessions), `who` (login sessions with terminal), `last -n 20` (recent login history with source IPs), `lastb -n 20` (recent failed logins), `journalctl -p err --since "2026-07-27 22:00:00" --until "2026-07-28 02:00:00" -n 100` (error-level events in the incident window), `grep "Accepted\|Failed password\|Invalid user" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $1,$2,$3,$9,$11}' | tail -40` (authentication events with source IPs), `ps auxf` (full process tree including parent-child relationships), `ss -tnp | grep -v "127.0.0.1"` (all non-loopback established connections with owning processes), `ss -tlnp` (all listening services), `ls -la /proc/*/exe 2>/dev/null | grep deleted` (processes whose binary was deleted after launch — anti-forensic indicator), and `ausearch -k privileged -ts "07/27/2026 22:00:00" -te "07/28/2026 02:00:00" -i | head -60` (privileged command executions in the incident window); from this sequence, identify: (a) the attacker's source IP, (b) the account used for initial access, (c) the timestamp of first successful authentication, (d) any processes running from world-writable locations, (e) any deleted-binary processes, and (f) the outbound connection destination; document all findings in the Identification section of the incident report before proceeding to any investigative action beyond observation

2. **Identify persistence — find every mechanism the attacker installed:** Conduct a systematic six-layer persistence audit covering every mechanism covered in Module 4; layer 1 — cron: audit all five cron locations for every user account with an interactive shell (`for u in $(awk -F: '$7=="/bin/bash"||$7=="/bin/sh"{print $1}' /etc/passwd); do echo "=== $u ==="; sudo crontab -l -u "$u" 2>/dev/null; done`), and review cron execution logs with `grep "CRON.*CMD" /var/log/syslog | grep -v "security-audit\|fim-check" | tail -30` to see what actually ran; layer 2 — systemd: run `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled | sort | diff - /opt/baseline/enabled-services.txt` to identify units not in the approved baseline, and `find /etc/systemd/system -name "*.service" -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp -type f` to find recently created units; also check user-level systemd: `find /home -maxdepth 3 -path "*/.config/systemd/user/*.service" 2>/dev/null`; layer 3 — SSH: cat all authorized_keys files for root and every user, run `sshd -T | grep -iE "port|listenaddress|acceptenv|authorizedkeysfile"` to check for sshd_config modifications, and verify no rogue sshd process is running with `ps aux | grep sshd | grep -v "^root.*sshd -D"` (the legitimate sshd master process runs as root with -D); layer 4 — shell startup files: `find /home /root -name ".bashrc" -o -name ".bash_profile" -o -name ".profile" 2>/dev/null | xargs grep -l "nc\|wget\|curl\|python.*socket\|bash -i\|/dev/tcp" 2>/dev/null`; also check `/etc/profile.d/` and `/etc/rc.local`; layer 5 — shared library injection: `sudo cat /etc/ld.so.preload`, `sudo cat /proc/1/environ | tr '\0' '\n' | grep LD_PRELOAD`, and `find /lib/security /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/security -newer /opt/baseline/timestamp -type f 2>/dev/null`; layer 6 — filesystem anomalies: `find /tmp /var/tmp /dev/shm -type f -executable 2>/dev/null` and `find /var/www /opt -name "*.php" -newer /var/log/dpkg.log -type f 2>/dev/null` (web shell detection); the simulator has persistence present in four of the six layers — identify all four, document the location and content of each, map each to its ATT&CK technique ID, and classify each as the primary persistence mechanism or a secondary redundancy the attacker installed

3. **Detect privilege escalation — identify how the attacker gained elevated privileges:** The initial triage revealed failed sudo attempts by an unexpected account; investigate the full privilege escalation chain using four evidence sources: (a) `getcap -r / 2>/dev/null` — the simulator has one unexpected capability set on a binary that should not have it; identify the binary, the capability, and the exploitation path; (b) `find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | grep -v -f /opt/baseline/suid-baseline.txt` — check for SUID binaries not in the approved baseline; (c) `sudo -l -U compromised-account` — check whether the compromised account was granted sudo access after initial compromise (a common attacker action to ensure persistent privileged access); (d) `ausearch -k privileged -ts "07/27/2026 22:00:00" -te "07/28/2026 02:00:00" -i | grep "compromised-account\|webapp\|www-data"` — extract every privileged command the attacker executed including timestamps; from these four sources, reconstruct the escalation chain: which account was initially compromised, what vulnerability or misconfiguration allowed escalation, what privileged commands were run after escalation, and whether root access was achieved; map the complete escalation chain to MITRE ATT&CK technique IDs with confidence levels (high/medium/low) based on the quality of evidence for each step

4. **Secure SSH — remediate the authentication and access control layer:** Before any containment action, check the current SSH configuration for attacker-introduced modifications: `diff /etc/ssh/sshd_config /opt/baseline/sshd_config.baseline` (the simulator has one directive the attacker modified); identify the directive, its current malicious value, and its required hardened value; restore the correct value and then apply the full Helix Corp SSH hardening standard: PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no, MaxAuthTries 3, LoginGraceTime 30, X11Forwarding no, AllowAgentForwarding no, PermitTunnel no, ClientAliveInterval 300, ClientAliveCountMax 2, LogLevel VERBOSE, and AllowUsers set to only the accounts that require SSH access for this server's operational role; remove all unauthorised keys from authorized_keys for every user; validate with `sshd -t`, reload with `systemctl reload sshd`, and verify with `sshd -T | grep -iE "permitrootlogin|passwordauthentication|maxauthtries|allowusers|loglevel"`; verify that no authorised key was removed — after removing attacker keys, confirm the remaining keys belong to known administrators by checking key fingerprints against the key inventory at /opt/baseline/authorized-keys-inventory.txt using `ssh-keygen -l -f /path/to/authorized_keys`

5. **Harden the system — apply post-incident hardening controls:** After containment, apply the controls that would have prevented or limited this incident; using the lynis audit as a guide, identify the top three hardening gaps the attack exploited and remediate each: run `sudo lynis audit system --quick` and cross-reference the Warnings with the incident findings; apply any missing sysctl hardening from /etc/sysctl.d/99-cis-hardening.conf (`sysctl --system` and verify each parameter); verify PAM lockout is active (`grep pam_faillock /etc/pam.d/common-auth`) — if the attacker performed a brute-force authentication attempt that pam_faillock should have blocked but did not, check the faillock configuration for the relevant service's PAM stack; verify and tighten AllowUsers in sshd_config if any unexpected account was able to log in; review and tighten sudoers entries — remove any grant that should not exist (`visudo -c` after each edit); verify AppArmor or SELinux is in enforce mode for the nginx and webapp processes; record the initial lynis hardening_index score from /var/log/lynis-report.dat, apply remediations, run lynis again, and record the final score

6. **Verify package integrity — confirm the attack did not trojanise system tools:** A sophisticated attacker may replace system utilities (ps, ls, ss, netstat, find) with modified versions that hide their presence; conduct a comprehensive package integrity verification before trusting any tool output: run `dpkg --verify 2>/dev/null | grep -v "^.$"` and record every file with a modification flag; run `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/binaries-manifest.sha256 2>&1 | grep FAILED` and record every binary that fails verification; run `sha256sum -c /opt/baseline/critical-files.sha256` for the critical configuration files; for any binary that fails verification, immediately stop using it and retrieve a clean copy from the package manager with `apt install --reinstall package-name`; verify the reinstalled binary: `dpkg --verify packagename` should now return no output for that package's files; the simulator has one modified system binary — identify it, reinstall the package it belongs to, and verify the reinstall resolved the checksum mismatch; after verifying all system tools are clean, run the full AIDE check: `sudo aide --check 2>&1 | grep -E "changed|added|removed"` and record every deviation that was not already captured in the incident evidence; any deviation beyond the known-attacker-files is a new finding that extends the incident scope

7. **Audit users — verify account integrity and access control:** The attacker may have created backdoor accounts, modified existing account properties, or granted new privileges; conduct a full account audit: run `awk -F: '($3==0){print}' /etc/passwd` (any UID-0 account other than root is a critical finding), `awk -F: '($7=="/bin/bash"||$7=="/bin/sh"){print $1,$3}' /etc/passwd` (all interactive-shell accounts with their UIDs — any account that should be a service account with nologin but has a shell is a finding), `sudo awk -F: '($2==""){print $1}' /etc/shadow 2>/dev/null` (accounts with empty passwords), `diff <(awk -F: '{print $1}' /etc/passwd | sort) /opt/baseline/passwd-userlist.txt` (accounts not in the pre-incident baseline), and `diff <(awk -F: '{print $1}' /etc/group | sort) /opt/baseline/group-baseline.txt` (groups not in the baseline); for each account identified as suspicious: run `last -n 20 username` to see their login history, `sudo crontab -l -u username` to see their cron jobs, and `cat /home/username/.bash_history 2>/dev/null` to see their command history; for any account confirmed as a backdoor, lock it with `usermod -L username` and document it as an IOC; do not delete accounts until the investigation is complete — deletion destroys the audit trail; verify all sudo grants with `cat /etc/sudoers /etc/sudoers.d/*` and remove any grant that was not present in the baseline

8. **Build an incident timeline — produce the eight-entry chronological record:** Using all evidence gathered across tasks 1–7, construct a complete incident timeline with exactly eight entries, each in the five-field format: UTC Timestamp | Source | Event Description | Affected Entity | ATT&CK Technique; the timeline must cover the complete kill chain from initial access to detection: entry 1 — initial reconnaissance or first probe (if evidence exists), entry 2 — initial access event (first successful authentication), entry 3 — first attacker command after access (from bash_history or auditd), entry 4 — privilege escalation event (identified in task 3), entry 5 — first persistence mechanism installed (earliest timestamp from task 2 findings), entry 6 — second or subsequent persistence mechanism installed, entry 7 — attacker's primary objective action (data access, lateral movement attempt, or payload execution), entry 8 — detection event (the SIEM alert or the first anomalous entry that triggered the investigation); write the timeline to /mnt/evidence/incident-timeline.txt; verify that each timestamp is in UTC, each entry has an explicit ATT&CK technique ID, and the causal chain between entries is evident from reading them in sequence — a reviewer who was not part of the investigation must be able to understand the attack narrative from the timeline alone

9. **Recover services — restore production capability with verification:** The business unit requires service restoration as soon as the system is verified clean; execute the recovery sequence in the correct order: (1) run the complete eradication verification — all four persistence mechanisms removed (`crontab -l / systemctl list-unit-files / cat authorized_keys / cat /etc/ld.so.preload` all return clean or match baseline), privilege escalation vector removed (getcap and find -perm -4000 match baseline), modified system binary reinstalled (dpkg --verify returns clean), AIDE check passes (`sudo aide --check` reports no unexpected differences); (2) update the AIDE database to incorporate the approved post-incident configuration changes: `sudo aide --update && sudo mv /var/lib/aide/aide.db.new /var/lib/aide/aide.db`; (3) restart affected services in dependency order — nginx first, then the webapp service — and verify each with `systemctl status servicename` confirming active (running) state and no error-level journal entries with `journalctl -u servicename -n 10 -p warning`; (4) verify the monitoring and security automation suite is fully active: `systemctl list-timers | grep -E "security|fim"` (timers scheduled), `auditctl -l` (all audit rules loaded), `ufw status verbose` (firewall default-deny with correct rules), `faillock --user webapp 2>/dev/null` (pam_faillock state clear); (5) send a service restoration notification: `logger -t incident-recovery "Night Sentinel: services restored on $(hostname) at $(date -u)"`; run `grep "incident-recovery" /var/log/syslog` to confirm the logger entry was recorded

10. **Produce a professional incident response report — deliver the final PICERL artefact:** Write /root/night-sentinel-report.md as the complete incident response report for Meridian Financial; the report must contain seven sections: (1) Title Block — Incident ID: NS-2026-001, System: hostname and IP, Classification: Confirmed Intrusion, Detection Date: 2026-07-28 at UTC time of detection, Resolution Date: completion timestamp, Lead Responder: learner's name or alias, Severity: Critical; (2) Executive Summary — 200 words maximum describing the incident in business terms: what system was compromised, what the attacker accessed or attempted, what the business impact was, when the incident was resolved, and whether customer data was exposed; (3) Attack Timeline — the eight-entry timeline from task 8, presented as a formatted table with the five fields as column headers; (4) Technical Narrative — a chronological walkthrough of the investigation findings, organised by investigation phase: Initial Access, Execution, Persistence (all four mechanisms), Privilege Escalation, and Attacker Objective; for each phase, describe what evidence was found, which commands produced it, and what it reveals about attacker capability and intent; (5) IOC List — a table with five columns (Type/Value/Source/ATT&CK Technique/Confidence) covering every attacker artefact identified: IP addresses, malicious filenames with their SHA-256 hashes, modified binary names, backdoor account names, and unauthorised authorised keys with fingerprints; (6) Recommendations — three specific actionable items each addressing a distinct gap: (a) the detection gap (what monitoring change would have produced an alert before the attacker achieved objectives), (b) the hardening gap (what configuration change would have blocked or limited the attack), (c) the process gap (what procedure should have been in place that was not); each recommendation must name the specific control, the current state, the target state, and an estimated implementation effort; (7) Lessons Learned — a post-incident review answering four questions: what went well (controls that limited the attacker), what was missed initially (why the attacker had dwell time before detection), what would be done differently (process improvements), and what new detections were added to the monitoring baseline as a direct result of this incident

**Assessment Criteria:** Twelve-command triage sequence completed and scope fully documented before any action (10%), all four persistence mechanisms identified across the correct layers with ATT&CK mapping and confidence levels (20%), complete privilege escalation chain reconstructed with multi-source evidence (15%), SSH hardening applied — attacker-modified directive identified and corrected, all unauthorised keys removed, AllowUsers restricted, sshd -T verified (10%), modified system binary identified and reinstalled with dpkg --verify confirming clean (10%), user account audit completed — all backdoor or anomalous accounts identified and locked (10%), eight-entry timeline in five-field format with correct ATT&CK technique IDs and causal chain (10%), services restored in correct sequence with all monitoring systems verified active (5%), incident report contains all seven sections with IOC table, three-gap recommendations, and lessons learned (10%)
**KG Validation:** OS-L06 (blue team operations — privilege escalation detection/containment/eradication across the full incident lifecycle), OS-L09 (Syslog/journald — evidence extraction from journalctl, /var/log/auth.log, /var/log/syslog across the investigation), OS-L10 (Bash scripting — fim-check.sh and security-audit.sh deployed and verified during recovery phase), OS-L11 (AppArmor enforcement verified as part of post-incident hardening), OS-L13 (SSH hardening applied, PAM faillock verified, AllowUsers enforced), OS-L15 (Auditd — ausearch -k privileged and identity as primary evidence sources for escalation and timeline reconstruction), SEC-17 (Kill Chain — eight-entry timeline maps to kill chain phases), SEC-18 (MITRE ATT&CK — all findings mapped to technique IDs with confidence levels)
**Mastery Gate:** ≥85% to unlock Academy 4 — Windows Security

---

# Academy Summary

Academy 3 takes the learner from first contact with a Linux terminal to conducting a professional incident response investigation on a compromised enterprise server. The journey spans five courses, each building directly on the foundations of the last, and is unified by a single governing philosophy: defenders must understand systems at the level of an attacker to protect them effectively.

**Course A3.C1 — Linux Fundamentals** establishes the conceptual and practical foundation. Learners navigate the filesystem hierarchy (OS-L01), interpret and modify Unix permission bits and ACLs (OS-L02), manage users and groups with correct UID/GID discipline (OS-L03), and control installed software through the package management layer (OS-L04). By the end of Course 1 the learner can operate independently on a Linux command line — reading, writing, and organising files; controlling access; and understanding why the filesystem layout looks the way it does.

**Course A3.C2 — System Administration** moves from the filesystem to the running system. Learners learn to inspect and control processes (OS-L08), manage systemd services and understand the boot sequence (OS-L08 extended), configure network interfaces and routing tables, and administer local and network storage. The course capstone requires administering a multi-role server under realistic operational constraints — package failures, a process consuming runaway memory, and a service requiring manual recovery.

**Course A3.C3 — Security Hardening** is the course where the platform's defensive orientation becomes explicit. Learners apply CIS Benchmark Level 1 and Level 2 controls, harden the SSH daemon and understand each configuration directive's security implication, build a default-deny firewall policy with UFW, deploy and interpret SELinux and AppArmor mandatory access control (OS-L11), configure auditd with a purpose-built rule set (OS-L15), and run lynis to benchmark a system against a hardening baseline. The capstone requires taking a freshly installed server from default to production-hardened state against a 40-point rubric.

**Course A3.C4 — Bash Scripting for Security** builds the automation muscle that separates an effective defender from a slow one. Learners progress from scripting foundations through conditional logic, functions and error handling, and file processing pipelines (OS-L07), then apply those skills to security-specific automation: scheduled scanning and alerting (OS-L07 + T1053.005 detection), log aggregation and pattern matching, and a final enterprise scripting capstone that requires building a self-contained security audit toolkit deployable across multiple hosts.

**Course A3.C5 — Advanced Linux Security** synthesises all prior learning and introduces attacker-perspective techniques so that defenders understand what they are protecting against. Module 1 covers the kernel-level attack surface: SUID/SGID bits and capability abuse (OS-L06, OS-L10), the LD_PRELOAD shared-library hijack path (OS-L13), and namespace and cgroup escapes (OS-L12). Module 2 translates that knowledge into automated defence: security audit scripts, file integrity monitoring pipelines, log analysis automation, and scheduled security workflows. Module 3 covers enterprise-scale Linux security: PAM stack configuration, NSS and LDAP integration, PKI and certificate infrastructure, and multi-host audit aggregation. Module 4 is devoted entirely to blue team operations: digital forensics on a Linux host, threat hunting through auditd and journald, hardening a production system against the kill chain, and the course capstone — Operation Night Sentinel — a full-fidelity incident response simulation on a compromised financial application server requiring evidence collection, persistence eradication, privilege escalation reconstruction, timeline building, and professional report production.

Across 5 courses, 20 modules, and 80 lessons the learner has built a complete, deployable defensive Linux skill set aligned to the CompTIA Linux+, RHCSA, Security+, CySA+, CEH, and OSCP certification domains, and is prepared to pursue any of seven security and operations career paths.

---

# Skills Acquired

## 1. Linux Fundamentals
- Navigate and interpret the Filesystem Hierarchy Standard (FHS) — `/bin`, `/sbin`, `/etc`, `/var`, `/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev`, `/tmp`
- Read, write, delete, copy, move, and link files using core utilities with correct flag selection
- Interpret and modify standard Unix permission bits (rwx), special bits (SUID, SGID, sticky), and POSIX ACLs with `getfacl` / `setfacl`
- Understand inode structure and how the kernel resolves paths, symlinks, and hard links
- Search filesystems efficiently with `find`, `locate`, `which`, and `type`; redirect and pipe output through grep, awk, sed, sort, uniq, and wc
- Read and interpret `/proc` and `/sys` pseudo-filesystem entries as live system state

## 2. System Administration
- Install, update, remove, and verify packages with `apt` / `dpkg`; hold and pin package versions; audit installed package integrity with `dpkg --verify`
- Configure and query network interfaces with `ip addr`, `ip link`, `ip route`; read ARP cache and routing tables; configure static addresses and DNS resolution
- Mount and unmount filesystems; interpret `/etc/fstab`; administer disk partitions and logical volumes; monitor disk health and inode consumption
- Configure and control systemd targets, units, and timers; read and write unit files; understand the boot sequence from firmware through `systemd` to login prompt
- Administer NFS and Samba shares; configure client-side mounts; understand service account requirements for network file services

## 3. Process & Service Management
- List, inspect, and interpret running processes with `ps`, `pgrep`, `top`, `htop`, `lsof`, and `/proc/PID/`
- Send signals to processes correctly — SIGTERM for graceful shutdown, SIGKILL for force termination, SIGHUP for reload — and understand why signal choice matters
- Identify zombie and orphan processes; interpret process state columns; understand memory and CPU priority with `nice` and `renice`
- Manage systemd services: enable, disable, start, stop, restart, reload; read unit status and journal output; write drop-in override files
- Configure systemd timers as a modern, audit-logged alternative to cron; compare cron and timer scheduling for security implications
- Recognise attacker-abused service management patterns: persistence via cron (T1053.005), rogue systemd units (T1543.002), and processes with anomalous parent-child relationships

## 4. Networking
- Configure network interfaces, static routes, and DNS resolvers; interpret `ss`, `netstat`, and `ip` output
- Administer SSH: generate and deploy key pairs, configure `~/.ssh/config` and `sshd_config`, implement certificate-based authentication, understand each hardening directive's effect
- Build and manage UFW firewall policies; understand default-deny posture, logging rules, and rule ordering; interpret connection tracking state
- Analyse network traffic with `tcpdump` at the command line — filter expressions, output formats, and evidence capture to pcap
- Understand host-based network isolation: network namespaces, veth pairs, and how containers leverage namespace separation

## 5. Security Hardening
- Apply CIS Benchmark Level 1 and Level 2 controls to a production Linux system — kernel parameter hardening via `sysctl`, filesystem mount options (`noexec`, `nosuid`, `nodev`), and service minimisation
- Configure and interpret SELinux contexts, booleans, and policy modules; use `audit2allow` to generate minimal policy exceptions; understand MAC enforcement vs. DAC
- Configure and interpret AppArmor profiles in enforce and complain modes; write custom profiles; understand profile inheritance
- Deploy and configure auditd with a purpose-built rule set covering privileged command execution, sensitive file access, user/group changes, and network-related activity; query the audit log with `ausearch` and `aureport`
- Run lynis, interpret the hardening index score, and prioritise findings by risk; understand the difference between informational, warning, and critical findings
- Manage file integrity monitoring with AIDE: build a baseline, schedule verification checks, interpret the deviation report, and update the database after approved changes

## 6. Bash Scripting
- Write portable, defensive Bash scripts: `set -euo pipefail` discipline, quoting all variables, testing return codes, providing usage messages, and using `mktemp` for temporary files
- Use functions, local variables, and return values to write modular, testable scripts
- Process structured and unstructured text: parse `/etc/passwd`, `/etc/group`, log files, and command output with awk, sed, and grep; handle CSV, space-delimited, and colon-delimited formats
- Write scheduled security scripts: cron job format, systemd timer alternatives, log rotation awareness, and idempotent script design
- Build security automation tools: log aggregators, file integrity checkers, port scanners, user auditors, and AIDE deviation reporters that produce structured, timestamped output
- Implement alerting in scripts: write to syslog with `logger`, send email notifications, and write structured reports to designated evidence directories

## 7. Monitoring & Logging
- Understand the Linux logging architecture: kernel ring buffer → rsyslog/journald → syslog facilities and severity levels → log files and systemd journal
- Query journald with `journalctl` — unit filters, time ranges, priority levels, output formats, and boot selectors
- Configure rsyslog: facility.severity selectors, remote forwarding with TCP/TLS, log rotation via logrotate, and retention policy
- Interpret `/var/log/auth.log` for authentication events: successful and failed logins, sudo usage, SSH key vs. password authentication, and PAM events
- Read auditd event records: `type=SYSCALL`, `type=PATH`, `type=EXECVE` records; correlate events across an audit log to reconstruct a command sequence; use `ausearch -k` and `aureport --failed`
- Recognise log-clearing and log-tampering techniques; understand why auditd's immutable mode (`-e 2`) protects the rule set

## 8. Incident Response
- Execute the PICERL framework (Preparation → Identification → Containment → Eradication → Recovery → Lessons Learned) on a compromised Linux host
- Collect volatile evidence in the correct order: running processes → active connections → routing table → ARP cache → open files → RAM image → disk image
- Identify and document all four common Linux persistence layers: cron jobs (user and system), systemd service units and timers, SSH `authorized_keys` backdoors, and shared-library hijacks via `/etc/ld.so.preload`
- Reconstruct a privilege escalation chain using multi-source evidence: auditd SYSCALL records, bash history, capability listings with `getcap`, SUID file enumeration with `find -perm -4000`, and package integrity with `dpkg --verify`
- Build an eight-entry incident timeline in the five-field format (UTC Timestamp / Source / Event Description / Affected Entity / ATT&CK Technique) covering the complete kill chain
- Produce a professional PICERL incident response report with Executive Summary, Attack Timeline, Technical Narrative, IOC Table, Three-Gap Recommendations, and Lessons Learned sections

## 9. Blue Team Operations
- Apply the attacker's mental model to defensive decisions: understand SUID/SGID abuse (T1548.001), LD_PRELOAD hijacking (T1574.006), cron persistence (T1053.005), rogue systemd units (T1543.002), and SSH backdoors (T1021.004) from the attacker's perspective so that detections are designed to catch real techniques
- Hunt for indicators of compromise in auditd logs, journald, bash history, `/proc`, and the filesystem — not just in dedicated SIEM alerts
- Deploy and verify a defence-in-depth stack: auditd rule set, AppArmor/SELinux enforcement, PAM lockout policy, AIDE file integrity monitoring, and UFW default-deny firewall — and confirm each layer is active before declaring a system clean
- Conduct post-eradication verification: four-layer persistence check, privilege escalation vector removal, system binary integrity check, AIDE database update, and service restoration in dependency order
- Write and deliver security findings to a non-technical audience: executive summary language, business-impact framing, and actionable recommendations at three levels (detection gap, hardening gap, process gap)
- Operate within ethical and legal constraints on incident response: evidence chain-of-custody discipline, prohibition on active attribution, data minimisation when collecting user artefacts, and mandatory escalation thresholds

# Certification Mapping

| Certification | Coverage | Domains Addressed |
|---|---|---|
| **CompTIA Linux+** | Fully Covered | System Management (A3.C1–C2), Security (A3.C3–C5), Scripting (A3.C4), Troubleshooting (A3.C2, A3.C5) — Academy 3 addresses all four Linux+ domain areas with hands-on practicals that match exam performance expectations |
| **RHCSA (Red Hat Certified System Administrator)** | Fully Covered | Essential tools, file management, users and groups, networking, software management, systemd services, storage — all covered in A3.C1–C2; Note: Red Hat-specific tooling (subscription-manager, dnf, SELinux policy tooling on RHEL) is introduced conceptually; learners should supplement with a RHEL lab environment for exam readiness |
| **CompTIA Security+** | Partially Covered | Domain 2 (Threats, Vulnerabilities, Mitigations) — Linux attack techniques in A3.C5; Domain 4 (Security Operations) — hardening, monitoring, incident response in A3.C3–C5; Domains 1, 3, 5 are addressed at the Academy 1–2 level and not repeated here |
| **CompTIA CySA+ (Cybersecurity Analyst+)** | Fully Covered | Domain 1 (Security Operations) — log analysis, monitoring, alerting in A3.C3–C5; Domain 2 (Vulnerability Management) — lynis, AIDE, hardening in A3.C3; Domain 3 (Incident Response) — PICERL, timeline, report in A3.C5.M4; Domain 4 (Reporting and Communication) — incident report writing in A3.C5 Capstone |
| **CEH (Certified Ethical Hacker)** | Partially Covered | Module 6 (System Hacking — Linux) — SUID, LD_PRELOAD, capability abuse, privilege escalation in A3.C5.M1; Module 16 (Hacking Web Servers — Linux service context) — process management and service security in A3.C2–C3; CEH domains covering network exploitation, web application hacking, and malware analysis are not addressed in Academy 3 |
| **OSCP (Offensive Security Certified Professional)** | Introduced | Linux privilege escalation via SUID (T1548.001), LD_PRELOAD (T1574.006), and Linux capability abuse (OS-L10) — introduced in A3.C5.M1 from the defensive perspective; learners understand the technique and its forensic artefacts but have not practised exploitation in an offensive lab environment; OSCP requires dedicated offensive training (Academy 5 — Offensive Security, if available) |

# Career Mapping

| Role | Academy 3 Contribution | Key Courses |
|---|---|---|
| **Linux Administrator** | Core role enablement — Academy 3 provides the complete administrative skill set: filesystem management, package administration, user and group management, service management, network configuration, storage administration, and scripting automation | A3.C1 (Fundamentals), A3.C2 (System Administration), A3.C4 (Bash Scripting) |
| **SOC Analyst** | Essential platform knowledge — the majority of enterprise infrastructure runs on Linux; SOC analysts must be able to read auditd logs, journald, `/var/log/auth.log`, and syslog in their original format without depending solely on SIEM normalisation; Academy 3 builds this raw-log fluency | A3.C3 (Security Hardening), A3.C5.M3–M4 (Monitoring, Blue Team) |
| **Security Analyst** | Hardening and audit skills — security analysts validate hardening posture, interpret vulnerability scanner output in the context of running configuration, and advise on CIS Benchmark controls; Academy 3 covers all of these capabilities with hands-on practicals | A3.C3 (Security Hardening), A3.C4 (Scripting), A3.C5 (Advanced Security) |
| **Systems Administrator** | Full platform coverage — systems administrators manage multi-user Linux environments, automate operational tasks, and are the first responders to service failures; Academy 3's five-course progression covers all core sysadmin competencies with security-first framing | A3.C1–C4 (all courses), A3.C5.M3 (Enterprise Administration) |
| **DevSecOps Engineer** | Security automation and pipeline awareness — DevSecOps engineers integrate security controls into the deployment pipeline; Academy 3 provides the Linux scripting and service management foundation required to write pre-commit hooks, deployment verification scripts, and runtime security monitors | A3.C4 (Bash Scripting), A3.C5.M2 (Automation for Security Operations), A3.C3 (Hardening as code) |
| **Incident Responder** | Complete foundational skill set — incident responders must be able to collect forensic evidence, identify persistence, reconstruct timelines, and write professional reports on Linux systems; Academy 3's A3.C5.M4 and the Operation Night Sentinel capstone directly simulate the full incident response workflow | A3.C5.M4 (Blue Team Operations), A3.C5 Capstone (Night Sentinel), A3.C3 (Hardening context) |
| **Penetration Tester (Linux skills)** | Foundational Linux enumeration and privilege escalation understanding — penetration testers must understand the Linux attack surface from first principles; Academy 3 builds this understanding from the defensive side (what to harden, what to monitor) which directly informs offensive enumeration methodology; learners understand SUID abuse, LD_PRELOAD, and capability exploitation as forensic artefacts, which is the prerequisite for learning to exploit these techniques offensively | A3.C5.M1 (Attack Surfaces), A3.C2 (Process and Service context), A3.C3 (Hardening — understanding what blocks attacks) |

# Recommended Next Academy

## Academy 4 — Windows Security

Academy 4 takes the learner from Linux-native to platform-agnostic defender by introducing the Windows operating system, Active Directory, and the Windows security model — the dominant platform in enterprise environments and the primary target of ransomware operators, nation-state actors, and commodity malware campaigns.

**Why Windows knowledge is essential after Linux mastery:**

Real enterprise environments are heterogeneous. A SOC analyst monitoring a corporate network will see Linux servers, Windows workstations, Windows Server domain controllers, and Windows-native services (Active Directory, Kerberos, NTLM, SMB, RDP) in a single environment. An incident responder investigating a ransomware incident will likely start on a Windows workstation, pivot to Active Directory, and discover lateral movement that touched both platforms. A security analyst hardening a production environment must understand Group Policy, Windows Defender, Windows Event Logs, and the Windows registry — not just Linux hardening controls.

**How Windows security complements Linux security:**

The conceptual foundations built in Academy 3 transfer directly:

| Linux (Academy 3) | Windows (Academy 4) |
|---|---|
| `/etc/passwd` and `/etc/shadow` — local user database | SAM database and Active Directory — local and domain user stores |
| `sudo` and `/etc/sudoers` — privilege escalation controls | UAC and `SePrivilege` tokens — Windows privilege model |
| `auditd` and `journald` — system-level audit logging | Windows Event Log — Security/System/Application channels |
| `sshd_config` — remote access hardening | RDP security configuration, NLA enforcement |
| `cron` and `systemd timers` — scheduled task persistence | Task Scheduler — Windows persistence mechanism |
| `LD_PRELOAD` — shared-library hijacking | DLL hijacking — Windows equivalent attack technique |
| SELinux/AppArmor — mandatory access control | Windows Defender Application Control (WDAC) |
| `ps`, `/proc` — process inspection | Process Explorer, Get-Process, WMI — Windows process enumeration |

Learners who have completed Academy 3 already understand how operating systems manage users, processes, services, file permissions, network configuration, and audit logging at a conceptual level. Academy 4 introduces a new implementation of these same concepts, with the added complexity of Active Directory, Group Policy, and Windows-specific attack techniques (Pass-the-Hash, Kerberoasting, DCSync). The cross-platform comparison makes both platforms more understandable, not less.

**Recommended progression:** Complete all five Academy 3 course capstones with ≥85% before beginning Academy 4. Learners who have earned the Operation Night Sentinel completion mark have demonstrated the incident response discipline that will be extended to Windows environments in Academy 4.

---

# Academy Completion Checklist

Complete all items below before marking Academy 3 as finished.

## 1. Knowledge
- [ ] Can explain the Linux Filesystem Hierarchy Standard and the purpose of each top-level directory
- [ ] Can describe the Unix permission model (rwx bits, SUID/SGID, sticky bit, ACLs) and explain when each is applied
- [ ] Can explain the boot sequence from firmware through GRUB, the kernel, initrd, and systemd to the login prompt
- [ ] Can describe the three main Linux logging systems (kernel ring buffer, rsyslog, journald) and how they relate
- [ ] Can explain the PICERL incident response framework and the evidence collection order (evidence volatility ordering)

## 2. Practical Labs
- [ ] Completed all 20 module practicals with documented artefacts
- [ ] Can reproduce the file permission audit workflow from scratch without consulting notes
- [ ] Can configure auditd rules, trigger events, and query the audit log end-to-end
- [ ] Can deploy AIDE, build a baseline, make a monitored change, and interpret the deviation report
- [ ] Can write a Bash security audit script that produces a structured timestamped report

## 3. Capstones
- [ ] A3.C1 Capstone — completed with ≥85%
- [ ] A3.C2 Capstone — completed with ≥85%
- [ ] A3.C3 Capstone — completed with ≥85%
- [ ] A3.C4 Capstone — completed with ≥85%
- [ ] A3.C5 Capstone (Operation Night Sentinel) — completed with ≥85%

## 4. Troubleshooting
- [ ] Can diagnose a failed systemd service start using `journalctl -u` and `systemctl status`
- [ ] Can identify a process holding a port and terminate it safely
- [ ] Can recover from a locked user account (`pam_faillock` state) and a misconfigured `/etc/sudoers`
- [ ] Can identify and resolve a permission denied error on a file protected by SELinux or AppArmor

## 5. Security Hardening
- [ ] Can apply CIS Benchmark Level 1 controls to a freshly installed server and verify each control is in effect
- [ ] Can harden `sshd_config` to disable password authentication, restrict allowed users, and change the listening port
- [ ] Can build a UFW default-deny policy with specific allow rules for required services and verify with `ufw status verbose`
- [ ] Can deploy a lynis scan and prioritise the top five findings by risk level

## 6. Incident Response
- [ ] Can collect volatile evidence from a running system in the correct order (processes → connections → routing → ARP → open files)
- [ ] Can identify all four Linux persistence mechanism layers (cron, systemd, authorized_keys, ld.so.preload)
- [ ] Can reconstruct a privilege escalation chain using auditd logs, bash history, and `find -perm -4000`
- [ ] Can produce an eight-entry incident timeline in the five-field format (UTC Timestamp / Source / Event / Entity / ATT&CK)
- [ ] Can write a professional incident response report with Executive Summary, IOC Table, and Recommendations

## 7. Bash Scripting
- [ ] Can write a Bash script with `set -euo pipefail`, proper quoting, usage message, and `mktemp` for temp files
- [ ] Can write a log aggregation script that parses `/var/log/auth.log` and reports failed login counts by IP
- [ ] Can write a file integrity monitoring script that compares a current hash manifest against a saved baseline
- [ ] Can schedule a security script with both cron and a systemd timer and verify both are running with correct output

## 8. Enterprise Administration
- [ ] Can configure PAM stack (`/etc/pam.d/common-auth`) for password quality and account lockout policy
- [ ] Can integrate a Linux host with an LDAP directory for centralised authentication and explain the NSS/PAM relationship
- [ ] Can deploy and rotate PKI certificates on a Linux service using OpenSSL and configure the service to use TLS
- [ ] Can aggregate auditd logs from multiple hosts to a central log server and query the aggregated stream
